# The French Revolution and Napoleon - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It begins with a question that sounds simple and is not - why did a government that had run out of money end up losing not merely its solvency but its right to exist? - and it ends with a legal order that survived the men who made it, the terror they used, the general who ended their republic and the monarchy that replaced him. Between those two points it teaches the legal architecture of privilege under the old regime; the fiscal and subsistence crisis that set the date; what ideas contributed and what they could not; the exact sequence of 1789 from the summoning of the Estates-General to the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen; the peasants, artisans and Paris crowds without whom none of it would have moved; the constitutional monarchy and why it broke; the religious rupture; war and radicalisation; the Jacobin republic, its constitution and its Terror; the retreat through the Directory; the people the new citizenship excluded; what actually changed in French society; Napoleon's seizure of power, his legal and administrative settlement, his empire and his fall; and finally the historians' quarrel about what the whole thing was. The conceptual grammar of rights, consent and sovereignty belongs to the overview topic; the American sequence belongs to its own topic; the post-1815 settlement belongs to the topic after this one. Here you learn the French event itself, in the order in which one part of it caused the next.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. Each answers a problem created by the lesson before it, which is why the sequence is not simply chronological: Lesson 5, on the crowd, comes after Lesson 4, on the Assembly, because you cannot see what the crowd did until you know what the Assembly could not do by itself. Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the single habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the standard account of this topic and its historiographical companion, the standard chronology and comparison set for the subject, the standard school chapter on this topic, a standard political-theory text, a standard reference account, or a verified official question paper | Assert it. Dates, named documents and recorded figures can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

Two further disciplines are specific to this topic, and both exist because this is the part of the syllabus where confident invention is most common.

**The quotation and figure control.** Do not quote Rousseau, Robespierre or the Declaration from memory, and do not invent casualty totals. The Declaration is quoted here only in wording that can be checked against a printed text, no revolutionary orator is quoted at all, and the one execution figure that standard accounts carry is always given with its exact scope. Where a number is contested, the contest is taught rather than hidden.

**The precision discipline.** The Revolution legislated constantly, and its measures, assemblies and factions have names and dates that an examiner expects to see used correctly. Those names are an asset rather than a hazard: the Civil Constitution of the Clergy, the Convention, the Committee of Public Safety, 9 Thermidor, the Concordat and the Continental System each fix a mechanism to a moment, and a candidate who deploys them accurately writes a visibly better answer than one who gestures at "a measure against the Church" or "a reaction". What loses marks is the opposite habit - inventing particulars, or padding an answer with half-remembered detail that cannot be defended if challenged. The rule throughout this session is therefore simple: use the standard name and the verified date, use them sparingly and in the service of an argument, and never manufacture a title, a figure or an attribution to fill a gap.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Dependency-led question | Stage |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What exactly was the old regime - an unfair society, or something more precise? | Foundation |
| 2 | Why 1789? What turned a chronic problem into a terminal one? | Foundation |
| 3 | What did ideas and the Atlantic example actually contribute - and what could they not do? | Foundation |
| 4 | How did a tax assembly become a sovereign one, and how fast? | Core |
| 5 | Who supplied the force? What did peasants, artisans and crowds actually change? | Core |
| 6 | What was built between 1789 and 1791, and why did it fail within two years? | Core |
| 7 | Why did the religious question, of all questions, become the unhealable one? | Core |
| 8 | Why did war destroy the monarchy the constitution had just preserved? | Core |
| 9 | What was the Jacobin republic, and how should the Terror be explained? | Core |
| 10 | Why did the Terror end, and why did its ending lead to a soldier? | Core |
| 11 | Who was left outside the new citizenship, and does that change the verdict? | Core |
| 12 | Strip away the politics: what materially changed in France? | Core |
| 13 | Why a general, and why in 1799 rather than 1795 or 1793? | Core |
| 14 | What did Napoleon build at home, and how much of it was the Revolution's? | Core |
| 15 | How did an empire founded on revolutionary principles produce resistance in their name? | Core |
| 16 | What survived 1815, and in what form? | Core |
| 17 | What do historians actually disagree about, and how should a candidate weigh it? | Advanced |
| 18 | How is all of this converted into marks? | Synthesis |

*Read downwards as a chain of enabling conditions and sideways where two pressures acted at once. The single most examinable feature of the diagram is that it has two separate outputs at the bottom, not one: the social settlement and the political settlement part company after 1799, and almost every good verdict on this topic depends on keeping them apart.*

**Scope of this session.** The conceptual apparatus - what "rights", "consent", "sovereignty" and "privilege" mean as political categories, and who the Enlightenment writers were - belongs to the overview topic that owns it, and is used here only where the French sequence requires it. The American war of independence belongs to its own topic; this session uses it twice, as a fiscal cause and as a comparison, and does not narrate it. The Congress of Vienna and the Concert of Europe belong to the topic that follows; this session ends at Waterloo and hands over. Where a point is owned elsewhere, the lesson says so rather than re-narrating it.

## Lesson 1 - The old regime as a legal order, not merely an unfair society

*The top half of the diagram is what 1789 abolished. The bottom half is why the abolition did not settle anything, and it is the reason this topic has ten more years to run.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Most societies in most centuries have been unequal. That by itself explains nothing, because most of them did not have revolutions. So the first task in this topic is to say what was distinctive about French inequality, and the answer is not that it was unusually severe. It is that it was **written into the law**.

In an ordinary unequal society, a rich man and a poor man stand in the same legal position and differ in what they own. Under the old regime, a nobleman and a merchant could stand in the same financial position and differ in what they *were*. One belonged to a category that paid certain taxes and one to a category that did not; one could hold certain offices and one could not; one answered to certain courts and one to others. Wealth might be identical, and status was not transferable by earning more.

That distinction is the whole of Lesson 1, and it decides the shape of everything after it. If the grievance had been that some people were rich and others poor, the remedy would have been redistribution. Because the grievance was that some people were legally privileged and others legally burdened, the remedy was **abolition of the legal categories themselves** - and that is a constitutional act, not an economic one. It is why the Revolution's first great achievement was a piece of law rather than a transfer of wealth, and why a peasant who gained his freedom from seigneurial dues did not thereby gain land.

### 2. The three estates, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** The standard account for this topic sets out the old-regime order as three estates. The **First Estate**, the clergy, enjoyed privilege and tax exemptions, and by doing so linked the Church to the old order. The **Second Estate**, the nobility, held privilege, office and much land, and was the beneficiary of the feudal hierarchy. The **Third Estate**, the common people, formed **about 95 per cent of the population** on the older survey's figure, and carried both the tax burden and the political exclusion.

✅ **Fact:** The chapter spine that the standard account follows opens with exactly this: French society during the late eighteenth century, described as three estates and unequal privilege. It is presented as the starting point of the whole subject, not as background colour.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the asymmetry the three-line description conceals. The first two estates are defined by what they *receive* - exemption, office, land, sanction. The third is defined by what it *supplies* - taxes - and by what it lacks - political voice. A society can survive that arrangement for a long time provided the state does not need much money and the excluded do not have an argument. Lesson 2 removes the first condition and Lesson 3 removes the second.

✅ **Fact:** The same account records the clergy's specific function in the structure: its privileges linked the Church to the old order. That is a statement about ideology, not only about revenue. An institution that taught obedience, controlled much of what could be printed and taught, and was simultaneously exempt from ordinary taxation was not a neutral spiritual body but a load-bearing wall of the political structure.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why the religious question could not be separated from the political one, and it is the reason Lesson 7 exists. An attack on privilege was necessarily an attack on the Church's privileges; an attack on the Church's privileges necessarily raised the question of the Church's authority; and once that question was raised there was no way to confine it to finance.

### 3. Inside the Third Estate: one legal category, three different grievances

This is where most answers go wrong, and it is worth slowing down. "The Third Estate" is a legal term. It is not a social class, and treating it as one produces the familiar and costly sentence that the Revolution was made by "the middle class".

✅ **Fact:** The standard account identifies three distinct forces inside that single legal category. The **peasantry** faced rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests that made life insecure, and supplied anti-feudal anger. The **bourgeoisie or middle class** had wealth without matching status or power, and supplied leadership in the institutional rupture. **Urban workers and artisans** faced long hours and poverty, formed secret societies, produced unrest, and supplied mass pressure from below.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion restates the same three forces in terms of what each wanted and what each got. The bourgeoisie wanted an end to privilege, office by merit and property rights, and gained in law, status and institutional power. The peasantry wanted relief from dues, rents and feudal burdens, and made major anti-feudal gains. Urban workers, whose politics the companion associates with the sans-culotte movement, wanted cheap bread, political voice and social equality; they helped drive the radical phase, but economic equality remained incomplete.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** set those three rows side by side and the Revolution's whole internal history becomes visible in advance. Two of the three demands - abolition of privilege, abolition of seigneurial burdens - are **abolitions**, and an abolition can be legislated once and be done. The third demand - cheap bread, social equality - is a **provision**, and a provision has to be delivered continuously by a state that has the resources and the will. That is why the bourgeois and peasant programmes could be substantially completed, and the artisan programme could not. It is also why the same coalition that made 1789 could not hold together through 1793.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's own caution is explicit: the Third Estate was internally divided, and bourgeois and peasant aims were not identical.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The Third Estate was a legal category containing at least three incompatible social programmes; the Revolution's phases are, in large part, the successive dominance of one of them.

### 4. Privilege as the target: the formulation that earns the mark

✅ **Fact:** The standard account states the claim precisely in its evidence bank: privilege was **legally, not merely economically, entrenched**, since the First and Second Estates held tax exemptions, office and land while the Third Estate carried the burden. The analytical significance recorded there is exact - it makes the target of 1789 **legal privilege, not wealth as such**.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text supplies an independent and very usable formulation of the same point. It records D. D. Raphael's statement, in *Problems of Political Philosophy* (1976), that "The French Revolutionaries, in demanding equality, were demanding a removal of arbitrary privilege, such as that which confined political rights to the rich and the well-born."

✅ **Fact:** The same text records Rousseau's distinction, from the *Discourse on the Origin of Inequality* (1755), between **natural inequality** - differences of age, health, bodily strength and qualities of mind - and **conventional inequality**, which consists in "the different privileges that some men enjoy to the exclusion of others, such as inequalities of wealth, prestige and power". Natural inequality is more or less ordained by nature; conventional inequality is man-made, emanating from a social order deliberately designed by men.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that distinction is the intellectual hinge of the whole topic and it is worth one sentence in almost any answer on causes. Once inequality is described as **man-made**, it becomes something that can be *unmade* - and the question shifts from whether the arrangement is bearable to whether it is defensible. An arrangement that cannot be defended and is known to be man-made is not merely unpopular; it has lost its title to continue. That is the precise sense in which the eighteenth century made the old regime vulnerable, and it is a sharper claim than the usual sentence about the Enlightenment "inspiring" the Revolution.

### 5. What the peasantry actually faced, and why "feudalism" is the right word here

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** candidates often hesitate over the word "feudal", because by 1789 France was not a society of armoured vassals. The hesitation is unnecessary if the word is used in the sense the sources use it: a surviving structure of **seigneurial rights** - dues, charges and obligations attached to land and owed to a lord by virtue of his status - layered on top of ordinary rent and royal taxation.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account describes the peasant condition as rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests combining to make life insecure, and identifies the resulting political emotion precisely as **anti-feudal anger**.

✅ **Fact:** The same account records the outcome that this anger eventually secured, and it is the largest single social fact in the topic: **feudal laws were annulled**, and that annulment survived every subsequent political reversal, including Napoleon's seizure of power.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the strongest available evidence for the claim that the Revolution was a genuine social transformation and not merely a change of governing personnel. A change of rulers can be reversed by another change of rulers. The abolition of a legal relationship between millions of cultivators and thousands of lords cannot easily be reversed, because reversal would require identifying, re-imposing and re-collecting obligations from a population that has stopped paying them and has armed itself in the interval. Lesson 12 develops this; it is worth noticing here that the peasantry's gain is the one that proved hardest to undo.

### 6. The bourgeoisie: wealth without status, and what that produces

✅ **Fact:** The standard account describes the middle class as having **wealth without matching status or power**, and the chapter spine it follows devotes a section to a growing middle class envisaging an end to privileges, driven by resentment of exclusion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal mechanism here is specific and worth stating rather than assuming. A group that is poor and excluded may riot; a group that is wealthy and excluded litigates, publishes, organises and, when a constitutional opportunity appears, drafts. The bourgeoisie's distinctive contribution was therefore not anger - the peasantry supplied more of that - but **institutional competence**: the capacity to turn a grievance into a constitutional claim, a claim into a procedure, and a procedure into a new body with a new name. Lesson 4 is entirely about that capacity in action over six weeks.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records what this group obtained: gains in law, status and institutional power.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and here is the standard trap, stated in advance so that Lesson 17 can take it apart properly. From "the bourgeoisie led the institutional rupture and gained from it", it does not follow that the Revolution *was* a bourgeois revolution in the sense of being caused by, designed for, or confined to that class. The companion's own debate row is careful: one side holds that the middle class led the institutional rupture; the other that peasants and urban crowds drove the radicalisation; and the recommended synthesis is a **multi-class revolution with shifting leadership**. Hold that formulation; the historiography lesson will earn it.

### 7. Urban workers: the force that is easiest to leave out

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records urban workers and artisans as facing long hours and poverty, forming **secret societies**, producing unrest, and constituting mass pressure from below.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion is emphatic about why this matters for writing: the Revolution rested **not only on elite thinkers but also on workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd**, and the companion states the writing rule directly - do not write the event as if pamphlets alone toppled the old regime.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reason this group is so often omitted is that its contribution does not appear in the documents that survive most visibly. Constitutions, declarations and decrees record the work of assemblies. The pressure that kept those assemblies in existence, protected them at decisive moments and pushed them past their own intentions left fewer documents and no signatures. An answer built only from the documentary record will therefore systematically under-weight exactly the force the sources warn you not to omit. Lesson 5 restores it.

### 8. Why this lesson had to come first

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** everything that follows depends on the distinction established here between a **legal order** and an **economic condition**, for four reasons that recur throughout the topic.

| The distinction explains | How |
|---|---|
| Why abolition came before redistribution | Legal categories can be abolished by decree; distributions cannot |
| Why the peasantry's gains stuck | An abolished obligation has no natural constituency to restore it |
| Why the workers' gains did not stick | Their demand required continuous provision, not a single abolition |
| Why Napoleon could keep the social settlement while ending the republic | The settlement was a body of law, and law can be codified and kept by any regime that finds it useful |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fourth row is the one that turns a description into a thesis, and it is the skeleton of the single most likely Napoleon question in this part of the syllabus. Keep it in reserve until Lesson 14.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Describing the old regime as a "legal order of privilege" is too tidy. In practice, nobles were often impoverished, some commoners were wealthier than most nobles, offices were purchased rather than inherited, and the Third Estate contained everyone from a Paris banker to a landless labourer. A category so internally varied cannot have been the real cause of anything.

**Reply:** The objection is correct about the variety and wrong about the conclusion, and the reply is the point of the lesson. Precisely because the category contained a banker and a labourer, the thing they shared was not an economic interest - it was a **legal position**. The banker's grievance was that his wealth bought him no status; the labourer's was that his poverty was taxed while others' wealth was not. Those are different grievances with a single legal address, which is exactly why they could be combined in 1789 and could not stay combined in 1793. The variety is not a defect in the analysis; it is the analysis.

**Residual:** What remains unsettled is the relative weight of the different burdens as felt in daily life - how much of the peasant's insecurity came from seigneurial dues, how much from royal taxation, how much from rent and how much from the harvest. Standard accounts list all four together without apportioning them, and that apportionment is a genuine historical question. Teach the combination and leave the weighting open rather than inventing a hierarchy.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the opening of every causes answer and the first body paragraph of most transformation answers. The move that earns marks is refusing the generic opening about inequality and substituting the precise one about legal privilege, then immediately splitting the Third Estate into its three programmes so that the later phases have somewhere to come from. For a question on how far the Revolution transformed society, this lesson supplies the baseline against which the transformation is measured; for a question on causes, it supplies the structural half of the answer, with Lesson 2 supplying the conjunctural half and Lesson 3 the ideological half. Do not spend more than four or five lines on the estates in a 250-word answer - the examiner's marks are in the consequences, not in the description.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Describing the old regime as merely "unequal" and thereby failing to explain why abolition rather than reform was the demand.
- Treating the Third Estate as a class, and so reducing the Revolution to a middle-class project.
- Writing "feudalism" without saying what survived of it - the seigneurial dues and obligations attached to land and status.
- Attributing the whole movement to writers and pamphlets, against the explicit warning in the sources.
- Assuming that because the bourgeoisie gained most in law and office, it must have caused the Revolution; leadership and causation are different claims.
- Giving the 95 per cent figure without noting that it is a figure for the whole Third Estate and therefore includes the wealthy bourgeoisie it is often used to exclude.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three estates: clergy, nobility, and the Third Estate at about 95 per cent of the population on the older survey's figure.
2. ✅ First Estate: privilege plus tax exemptions; its privileges linked the Church to the old order.
3. ✅ Second Estate: privilege, office and much land; beneficiary of the feudal hierarchy.
4. ✅ Third Estate: carried the tax burden and the political exclusion.
5. ✅ Privilege was legally, not merely economically, entrenched - so the target of 1789 was legal privilege, not wealth as such.
6. ✅ Inside the Third Estate: peasantry (rent, dues, taxes, bad harvests → anti-feudal anger); bourgeoisie (wealth without matching status or power → institutional leadership); urban workers and artisans (long hours, poverty, secret societies, unrest → mass pressure from below).
7. ✅ What each obtained: bourgeoisie - gains in law, status and institutional power; peasantry - major anti-feudal gains; urban workers - political voice but incomplete economic equality.
8. ✅ The sources warn explicitly that the Third Estate was internally divided and that bourgeois and peasant aims were not identical.
9. ✅ Feudal laws were annulled, and that annulment survived Napoleon's seizure of power.
10. ✅ Rousseau's *Discourse on the Origin of Inequality* (1755) distinguishes natural inequality from conventional inequality - privileges some enjoy to the exclusion of others.
11. ✅ D. D. Raphael's formulation (1976): the revolutionaries demanded the removal of **arbitrary privilege**, such as that confining political rights to the rich and the well-born.
12. ⚠️ Abolitions can be legislated once; provisions must be delivered continuously - which is why the bourgeois and peasant programmes could be completed and the artisan programme could not.
13. ⚠️ Do not write the Revolution as if pamphlets alone toppled the old regime; the sources name workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd.
14. ⚠️ Leadership is not causation: a multi-class revolution with shifting leadership is the defensible formulation.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate opens an answer with: "The French Revolution happened because French society was deeply unequal, with a rich nobility and a poor peasantry." Identify the two analytical errors and rewrite the opening so that it earns marks.

**Model answer:** The first error is category: the decisive inequality was legal rather than economic, since the First and Second Estates held tax exemptions, office and land by status while the Third Estate carried the burden by status, which is why the demand of 1789 was the abolition of privilege rather than the redistribution of wealth. The second error is composition: "a poor peasantry" replaces the Third Estate with one of its three components, losing the bourgeoisie, whose wealth without matching status or power supplied the institutional leadership, and the urban workers and artisans, whose long hours, poverty, secret societies and unrest supplied mass pressure from below. A rewritten opening runs: the old regime was not simply an unequal society but a legally constituted order of privilege, in which exemption from taxation, access to office and jurisdictional advantage followed from estate membership rather than from wealth; the Third Estate, about ninety-five per cent of the population, was a single legal category containing at least three distinct programmes - the bourgeoisie's demand for careers open to talent, the peasantry's demand for relief from seigneurial dues, and the urban artisan's demand for cheap bread and a political voice - and the Revolution's successive phases record the shifting dominance of one of those programmes over the others.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that identifying the bourgeoisie as the leading group settles the question of causation. The sources record bourgeois leadership of the institutional rupture and, in the same breath, record that peasants and urban crowds drove the radicalisation; the defensible verdict is a multi-class revolution with shifting leadership, not a bourgeois revolution with popular extras.

**Bridge to Lesson 2:** A legally privileged order of this kind had existed for centuries and had survived worse harvests and heavier grievances. So the structure cannot by itself explain the date. The next lesson asks what changed in the 1780s to convert a permanent condition into a terminal crisis.

```text
   THE CONSTITUTIONAL QUARREL NOW HAS A PHYSICAL FORCE BEHIND IT
```

*Follow the left column and you have the causes answer in nine steps. The bottom two lines are what make the difference between a constitutional crisis, which France had survived before, and a revolution, which it had not.*

### 1. The structural condition and the conjunctural trigger - and why you need both

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lesson 1 gave a structure that had lasted for centuries. A structure that lasts for centuries cannot explain an event in a particular year. Every good causes answer therefore has two halves: a **structural** half, which says why this society was vulnerable, and a **conjunctural** half, which says why it collapsed when it did. Lesson 2 is the second half, and the examiner's word for the skill involved is *weighting*.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account and its older survey together attribute the crisis to a combination of three things: **social inequality, fiscal disorder and intellectual challenge.** Not one of them; the combination.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that three-part formula is worth memorising verbatim as a sentence in an answer, because it pre-empts the single-cause error in both of its common forms - the answer that makes the Revolution a product of philosophy and the answer that makes it a product of hunger.

### 2. The fiscal breakdown, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** The older survey that the standard account relies on describes the French monarchy as **financially troubled and ultimately pushed toward bankruptcy**.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's timeline records the consequence: the **Estates-General meets in May 1789**, and the entry's explanation of significance is that the fiscal crisis opens the old regime to challenge.

✅ **Fact:** The same account's evidence bank states the analytical role of this fact with unusual precision. Fiscal breakdown **opened the political door**, and that is what "explains *why in 1789* and not earlier". The limitation attached to it in the same row is equally important: **fiscal crisis alone had been survived before; it became fatal only when combined with ideology and subsistence stress.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account supplies the particulars an answer should use. **Louis XVI** of the **Bourbon** house ascended the throne in **1774**, aged twenty and married to the Austrian princess **Marie Antoinette**, and found an empty treasury. Long years of war had drained French resources, to which was added the cost of an extravagant court at the palace of **Versailles**. Under Louis XVI, France helped the thirteen American colonies win independence from Britain; that war added more than a **billion livres** to a debt already above **2 billion livres**, and lenders began charging **10 per cent** interest, so a rising share of the budget went on interest alone.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this licenses a sharper use of the Atlantic connection than most answers manage. The American intervention was a **fiscal** cause of the French Revolution - a specific, datable, financial transmission - which is a much stronger claim than the vague statement that America "inspired" France, and Lesson 3 will show why the vague version is penalised. What it does not license is a ledger of ministers, reform projects and failed expedients; that belongs to a specialist account and no answer needs it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal work is done by the *form* of the crisis rather than by its arithmetic. What matters is that the state's insolvency could not be solved inside the existing constitution. The classes with taxable capacity were exempt by legal status; their status was guaranteed by the same monarchy that now needed their money; and the only route to taxing them ran through a body that would have to be asked. That is a structural dead end, and the diagram above is its map.

### 3. Why summoning an assembly was the point of no return

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this step deserves more attention than it usually gets, because it is where an answer can show mechanism rather than narrative.

Calling a representative body to authorise taxation is a routine act in a constitutional monarchy. In a monarchy where no such body had met for generations, it is not routine at all: it is an admission, made by the crown itself, that the crown alone cannot do what it needs to do. The admission is fatal in three separate ways.

| The admission concedes | Consequence |
|---|---|
| That royal authority is insufficient for the state's central task | Absolutism is publicly contradicted by its own government |
| That consent is required from the taxed | The principle of consent is introduced by the crown, not by its critics |
| That a body exists which is entitled to give or withhold that consent | A rival site of authority is created, with a legal summons in its hand |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the decisive one. Once a body is in the room with a legal title to be there, every subsequent question - how it votes, whom it represents, what it may do - is a question about sovereignty rather than about revenue. The crown had not intended to raise that question; it could not avoid raising it; and it had no procedure for closing it once raised. Lesson 4 follows the question through the six weeks in which it was answered.

### 4. The subsistence crisis: what hunger contributes, and what it does not

✅ **Fact:** The chapter spine the standard account follows contains a section titled *The struggle to survive*, whose core point is the **tax burden and the bread crisis**, and whose significance is recorded as material distress mattering.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's evidence bank states the causal role exactly: subsistence stress **mobilised the crowd**, and it "supplies the mass force behind elite constitutionalism". The attached limitation is equally exact: **bread riots are not by themselves revolutionary programmes.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that pair of statements is one of the most useful things in this topic, because it tells you precisely how to use hunger in an answer. Hunger supplies **force without direction**. Constitutional argument supplies **direction without force**. Neither is sufficient; the Revolution is what happens when the two are available in the same city in the same summer. Write it that way and you have explained a conjunction rather than listed two causes.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account also records the peasant side of the same stress: rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests together made life insecure.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and notice that the urban and rural forms of distress point in different directions, which matters later. The urban consumer wants the price of bread held down. The rural producer of grain does not. That divergence does not appear in 1789, when both are pressing against the same order, but it is one reason the revolutionary coalition becomes harder to hold as the decade proceeds.

### 5. Putting the three causes in the right relation

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a causes answer is graded on relation, not on enumeration. The three elements are not parallel; they do different jobs, and the following division of labour is defensible from the evidence banks in the sources.

```text
SOCIAL INEQUALITY (legal privilege)
        -> supplies the TARGET
           what is attacked, and why abolition rather than reform

FISCAL DISORDER (bankruptcy, the Estates-General)
        -> supplies the TIMING and the OPENING
           why 1789; and the institutional door through which the
           attack could be legally delivered

SUBSISTENCE STRESS (harvests, bread, tax burden)
        -> supplies the FORCE
           the crowds that make the attack survivable

INTELLECTUAL CHALLENGE (Lesson 3)
        -> supplies the LANGUAGE OF RIGHT
           which converts grievance into a claim that privilege is
           not merely burdensome but illegitimate
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four causes, four different functions, one event. If an examiner asks you to "analyse the causes", this block is the analysis - the word *analyse* means separate the parts and state their relation, and a list of four causes with no relation between them is precisely what the directive is asking you not to write.

### 6. The counterfactual that tests the claim

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** test the weighting by removing each element in turn, which is the historian's standard discipline and reads very well in an answer.

| Remove | Plausible outcome |
|---|---|
| The fiscal crisis | No summons, no assembly, no legal opening; grievance continues without a constitutional vehicle |
| The subsistence crisis | A constitutional quarrel among elites, of the kind French history already contained, with no force to prevent its suppression |
| The legal structure of privilege | A revenue dispute about who pays how much, negotiable by compromise, because nobody's legal identity is at stake |
| The language of right | Demands for relief and redress rather than for the abolition of an illegitimate order; reform, not revolution |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table is the argument for the sources' own formulation that the causes **interacted**. Each removal produces a serious crisis and none produces 1789. That is what "necessary but not sufficient" means when it is doing work rather than hedging.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This account makes the Revolution look almost accidental - a solvency problem that happened to coincide with a bad harvest. If the timing turns on contingencies of that kind, then the deep structural analysis of Lesson 1 was decorative, and the real explanation is a run of bad luck in the 1780s.

**Reply:** The objection confuses the trigger with the explanation. Bad harvests and revenue shortfalls are common; they produce revolutions only where a structure has already made the state unable to absorb them. The structure determines what a shortfall *means*: in a state that can tax its wealthiest subjects, a deficit is a budget problem; in a state whose wealthiest subjects are exempt by law and whose exemption the state itself guarantees, the same deficit is a constitutional impasse. That is why the sources hold that fiscal crisis had been survived before and became fatal only in combination. The contingency selects the year; the structure decides that the year will be terminal rather than difficult.

**Residual:** There is no defensible way to weigh the relative contribution of the fiscal and the subsistence crises against each other. Both are attested; neither is quantified; and an answer should present them as jointly necessary rather than assign percentages it cannot support.

### UPSC application

This lesson owns the "why then" half of every causes question, and it is also the lesson that rescues an answer from narrative. When the directive is *analyse*, use the four-function block in section 5 as the paragraph structure itself: target, timing, force, language. When the directive is *trace the phases*, this lesson supplies only the opening move and should be compressed to three lines. For a question on the enduring relevance of the Revolution, this lesson contributes one specific and under-used point - that the crisis of 1789 was in the first instance a crisis about **who may consent to taxation**, which is why the question of representative consent became the permanent form in which legitimacy is contested.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Listing causes without stating their relation, when the directive was *analyse*.
- Inventing a deficit figure or a reform ministry to fill out the fiscal paragraph; the debt magnitudes and the American war are the defensible particulars, and nothing further is needed.
- Making hunger the cause, when the sources say explicitly that bread riots are not by themselves revolutionary programmes.
- Making bankruptcy the cause, when the sources say explicitly that fiscal crisis alone had been survived before.
- Missing the mechanism by which summoning an assembly concedes the principle of consent before a single vote is taken.
- Forgetting that the urban demand for cheap bread and the rural interest in grain prices are not the same interest.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The crisis is attributed to a combination of social inequality, fiscal disorder and intellectual challenge.
2. ✅ The monarchy is described as financially troubled and ultimately pushed toward bankruptcy.
3. ✅ The Estates-General meets in **May 1789**; the fiscal crisis opens the old regime to challenge.
4. ✅ Fiscal breakdown "opened the political door" and explains why 1789 and not earlier.
5. ✅ Limitation: fiscal crisis alone had been survived before, and became fatal only with ideology and subsistence stress.
6. ✅ The chapter spine's section *The struggle to survive* records tax burden and bread crisis; material distress mattered.
7. ✅ Subsistence stress supplied the mass force behind elite constitutionalism.
8. ✅ Limitation: bread riots are not by themselves revolutionary programmes.
9. ✅ Peasant insecurity came from rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests together.
10. ⚠️ The four causes have four functions: privilege supplies the target, bankruptcy the timing and opening, hunger the force, rights-language the claim of illegitimacy.
11. ⚠️ Summoning an assembly concedes three things at once: that royal authority is insufficient, that consent is required, and that a rival site of authority exists.
12. ⚠️ Hunger is force without direction; constitutional argument is direction without force; the Revolution is their conjunction.
13. ⚠️ The counterfactual test - remove each cause in turn - yields a serious crisis every time and 1789 only when all are present.
14. ✅ The defensible fiscal particulars are Louis XVI's accession in **1774**, the empty treasury, the **Versailles** court, the American war adding over a **billion livres** to a debt already above **2 billion livres**, and **10 per cent** interest; go no further.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain, in mechanism rather than narrative, how a problem of state solvency became a question about sovereignty - and identify the point in the chain at which the outcome ceased to be reversible.

**Model answer:** The state required revenue; the groups with the greatest taxable capacity were exempt not by evasion but by legal status; and that status was guaranteed by the very monarchy that now needed their contribution, so the crown could not tax them without either repudiating the legal order on which it rested or obtaining their consent. It chose consent, which required summoning the Estates-General in May 1789, and that summons conceded three propositions simultaneously - that royal authority alone was insufficient for the state's central task, that the taxed were entitled to be asked, and that a body existed with a legal title to give or withhold an answer. Once that body assembled, the immediate practical question of how it should vote was inseparable from the question of whom it represented, and the question of representation is the question of where sovereignty lies. The point of no return is therefore the summons itself rather than any later act of defiance, because from that moment the crown had created an institution it had no constitutional procedure for dismissing, had publicly admitted the principle of consent, and had done both while a subsistence crisis was assembling crowds in the capital that would make the new body's survival a physical as well as a legal fact.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the bread crisis as the cause of the Revolution. Material distress supplied the force that made the constitutional challenge survivable, but the sources state flatly that bread riots are not by themselves revolutionary programmes; hunger explains the availability of crowds, not the content of what the crowds were used to achieve.

**Bridge to Lesson 3:** We now have a vulnerable structure and a terminal conjuncture. What we do not yet have is a reason why the people involved thought they were entitled to remake the constitution rather than merely to petition for relief. That entitlement came from an argument, and the next lesson establishes exactly how much the argument contributed - and how much it did not.

## Lesson 3 - What ideas contributed, what the Atlantic example contributed, and the limits of both

Progress: 3/18 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: ideas as legitimacy rather than grievance; transmission through pamphlets, clubs and lawyers; Rousseau and the language of citizenship; the parallel-causation rule; the boundary with the overview and American topics

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's evidence-bank row on ideas supplying legitimacy rather than grievance and its transmission caution, the historiographical companion's debate row on Enlightenment against crisis, the standard chronology's parallel-causation trap, and a standard political-theory text on Rousseau, citizenship and the ambiguity of natural-rights doctrine
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the causal arrow, drawn correctly

```text
                 ENLIGHTENMENT ARGUMENT
              (authority must give reasons;
               privilege is conventional, not natural)
                          |
            +-------------+-------------+
            |                           |
            v                           v
   AMERICAN REVOLUTION          FRENCH REVOLUTION
        1775-1783                    1789-1799
            |                           |
   breaks an imperial link      destroys a domestic
                                social and legal order
            |                           |
            +-----------> influenced <--+
                          and encouraged
                          (NOT caused)

   WRONG:  America 1776 ------causes------> France 1789
   RIGHT:  ideas --------> America
           ideas --------> France
           America -------> France  (example and encouragement only)
```

*The two arrows from the top run in parallel. The horizontal arrow at the bottom is real but weak, and the word on it is "influenced", never "caused". This is the single most frequently penalised error in the comparative questions in this part of the syllabus.*

### 1. The precise contribution of ideas

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's evidence bank states the role of ideas in one line and it is the line to learn: **ideas supplied legitimacy, not the grievance.** The named thinkers are Locke, Montesquieu and Rousseau, whose treatment belongs to the overview topic. The analytical significance recorded is that the argument **converts grievance into a claim of right**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** unpack that, because the distinction between a grievance and a claim of right is doing a great deal of work. A grievance says: *this burden is too heavy.* A claim of right says: *you had no authority to impose it.* The first invites negotiation - the burden can be reduced. The second cannot be negotiated, because reducing an unauthorised imposition leaves the question of authority exactly where it was. That is the whole mechanism by which an argument makes a society ungovernable without any argument ever winning a battle.

✅ **Fact:** The same row records the limitation, and it is essential: **most peasants did not read the philosophes; transmission was mediated through pamphlets, clubs and lawyers.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the limitation is not a qualification bolted on to be safe; it changes the claim's shape. The argument did not act on the population directly, like weather. It acted through **intermediaries who had a professional stake in it** - men trained to state a case, accustomed to procedure, and placed in the local institutions where grievances were already being formulated. That is why the argument surfaces first as legal and constitutional demands rather than as a philosophical movement, and it is also why Lesson 1's point about bourgeois institutional competence matters here rather than only in Lesson 4.

### 2. The debate the sources themselves stage

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion presents the causal question as a genuine two-sided debate. One side holds that **the Enlightenment delegitimised the old order**; the other that **fiscal crisis, food stress and social inequality made revolt explosive**. The synthesis it recommends is that **ideas and crisis interacted.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "interacted" is only worth writing if you can say how. The defensible account of the interaction has three steps and they are not symmetrical.

```text
STEP 1  Crisis produces distress and fiscal impasse
        -> without argument, this yields riot and petition

STEP 2  Argument supplies a standard above existing law
        -> without crisis, this yields books, salons and complaint

STEP 3  Crisis forces the state to convene a body
        -> the body is staffed by men who already hold the argument
        -> the argument is now inside an institution with a legal title
        -> grievance is restated as an illegitimacy claim, ON THE RECORD
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** step three is the interaction. It is not that ideas plus hunger equals revolution by addition. It is that the fiscal crisis built a room, and the argument was already in the heads of the people who were summoned to fill it.

### 3. Rousseau and the language the Revolution used

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text records that **Rousseau is regarded as the source of inspiration of the great French Revolution (1789)**, describing him as a writer whose ideas "induced the revolutionary upsurge that shook the French polity to its foundations".

✅ **Fact:** The same text records the content that mattered. In the *Social Contract* (1762) Rousseau wrote that the **citizen is a free and autonomous person**, entitled to participate in all those decisions which are binding on all citizens; and, observing the rise of commercial society, he held the clash between the common good and private interests to be inevitable and deliberately gave **prominence to the common good over private interests**.

✅ **Fact:** The same text traces the idea of citizenship forward to this exact point: the idea "reached its zenith with the French Revolution (1789) and the consequent Declaration of Rights of Man and Citizen", and that Declaration **echoes the views of Rousseau**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow, and all three return later in the topic, so it is worth fixing them now.

First, if a citizen is defined by *participation in the decisions that bind him*, then a subject who is merely governed well is not a citizen at all. Good government is no longer a defence. That is a far more radical proposition than a complaint about taxes, and it is the reason the Revolution could not be bought off by concessions.

Second, if the common good is given prominence over private interests, then an institution claiming to express the common good may override particular interests - including, in principle, particular rights. Lesson 9 will have to confront that, because the Terror was carried out by men who believed they were acting for the common good and who could cite a theory that ranked it first. The claim is not that Rousseau caused the Terror; it is that the doctrinal materials for justifying coercion in the name of the common good were present in the tradition the revolutionaries drew on, and that this is a real problem rather than a slander.

Third, the vocabulary of citizenship was not invented in 1789. The same text records that the idea was redefined in seventeenth-century England, popularised by the exponents of the Glorious Revolution of 1688, and became very popular during the American Revolution of 1776, before reaching its zenith in France. ⚠️ The French contribution was therefore not the invention of the word but its **conversion into an operative legal status for an entire population**, which is a far stronger claim and a defensible one.

### 4. The Atlantic context, and the rule that governs it

✅ **Fact:** The standard chronology for the subject states the rule explicitly and states it as a trap: the American Revolution of **1775-83** and the French Revolution of **1789-99** are separate events roughly a decade apart; French revolutionaries were **influenced by, but did not directly cause,** American independence; and the causal arrow runs from Enlightenment ideas to **both** revolutions in parallel, not sequentially from one revolution to the other.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's directive map records the corresponding writing rule for comparative questions: when asked to compare the two revolutions, the demand is the **difference in social depth, violence and the property or feudal question**, and the instruction is explicit - do **not** treat the two as one "Atlantic revolution".

✅ **Fact:** The standard comparison set for the subject supplies the seven dimensions on which the comparison is run.

| Dimension | American Revolution | French Revolution |
|---|---|---|
| Core period | ✅ 1775-1783 | ✅ 1789-1799; Napoleonic phase to 1815 |
| Immediate conflict | ✅ Colonies against British imperial authority | ✅ Society and nation against the Bourbon old regime |
| Main claim | ✅ No taxation without representation; independence | ✅ End privilege; citizenship, popular sovereignty and equality before law |
| Social transformation | ✅ More limited within settler society | ✅ Directly attacked feudal privilege and the estate order |
| Constitutional result | ✅ Durable federal republic and written constitution | ✅ Monarchy, republic, Terror, Directory and Napoleonic empire |
| Key contradiction | ✅ Liberty coexisted with slavery and exclusion | ✅ Universal rights coexisted with gender, colonial and political exclusions |
| World-historical effect | ✅ Model of anti-colonial constitutional revolution | ✅ Wider transformation of citizenship, nationalism and social hierarchy |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read rows two and four together and the rest of the table follows. A revolution against an **external** authority can end when that authority withdraws; a revolution against an **internal** order can only end when the people who benefited from that order have been defeated, conciliated or destroyed. That single difference generates the contrast in rows three, five and seven, and it is the reason the French sequence runs for a decade and passes through a terror while the American does not.

**Where this belongs.** ⚠️ The American narrative - its taxes, its battles, its convention and its constitution - belongs to the topic that owns it, and is not retold here. The American case is used twice: as the comparison above, and in Lesson 16, where a verified examination question asks about the two revolutions jointly. The conceptual analysis of rights, consent and sovereignty belongs to the overview topic.

### 5. The ambiguity control: what a rights-argument can also do

✅ **Fact:** The same political-theory text that supplies the Declaration's language also supplies an important caution about it. Natural-rights doctrine, it records, "remains an ambiguous theory": it was "put forward at a historical juncture with a view to securing favourable conditions for a 'free market society'", and it follows that natural rights "can be interpreted in order to uphold the values of the particular class which invokes these rights".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most valuable sentence in the topic for a candidate who wants to *evaluate* rather than *celebrate*, and it is not a hostile source - it is a standard textbook recording a standard scholarly caution. It tells you that the same rights-language can be the instrument of universal emancipation and the instrument of a particular class's advantage, and that which of these it turns out to be is decided by politics rather than by the text. Lesson 11 shows the point in operation on women, the enslaved and the wage-earner; Lesson 17 shows historians arguing about it.

### 6. What ideas cannot be made to explain

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three limits, each of which prevents a common over-claim.

| Limit | Why it holds |
|---|---|
| Ideas cannot explain the **date** | The arguments were decades old by 1789; the fiscal and subsistence crises were not |
| Ideas cannot explain the **force** | The crowds that made the Assembly survivable were not assembled by reading |
| Ideas cannot explain the **direction of radicalisation** | Nothing in a rights-argument prescribes a republic, a terror or an emperor; those came from war, fear and factional conflict |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and yet the converse over-correction is equally wrong. Remove the argument and the crisis produces demands for relief, not a Declaration abolishing privilege as such. The standard account puts the point in one line - ideas supplied legitimacy, not the grievance - and an answer that reproduces that line with its limitation attached has already shown better judgement than one that argues for either extreme.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** If most of the population never read the philosophers, and if the arguments were available for decades without producing a revolution, then the intellectual cause is doing no real work. It is a retrospective dignity conferred on a bread riot by writers who preferred to credit writers.

**Reply:** The objection holds against a crude version of the claim and fails against the version the sources actually make. Nobody asserts that peasants read Rousseau. The claim is that the argument was held by the men who staffed the institutions - lawyers, officials, pamphleteers, club members - and that it determined what those men did with the opening the fiscal crisis gave them. The test is what was demanded. A crisis without the argument produces demands for tax relief, grain and the removal of particular officials, all of which are compatible with the old regime continuing. What was actually demanded in 1789 was the abolition of privilege as a legal category and a declaration of rights binding on the state - demands that make no sense without the argument and cannot be satisfied inside the order they attack.

**Residual:** One gap should be stated rather than concealed. There is no measure here of how widely the revolutionary argument was actually held among the deputies, and none is offered; and the causal debate is presented as a set of positions rather than as a roll of named schools, for the reasons Lesson 17 sets out. The naming that is done there - Burke, Tocqueville, Raphael, Engels and the Marxian reading - is of interpretations of the Revolution's meaning, which is a checkable and distinct question.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the third body element of a causes answer and the whole of the comparative apparatus. Two specific moves earn marks. The first is stating the transmission caution - through pamphlets, clubs and lawyers, not by direct readership - which shows that the candidate knows how an idea reaches a population. The second is drawing the causal arrow correctly in any question that names both revolutions: ideas to both, in parallel; America influenced and encouraged France; America did not cause France. The verified 2019 question that names both revolutions together is routed in the final question index, and this lesson plus Lesson 16 supply its comparative spine.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that the American Revolution caused the French Revolution.
- Writing that the Enlightenment caused the French Revolution, without the date, force and direction limits.
- Treating the two revolutions as one "Atlantic revolution", against the explicit instruction in the sources.
- Claiming direct popular readership of the philosophers.
- Quoting Rousseau, Robespierre or the Declaration from memory; quote only wording you can check, and paraphrase otherwise.
- Presenting rights-language as unambiguously emancipatory, when the standard caution records that it can be interpreted to uphold the values of the class that invokes it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Ideas supplied **legitimacy, not the grievance**; they convert grievance into a claim of right.
2. ✅ Transmission was mediated through **pamphlets, clubs and lawyers**; most peasants did not read the philosophes.
3. ✅ The debate is staged in the sources as Enlightenment delegitimisation against explosive material crisis, with the synthesis that **ideas and crisis interacted**.
4. ✅ Rousseau is recorded as the source of inspiration of the Revolution; the *Social Contract* (1762) defines the citizen as free and autonomous, entitled to participate in decisions binding on all.
5. ✅ Rousseau gave prominence to the common good over private interests.
6. ✅ The Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen echoes Rousseau's views; citizenship reached its zenith with 1789.
7. ✅ Citizenship was redefined in seventeenth-century England, popularised after 1688, and popular during the American Revolution of 1776, before its French zenith.
8. ✅ American Revolution 1775-83; French Revolution 1789-99; separate events about a decade apart.
9. ✅ French revolutionaries were **influenced by, not caused by**, American independence; the arrow runs from ideas to both in parallel.
10. ✅ Comparative demand: difference in social depth, violence and the property or feudal question; never one "Atlantic revolution".
11. ✅ Seven comparison dimensions: period, immediate conflict, main claim, social transformation, constitutional result, key contradiction, world-historical effect.
12. ✅ Natural-rights doctrine is recorded as ambiguous, capable of being interpreted to uphold the values of the class invoking it.
13. ⚠️ External-authority revolutions can end when the foreigner leaves; internal-order revolutions end only when the beneficiaries are defeated, conciliated or destroyed.
14. ⚠️ Ideas cannot explain the date, the force or the direction of radicalisation - and their absence cannot explain the demands that were actually made.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks you to explain how the American and French Revolutions laid the foundations of the modern world. Set out the causal relationship you must assert between the two revolutions, the relationship you must avoid, and the one comparative distinction that prevents the answer from flattening them.

**Model answer:** The relationship to assert is parallel derivation: the Enlightenment argument that authority must justify itself and that privilege is conventional rather than natural ran to both revolutions independently, and the American case additionally influenced and encouraged the French by example, without causing it. The relationship to avoid is any sequential claim that 1776 produced 1789, since the two are separate events about a decade apart and the sources treat the sequential arrow as an error rather than an oversimplification. The comparative distinction that prevents flattening is depth of social target: the American Revolution overthrew an external imperial authority and transformed settler society comparatively little, producing a durable federal republic under a written constitution, while the French Revolution attacked a domestic legal order of privilege and seigneurial obligation, and consequently passed through monarchy, republic, Terror, Directory and empire before its settlement stabilised. Both left a shared inheritance - rights-language, written constitutionalism and the location of authority in the people or the nation - and both left the same unresolved contradiction, since liberty coexisted with slavery and exclusion in one case and universal rights with gender, colonial and political exclusions in the other. The foundations claim is therefore best written as a division of contribution rather than a ranking.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because both revolutions used the same vocabulary they were the same kind of event. The vocabulary was genuinely shared; the social depth was not, and it is the depth that explains why one produced a stable constitutional republic and the other a decade of escalation ending in an emperor.

**Bridge to Lesson 4:** The structure, the timing, the force and the language are now all in place. What happened next took six weeks, and it is the fastest and most examinable sequence in the topic. The next lesson follows it day by day.

## Lesson 4 - Six weeks in 1789: how a tax assembly made itself sovereign

Progress: 4/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Estates-General to National Assembly; the Tennis Court Oath; the Bastille; the August decrees and the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's dated mini-timeline with its significance column, its chapter-spine section on the outbreak of the Revolution, its date-control list, its trap set on the Bastille and the Declaration, and its evidence bank on popular sovereignty; and a standard political-theory text for the verified wording of the Declaration
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the six weeks, with what each step converted

```text
 MAY 1789   ESTATES-GENERAL MEETS
            a consultative body summoned to authorise taxation
            status: the king's instrument
                      |
                      |  the question it cannot avoid:
                      |  how does it vote, and whom does it represent?
                      v
 17 JUNE    THIRD ESTATE DECLARES ITSELF THE NATIONAL ASSEMBLY
            converts REPRESENTATION OF AN ORDER
                  into REPRESENTATION OF THE NATION
            -> sovereignty begins shifting from king to nation
                      |
                      v
 20 JUNE    TENNIS COURT OATH
            converts A CLAIM TO REPRESENT
                  into A CLAIM TO CONSTITUTE
            -> constitution-making becomes the revolutionary claim
                      |
                      |  a claim of this size needs protection
                      v
 14 JULY    BASTILLE STORMED
            converts A LEGAL CLAIM
                  into AN IRREVERSIBLE PUBLIC FACT
            -> the fall of autocracy becomes a mass symbol
                      |
                      v
 4 AUG      DECREE ABOLISHING THE FEUDAL SYSTEM; TITHES
            ABOLISHED; CHURCH LANDS CONFISCATED
 AUG 1789   THE DECLARATION
            converts A NEW AUTHORITY
                  into A NEW STANDARD BINDING ON ALL AUTHORITY
            -> rights, equality before law and citizenship proclaimed
                      |
                      v
            THE OLD REGIME IS NOW LEGALLY DEAD
            what remains is the contest over what replaces it
```

*Five steps, five conversions. Learn the middle column and you can reconstruct the whole sequence under examination conditions, because each step is defined by what it changed rather than by what happened.*

### 1. The question the Estates-General could not avoid

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's timeline opens with the **Estates-General meeting in May 1789**, recording its significance as the moment the fiscal crisis opens the old regime to challenge.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** an assembly of three orders has to decide one procedural question before it can do anything else - whether it deliberates and votes as three separate orders or as one body. The question looks technical and is not. Voting by order gives each of the three an equal weight, so the two privileged orders can always outvote the third; voting as one body gives weight to numbers, and the third order is the overwhelming majority of the nation. The procedural question is therefore the sovereignty question in disguise, and it arrived on the first day.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records the underlying asymmetry in its must-know set: the **Third Estate represented the overwhelming majority but lacked privilege.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** which produces the argument that decides everything. If the nation is the people, and the Third Estate is almost all of the people, then an assembly of the Third Estate is not one order out of three - it is the nation in the room, with the other two orders as privileged minorities who may join it. That is not a compromise position; it is a redefinition, and it does not need the king's agreement to be stated.

### 2. 17 June: representation becomes sovereignty

✅ **Fact:** The standard account dates the decisive act exactly: on **17 June 1789 the Third Estate declares itself the National Assembly**, and the recorded significance is that **sovereignty begins shifting from king to nation.**

✅ **Fact:** The same account's evidence bank lists this act, together with the Tennis Court Oath and the abolition of the monarchy in 1792, as what established **popular sovereignty**, and records its permanent consequence: it makes **constituent power - "who may make the constitution" - a permanent question.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the verb in the source: sovereignty *begins* shifting. Not completes. A declaration by an assembly does not by itself transfer anything; it states a claim that then has to survive. The next three steps are the survival of that claim, and the whole of the rest of the topic is the working-out of what a claim of that kind lets people do.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase **constituent power** is worth adopting deliberately, because it is the concept that gives this topic its modern relevance. There is ordinary law-making power, exercised under a constitution, and there is the power to make the constitution itself. The old regime had no theory of the second, because the constitution was simply the accumulated arrangement of the realm. From 17 June 1789 onward, every European political argument has to answer the question of who holds constituent power, and it is a question that no settlement fully closes. Lesson 16 shows the question still open.

### 3. 20 June: the Tennis Court Oath

✅ **Fact:** The standard account dates the **Tennis Court Oath to 20 June 1789** and records its significance as the moment at which **constitution-making becomes the revolutionary claim.**

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion lists the Tennis Court Oath first among the indispensable markers of the topic, alongside the Bastille, the Rights of Man, the Jacobins, the Directory, the Napoleonic Code and Waterloo.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the step from 17 to 20 June is small in time and large in kind. On 17 June the Assembly says what it *is*. On 20 June it says what it will *do*, and binds itself to do it - which converts a status claim into a programme with a terminal condition attached. An assembly that has undertaken not to disperse until it has produced a constitution has made its own dissolution into a public defeat rather than a routine administrative act. That is a deliberate raising of the stakes, and it is the point at which retreat by either side becomes visibly costly.

✅ **Fact:** The Assembly's move was led by **Mirabeau**, born into a noble family but convinced of the need to end a society of feudal privilege, and by **Abbé Sieyès**, originally a priest, who wrote the influential pamphlet ***What is the Third Estate?***

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two names are worth carrying because of what they prove between them. A noble and a priest leading the Third Estate's self-declaration show that the rupture was not a clean confrontation between estates but a split *within* the privileged orders as well - which is precisely why the old regime could not close ranks against it.

### 4. 14 July: the Bastille, and the trap attached to it

✅ **Fact:** The standard account dates the **storming of the Bastille to 14 July 1789** and records its significance in one line: **the fall of autocracy becomes a mass symbol.** It is repeated in the must-know set in exactly that form.

✅ **Fact:** The same account carries an explicit correction, and it is one of the most frequently tested points in the topic. The misconception is that "the Bastille was important because many prisoners were inside"; the correction is that **its symbolic political value mattered more than the prisoner count.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** take the correction seriously rather than merely reciting it, because it teaches something general about revolutions. A fortress-prison in the capital is the physical form of arbitrary power - of the proposition that the state may detain a subject without answering to anyone. Its capture by a crowd demonstrates three things simultaneously, and none of them depends on who was inside. It demonstrates that the state's coercive machinery can fail; that the capital's population will act; and that the Assembly's legal claim now has an armed constituency. The number of prisoners is irrelevant to all three.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and this is where Lesson 2's conjunction becomes concrete. The Assembly had a claim and no force. The crowd had force and, by itself, no programme. On 14 July the two became available to one another. From that date, dispersing the Assembly would have required a confrontation with the population of the capital, which is a different proposition from dismissing a body of lawyers.

### 5. August 1789: the decrees and the Declaration

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's date-control list keeps two August events distinct: the **August decrees** and the **Rights Declaration** of 1789. Its timeline records the Declaration under **August 1789**, with the significance that **rights, equality before law and citizenship are proclaimed.**

✅ **Fact:** In its must-know set the same account states the content again: the **Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen proclaimed equality before law and rights of citizenship.**

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text supplies the Declaration's opening propositions in a verified form. It records that the **French Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen (1789)** expressed the idea as follows: "Men are born and remain free and equal in rights. Social distinctions can be based only upon public utility." It further records: "The aim of every political association is the preservation of natural and imprescriptible rights of man. These rights are liberty, property, security and resistance to oppression."

✅ **Fact:** The same text records that the Declaration was **inspired by Rousseau's revolutionary ideas**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read those two sentences as engineering rather than as rhetoric, because that is how marks are earned on them.

| The clause | What it abolishes | What it installs |
|---|---|---|
| "Men are born and remain free and equal in rights" | Rights as attributes of status | Rights as attributes of birth, therefore universal and non-forfeitable |
| "Social distinctions can be based only upon public utility" | Distinction by estate, lineage or privilege | A **justification requirement**: a distinction must now demonstrate a public purpose |
| "The aim of every political association is the preservation of... rights" | Government as an end in itself, owed obedience | Government as an instrument with a stated purpose, and therefore assessable against it |
| "liberty, property, security and resistance to oppression" | The duty of unconditional submission | A named right of resistance, written into the founding document |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second row is the sharpest and the least used by candidates. The Declaration does not abolish all social distinction; it subjects distinction to a test. Anyone who wishes to maintain a distinction must now say what public purpose it serves - which is exactly the demand that no defence of hereditary privilege can meet. That is a more precise and more defensible claim than "the Declaration proclaimed equality", and it is also what allows the same document to coexist, uncomfortably, with the property qualifications and exclusions of Lesson 11.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account attaches the correction that prevents over-claiming: the misconception that "the Declaration of Rights immediately ended all inequality" is answered by the statement that **it transformed law and legitimacy, but practice remained conflict-ridden.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account dates and describes the first of the two August acts precisely. On the **night of 4 August 1789** the Assembly passed a decree **abolishing the feudal system of obligations and taxes**; members of the clergy were forced to give up their privileges; **tithes were abolished**; and **lands owned by the Church were confiscated**, bringing the government assets worth at least two billion livres.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** keep the two August acts apart, because they do different work. The decree of 4 August destroys the **material** structure of privilege - dues, services, tithes and clerical immunities. The Declaration later that month states the **standard** by which any future arrangement will be judged. An answer that merges them loses the distinction between abolishing an order and constituting a new one, which is the distinction Lesson 12's whole verdict rests on. Note also the item with the longest consequences: confiscating Church lands solved a fiscal problem and created a religious one, and Lesson 7 follows what that cost.

### 6. Why the sequence is taught as a sequence

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each step in this lesson was made possible by the one before it and would have failed without it. That is worth stating explicitly, because it converts a chronology into an argument.

```text
Without the SUMMONS      there is no body with a legal title
Without 17 JUNE          the body remains one order among three
Without 20 JUNE          the claim to represent has no programme
Without 14 JULY          the programme has no protection
Without AUGUST           the protected programme produces no binding standard
Without the STANDARD     there is nothing for later movements to appeal to
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last line is the one that matters for the enduring-relevance question. The Revolution's most durable product was not a government - it produced several, and all of them fell - but a **text stating a standard**, which is the thing that outlived every government it was written for.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This is a Paris-centred, document-centred account. Six weeks of assembly proceedings and one crowd action in the capital cannot constitute a revolution in a country of twenty-odd millions, most of whom lived in villages and knew nothing of the tennis court.
