**Reply:** The objection is correct and the lesson is deliberately incomplete; Lesson 5 supplies what it leaves out. But the incompleteness runs the other way too. Rural action without the legal rupture produces local disorder that the state eventually suppresses, as it had before. The legal rupture without rural action produces a constitutional settlement on paper that the countryside ignores. What makes 1789 a revolution rather than either a riot or a reform is that both occurred, and that the Assembly then wrote the countryside's demand into law. The Paris sequence is therefore necessary to the explanation and insufficient on its own - which is precisely why the two lessons are separate and adjacent.

**Residual:** The dates and the significance of each step are firm; the parliamentary detail behind them - the deliberations, the voting arrangements finally adopted, the king's successive responses - is a specialist matter and is not reconstructed here. The conversions carry the argument, and no answer needs the procedural record.

### UPSC application

This is the lesson that supplies the narrative spine, and the discipline it requires is compression. In a 250-word answer the six weeks are worth three sentences, not a paragraph, and the sentences should carry the conversions rather than the events: a consultative body redefined itself as the nation, bound itself to produce a constitution, was protected by a popular rising that made its dissolution impossible, and then issued a standard binding on all future authority. The Bastille correction and the "did not end inequality" correction are both high-frequency and both cheap to include. For any question using the words *popular sovereignty* or *constituent power*, 17 and 20 June are the anchors.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Explaining the Bastille by the number of prisoners rather than by its symbolic political value.
- Merging the decree of 4 August with the Declaration; they are separately dated and do different work - one destroys the material structure of privilege, the other states the standard.
- Writing that the Declaration ended inequality; it transformed law and legitimacy while practice remained conflict-ridden.
- Quoting the Declaration from memory in unverified wording.
- Dating the National Assembly, the Oath and the Bastille loosely to "1789" when the examiner rewards 17 June, 20 June and 14 July.
- Treating the Assembly's self-declaration as a completed transfer of sovereignty rather than the beginning of a shift that still had to survive.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **May 1789** - Estates-General meets; fiscal crisis opens the old regime to challenge.
2. ✅ **17 June 1789** - the Third Estate declares itself the National Assembly; sovereignty begins shifting from king to nation.
3. ✅ **20 June 1789** - Tennis Court Oath; constitution-making becomes the revolutionary claim.
4. ✅ **14 July 1789** - Bastille stormed; the fall of autocracy becomes a mass symbol.
5. ✅ **4 August 1789** - decree abolishing the feudal system of obligations and taxes; tithes abolished; Church lands confiscated. **August 1789** - the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen; rights, equality before law and citizenship proclaimed.
6. ✅ The Third Estate represented the overwhelming majority but lacked privilege.
7. ✅ Correction: the Bastille mattered for its symbolic political value, not the prisoner count.
8. ✅ Correction: the Declaration transformed law and legitimacy; it did not immediately end all inequality.
9. ✅ Declaration, verified wording: "Men are born and remain free and equal in rights. Social distinctions can be based only upon public utility."
10. ✅ Declaration, verified wording: "The aim of every political association is the preservation of natural and imprescriptible rights of man. These rights are liberty, property, security and resistance to oppression."
11. ✅ The Declaration is recorded as inspired by Rousseau's revolutionary ideas.
12. ✅ 17 June and 20 June, with 1792, are what the sources list as establishing popular sovereignty and making constituent power a permanent question.
13. ⚠️ The five conversions: order → nation; representing → constituting; legal claim → public fact; new authority → binding standard; and a standard that outlives every government built on it.
14. ⚠️ "Social distinctions can be based only upon public utility" is a justification requirement, not an abolition of all distinction - which is why it can coexist with the later exclusions and still condemn them.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the storming of the Bastille is treated as a turning point even though the Assembly's decisive legal acts had already occurred in June - and state what the event contributed that no assembly resolution could have supplied.

**Model answer:** By 20 June the Assembly had already made the two claims that mattered: on 17 June it redefined itself from one order among three into the representative body of the nation, beginning the shift of sovereignty from king to nation, and on 20 June it bound itself to produce a constitution, so that constitution-making became the revolutionary claim. What those acts could not supply was enforceability, since an assembly asserting a title it cannot defend is making a request rather than a revolution. The events of 14 July supplied exactly that missing element by demonstrating that the state's coercive apparatus could fail, that the population of the capital would act, and that the Assembly's claim now had an armed constituency behind it - which together made the Assembly's dissolution a confrontation with the city rather than an administrative decision. This is also why the standard correction about the Bastille matters: the fortress mattered as the physical embodiment of arbitrary detention rather than for the number of prisoners inside, so its capture registered as the fall of autocracy becoming a mass symbol. The June acts changed what the Assembly was; July changed what could be done to it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Bastille as the start of the Revolution. The legal rupture preceded it by four weeks, and an answer that opens with 14 July has already lost the sovereignty argument that 17 and 20 June contain.

**Bridge to Lesson 5:** The crowd has now appeared twice as a force that made the legal rupture survivable, and both times it has been described from the Assembly's point of view. That is not good enough. The next lesson treats popular action as a cause in its own right, with its own aims, in the countryside as well as the capital.

## Lesson 5 - The Revolution from below: peasants, artisans and the Paris crowd

Progress: 5/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: rural anti-seigneurial action; the urban artisan and the sans-culotte politics; the crowd as a political actor rather than a backdrop; what popular pressure achieved and what it could not

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the historiographical companion's insistence that the Revolution rested on workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd and its table of what each social force wanted and obtained, together with the standard account's old-regime rows on the peasantry and urban workers and its phase table's attribution of each phase's social base
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three theatres, three tempos, one revolution

```text
  THE ASSEMBLY (Versailles, then Paris)
  instrument: decree            tempo: weeks
  aim: abolish privilege, write a constitution
  weakness: no force of its own
                |
                | needs protection            supplies legality
                v                                     ^
  THE CAPITAL (Paris)                                  |
  instrument: crowd action      tempo: days            |
  aim: bread, political voice, social equality         |
  weakness: no machinery to legislate ----------------->
                |
                | sets the outer limit of what the Assembly may concede
                v
  THE COUNTRYSIDE (everywhere else)
  instrument: refusal and direct action   tempo: seasons
  aim: end of seigneurial dues, rents and charges
  weakness: cannot capture the state; does not need to
                |
                v
  OUTCOME: the Assembly legislates what the countryside has
  already stopped paying, and the capital keeps the Assembly alive
  while it does so
```

*Three actors with different instruments, different clocks and different aims. Almost every failure of analysis in this topic comes from collapsing them into one "revolutionary movement" with a single programme.*

### 1. The instruction the sources give, and why it is unusual

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion states the point directly and unusually firmly: the older survey is "especially useful because it shows that the Revolution rested **not only on elite thinkers but also on workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd**."

✅ **Fact:** It converts that into an explicit writing rule: in Mains answers, **do not write the event as if pamphlets alone toppled the old regime.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is worth pausing on the fact that a source thinks this warning necessary. The warning exists because the documentary record is systematically biased toward the articulate. Assemblies leave minutes; writers leave books; crowds leave neither. A candidate who reconstructs the Revolution from what was written will therefore reconstruct a revolution made by writers - which is the error the source is trying to prevent, and which is also, as Lesson 17 will show, one of the oldest fault lines in the historiography.

### 2. The countryside: the largest and least visible revolution

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records the peasant condition as rent, dues, taxes and bad harvests making life insecure, and the resulting political force as **anti-feudal anger**.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records what the peasantry wanted and what it obtained: relief from **dues, rents and feudal burdens**, and, as achievement, **major anti-feudal gains**.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records the outcome in its Napoleon section and it is the most consequential sentence in the topic: **feudal laws were annulled, and many revolutionary gains survived even after Napoleon seized power.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the rural revolution has three properties that distinguish it from everything else in the topic, and all three are examinable.

First, it is **decentralised**. It did not need a capital, a leadership or a programme, because its method was refusal - to pay, to render, to acknowledge. A refusal repeated in thousands of villages does not require coordination to be effective; it requires only that enforcement become impossible.

Second, it is **self-completing**. Once obligations have gone unpaid for a season or two, and the records and the will to enforce them have gone, the legislation that abolishes them is ratifying an accomplished fact rather than creating one. This is why the abolition proved so hard to reverse: reversal would have required re-establishing, one village at a time, obligations that had already lapsed in practice.

Third, it is **satisfiable**. Unlike the artisan demand for cheap bread, the peasant demand had an end point. Once the dues were gone, the demand was met. That is why the countryside is a revolutionary force in 1789 and, increasingly, a conservative one thereafter: a group whose central demand has been satisfied has more to lose than to gain from further upheaval.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third property is the key to the whole second half of the topic. It explains why a regime that guaranteed the abolition of feudal dues could command rural acquiescence even while abolishing the republic - which is the single most important thing to understand about Napoleon's domestic position, and it is established here rather than in Lesson 14.

✅ **Fact:** The rural rising of **July-August 1789** has a standard name - the **Great Fear**. The standard school account describes its mechanism: rumours spread from village to village that the lords of the manor had hired bands of **brigands** who were on their way to destroy the ripe crops; caught in a frenzy of fear, peasants in several districts seized hoes and pitchforks, attacked **chateaux**, looted hoarded grain and **burnt the documents containing records of manorial dues**. Large numbers of nobles fled their homes, many migrating to neighbouring countries.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the detail that matters is the last one. Burning the records is not incidental violence; it is the precise act that makes an obligation unenforceable, because a due that cannot be evidenced cannot be collected. That is why the decree of 4 August ratified an accomplished fact, and it is the concrete form of this lesson's claim that the rural revolution was self-completing.

### 3. The capital: the crowd as a political instrument

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records urban workers and artisans as facing long hours and poverty, forming secret societies and producing unrest, and it classifies their contribution as **mass pressure from below**.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records their aims and their results: **cheap bread, political voice and social equality**; they **helped the radical phase**, but **economic equality remained incomplete**.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table attributes each phase's social base explicitly. The years 1789-91 are ⚠️ bourgeois-led constitutionalism **with peasant and crowd pressure**; 1792-94 are ⚠️ Jacobin republicanism **resting on sans-culotte Paris**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the change between those two rows is the most important social fact of the decade, and it is a change of *position*, not of *personnel*. In 1789-91 the crowd is a pressure on a bourgeois-led assembly - it can push, protect and threaten, but the programme is not its own. In 1792-94 the governing faction **rests on** it - which means the crowd's demands are now inside the programme. That is what radicalisation means concretely, and it is a better definition than any adjective.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the term **sans-culotte** deserves handling with care. The sources use it to name the politics of the urban working population of the capital - the artisans, small tradesmen and wage-earners whose demands were bread, voice and social equality - and that is the sense used throughout this session. It is a political description rather than an economic class in the later industrial sense, and an answer should use it as the sources do, without importing a nineteenth-century proletariat into an eighteenth-century city of workshops.

### 4. What popular force actually accomplished - four things

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** to avoid both romanticism and dismissal, state the achievements as specific functions.

| Function | Evidence |
|---|---|
| **Protection** - it made the Assembly's dissolution impossible | ✅ The Bastille's significance is recorded as the fall of autocracy becoming a mass symbol; ✅ the 1789-91 phase is recorded as resting on peasant and crowd pressure alongside bourgeois leadership |
| **Acceleration** - it turned drafting into abolition | ✅ Feudal laws were annulled, and the annulment survived Napoleon |
| **Radicalisation** - it supplied the social base of the republic | ✅ The 1792-94 phase is recorded as Jacobin republicanism resting on sans-culotte Paris |
| **Extension of citizenship** - it widened the political nation | ✅ The older survey treats 1792 as the moment when workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes first gained equal political rights in the French context |

✅ **Fact:** That last row is recorded in the historiographical companion in precisely those terms, and it is one of the most useful dates in the topic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is useful because it fixes what 1789 did *not* do. If equal political rights for the non-propertied date to 1792, then the settlement of 1789-91 conditioned political rights on property - and the Declaration's universal language was, in its first institutional form, administered by a restricted electorate. Lesson 6 takes up that gap as the central weakness of the constitutional monarchy, and Lesson 11 takes it up as an exclusion.

### 5. What popular force could not accomplish

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion is explicit: economic equality **remained incomplete**, and it attaches a trap to the point - **never say the French Revolution achieved full social equality**, because the older survey itself records that workers and artisans still lacked economic equality.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reason returns to Lesson 1's distinction. The peasant demand was an abolition and could be completed by a decree. The artisan demand was a **provision** - bread at a price people could pay, and a social order in which wage-earners were not merely equal before the law - and a provision must be produced continuously by a state with the resources and the will to produce it. No eighteenth-century state had either. The artisans therefore obtained the one thing that could be legislated, which was political voice, and not the thing they most immediately needed, which was subsistence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and this is the origin of the nineteenth century's central political argument, which the overview topic owns in doctrinal form: if legal equality has been achieved and political equality is being extended, what is the status of the claim that neither means anything without economic security? The French Revolution does not answer that question. It is the event that makes the question unavoidable.

### 6. The standard formulation for an answer

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the compressed version, usable in any question on the Revolution's social character:

> The Revolution was led in its institutions by a bourgeoisie that wanted privilege abolished and careers opened; it was made survivable by a capital that wanted bread and a voice; and it was made irreversible by a countryside that simply stopped paying. Each of the three obtained something different, and the differences are the reason the coalition could not hold.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Emphasising popular agency risks the opposite distortion - a crowd that is credited with everything and held responsible for nothing. Crowds did not write the Declaration, did not design the constitution, did not administer the new departments and did not codify the law. Every durable product of the Revolution was produced by educated men in assemblies.

**Reply:** The objection is right about authorship and wrong about causation, and the distinction is worth making explicitly because examiners reward it. Authorship and causation are different questions. Educated men in assemblies authored the texts; they did so under conditions - the survival of their assembly, the impossibility of enforcement in the countryside, the presence of an armed capital - that they did not create and could not have created. The sources record both halves: bourgeois leadership of the institutional rupture, and peasants and urban crowds driving the radicalisation. The defensible verdict the companion recommends is a **multi-class revolution with shifting leadership**, which concedes the authorship point and keeps the causal one.

**Residual:** What remains genuinely unsettled is the degree of autonomy of popular action - whether the crowds acted on aims of their own or were mobilised by political factions for factional purposes. The sources record the crowd's aims and the factions' dependence on it without adjudicating the direction of influence. This session records the dependence, records the aims, and does not claim to have resolved which was driving which.

### UPSC application

This lesson is what converts a competent answer into a good one on any question about the Revolution's social character, its transformation of society, or its phases. The examiner is looking for two things: that the candidate does not write a revolution made by writers, and that the candidate can distinguish what different social groups wanted and obtained. The four-function table in section 4 is the fastest way to demonstrate both. For the *how far did it transform society* directive, this lesson supplies the graded answer - complete for legal status, substantial for the peasantry, partial for urban workers - which Lesson 12 completes and Lesson 18 formalises.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing as if pamphlets alone toppled the old regime, against the explicit instruction in the sources.
- Claiming the Revolution achieved full social equality; the sources record that workers and artisans still lacked economic equality.
- Treating "the people" as one actor with one programme, when the countryside, the capital and the assembly wanted different things.
- Missing that equal political rights for the non-propertied are dated by the sources to the 1792-93 phase, not to 1789.
- Importing a modern industrial proletariat into the description of the artisan population of the capital.
- Reducing the Great Fear to generic "peasant unrest" and so losing the decisive detail that the records of manorial dues were burnt.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Revolution rested not only on elite thinkers but on workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd.
2. ✅ Writing rule from the sources: never present the event as if pamphlets alone toppled the old regime.
3. ✅ Peasant aims: relief from dues, rents and feudal burdens; achievement: major anti-feudal gains.
4. ✅ Feudal laws were annulled, and many revolutionary gains survived even after Napoleon seized power.
5. ✅ Urban worker aims: cheap bread, political voice, social equality; they helped the radical phase; economic equality remained incomplete.
6. ✅ Phase base, 1789-91: bourgeois-led constitutionalism with peasant and crowd pressure.
7. ✅ Phase base, 1792-94: Jacobin republicanism resting on sans-culotte Paris.
8. ✅ 1792 is recorded as the moment when workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes first gained equal political rights in the French context.
9. ✅ Trap: never say the Revolution achieved full social equality.
10. ⚠️ The rural revolution was decentralised, self-completing and satisfiable - which is why it stuck and why the countryside became conservative once satisfied.
11. ⚠️ Radicalisation is best defined structurally: the crowd moves from being a pressure on the programme to being inside it.
12. ⚠️ Four functions of popular force: protection, acceleration, radicalisation, extension of citizenship.
13. ⚠️ Abolitions can be completed; provisions must be sustained - which is why artisan demands could not be met.
14. ⚠️ Authorship and causation are different questions; conceding the first does not concede the second.
15. ⚠️ Verdict formula: led by the bourgeoisie, made survivable by the capital, made irreversible by the countryside.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the peasantry's gains from the Revolution proved almost impossible to reverse while the urban artisans' gains did not - and state what this contrast predicts about the political behaviour of each group after 1795.

**Model answer:** The peasant demand was for the extinction of seigneurial dues, rents and feudal burdens, and an extinction is a single legal act that can be completed; once obligations had ceased to be rendered across thousands of villages and the machinery and will to enforce them had lapsed, legislation annulling feudal laws ratified an accomplished fact rather than creating a new one, which is why the sources record that feudal laws were annulled and that this gain survived even Napoleon's seizure of power. The artisan demand was for cheap bread, a political voice and social equality, and only the second of these is a legal act; the first and third are continuous provisions requiring a state with the resources and the will to supply them indefinitely, which no eighteenth-century state possessed, so the sources record that they helped the radical phase while economic equality remained incomplete. The prediction follows directly. A group whose central demand has been satisfied and is legally secured has more to lose than to gain from further upheaval, and will support whatever regime guarantees the settlement - which is the basis of rural acquiescence in the authoritarian consolidation after 1799. A group whose central demand remains unmet has no such stake, but having supplied force rather than institutional leadership it has no machinery through which to press the demand once the faction that rested on it has fallen, which is why the artisan movement's political weight declines after the radical phase ends.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the peasantry was the revolutionary class and the artisans merely its auxiliaries. The two contributed different things at different tempos - the countryside made the social transformation irreversible, the capital made the political rupture survivable - and neither would have sufficed without the other or without the assembly that converted both into law.

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** Privilege is abolished, the countryside has stopped paying, the capital is armed and a constitution is being written. On paper, the Revolution is over and has succeeded. The next lesson asks why the settlement that resulted lasted barely two years.

## Lesson 6 - The constitutional monarchy, 1789-91: what was achieved, and why it did not hold

Progress: 6/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the settlement of 1791; equality before the law against political equality; the property condition on citizenship; the three unresolved crises that destroyed it

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's timeline entry for 1791 and its phase table with the recorded reason each phase gave way, its evidence bank on citizenship and its record that equal political rights for the non-propertied date to the 1792-93 phase; and a standard political-theory text on the distinction between legal, political and social equality and on the class reading of bourgeois equality
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what the 1791 settlement settled, and what it left standing

```text
   SETTLED                              LEFT STANDING
   -------                              -------------
   legal privilege abolished            the state's insolvency
   rights declared as a standard        the Church's position
   constitutional monarchy              a king with a stake in failure
   established                          hostile monarchies abroad
   feudal obligations annulled          bread prices
   careers opened in principle          who counts as a full citizen
          |                                     |
          |                                     |
          v                                     v
   AN ORDER THAT CANNOT BE            THREE CRISES THAT THE
   RESTORED                           SETTLEMENT CANNOT RESOLVE
          |                                     |
          +------------------+------------------+
                             |
                             v
        A REGIME THAT IS IRREVERSIBLE AND UNSTABLE AT ONCE
        -> the old regime cannot come back
        -> this settlement cannot continue
        -> therefore the movement must go forward, not stop
```

*The two columns are the whole lesson. A revolution that has destroyed what it attacked but cannot stabilise what it built has only one available direction, and that is the structural reason for radicalisation - before any personality, faction or foreign power is introduced.*

### 1. What the phase actually achieved

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table records the years **1789-91** as bourgeois-led constitutionalism with peasant and crowd pressure, and lists its achievements as the **end of legal privilege, the Declaration, and constitutional monarchy.**

✅ **Fact:** The same account's four-phase summary describes 1789-91 as National Assembly, Bastille, Rights of Man and constitutional monarchy, with the note that the **end of legal privilege begins**.

✅ **Fact:** The timeline entry for **1791** states the position in one line: **the old regime is legally broken but instability continues.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that sentence is the most economical statement of the whole phase and it deserves to be used as written. "Legally broken" is a completed achievement. "Instability continues" is not a qualification of it but a separate fact about everything the achievement did not touch. Candidates routinely write the first half and stop, which produces an answer in which the Revolution inexplicably continues after it has succeeded.

### 2. The equality that was achieved, and the equality that was not

This is the conceptual core of the lesson and it is where the marks are.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text records the distinction with unusual clarity, quoting a standard work of political theory: "The men of 1789 sought 'equality before the law', which for them meant eliminating aristocratic legal privileges and feudal obligations. The **Jacobins**, and the nineteenth century Republicans on whom their mantle descended, sought '**political equality**' or universal suffrage. To socialists these ends by themselves seem inadequate; they are, at best ways of achieving 'real' equality - **social equality**. Without that, 'political equality' is an illusion."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that passage is a gift, because it supplies the entire internal logic of the French decade in three named stages and attaches each to an identifiable political group.

```text
STAGE 1   EQUALITY BEFORE THE LAW      "the men of 1789"
          eliminate aristocratic legal privileges
          and feudal obligations
          -> achieved by the 1789-91 settlement
                     |
                     |  but who may vote?
                     v
STAGE 2   POLITICAL EQUALITY           the Jacobins, and after them
          universal suffrage            the nineteenth-century Republicans
          -> the demand of the next phase
                     |
                     |  but what is a vote worth without security?
                     v
STAGE 3   SOCIAL EQUALITY               socialists
          -> a nineteenth-century argument, not a French
             revolutionary achievement
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the diagram explains why the settlement of 1791 could not be a terminus. It had completed stage one and, in completing it, had made stage two askable. A society that has just abolished distinctions based on birth cannot easily explain why distinctions based on property should determine who may vote - and once that question has been asked out loud, the settlement is arguing from a weaker position than the one it has just destroyed.

✅ **Fact:** The sources supply the evidence that the settlement was indeed property-conditioned at this stage. The standard account records that citizenship "was gendered and property-conditioned by turns", and that it was **contested, not conferred once**; and the historiographical companion records that the older survey treats **1792** as the moment when workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes first gained equal political rights in the French context.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second of those facts is decisive by implication. If equal political rights for the non-propertied arrive in 1792, then the settlement immediately before 1792 did not grant them. The 1791 constitution therefore combined a universal declaration with a conditioned franchise - which is exactly the structure that its critics attacked and its defenders could not justify from its own premises.

### 3. Why property was written in, and the strongest form of the criticism

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the property condition was argued for, not simply assumed, and reconstructing the argument is what distinguishes evaluation from denunciation. The case ran that independence of judgement requires independence of circumstance: a man dependent on another for wages, tenancy or charity would vote as his superior directed, so extending the franchise to him would enlarge the patron's vote rather than create a new one. That is not a foolish argument. Its consequence is nevertheless exact - political rights tracked property, and property tracked the existing distribution of social power.

✅ **Fact:** The Declaration itself names **property** among the natural and imprescriptible rights whose preservation is the aim of every political association, in the wording a standard political-theory text supplies.

✅ **Fact:** The same text records the strongest criticism of that arrangement, quoting Engels in *Anti-Dühring* (1878): "This equality reduced itself to bourgeois equality before the law; ... bourgeois property was proclaimed as one of the essential rights of man".

✅ **Fact:** It records the corresponding general critique too: the new middle class invoked the principle of equality against the feudal system which blocked the development of the productive forces of the emerging capitalist system, and the demand for liberty, equality and fraternity became the watchword of 1789; but when that class came to power, **it refused to extend the principle of equality in substantive terms to the working class.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** handle this material with precision rather than enthusiasm, because it is easy to over-use. What it establishes is that the 1791 settlement had a **determinate class shape** and that the shape was visible to contemporaries and near-contemporaries. What it does not establish is that the settlement was a conspiracy or that its universal language was insincere. The more interesting and more defensible position is the one Lesson 3's ambiguity control sets up: the same rights-language that was given a property-conditioned institutional form also supplied the standard by which that form could be attacked - and within three years it was attacked successfully, which is what 1792 records.

### 4. The three unresolved crises

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table states the reason the phase gave way in five words: **fiscal, religious and war crises unresolved.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** those are three genuinely different kinds of failure, and an answer that distinguishes them is doing the examiner's work correctly.

| Crisis | Why the settlement could not resolve it | Where it is taught |
|---|---|---|
| **Fiscal** | The Revolution had abolished the old fiscal order without having built a new one that could fund the state; the original problem of 1789 was not solved by 1791 | Lesson 2 sets it up; the sources record no mechanism of solution, and none is asserted here |
| **Religious** | The attack on privilege necessarily reached the Church, and the Church's position could not be adjusted without raising the question of its authority | Lesson 7 |
| **War** | A revolution that declared universal principles and abolished a monarchy's prerogatives existed in a Europe of monarchies; the settlement had no means of making itself safe abroad | Lesson 8 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that the settlement's own success generates two of the three. Abolishing privilege reaches the Church; proclaiming universal rights alarms neighbours. Only the fiscal crisis is inherited rather than created. That is the precise sense in which the constitutional monarchy was undone by what it had achieved, and it is a much better sentence than the usual one about the king's untrustworthiness.

### 5. The monarch inside a constitutional monarchy - a structural problem

✅ **Fact:** The **Constitution of 1791**, completed by the National Assembly, separated the powers previously concentrated in one person and assigned them to a legislature, an executive and a judiciary, making France a **constitutional monarchy** with the king retaining a **veto**. Law-making was vested in an indirectly elected assembly: citizens voted for electors, who in turn chose the deputies. Only men above **25** who paid taxes equal to at least **three days of a labourer's wage** were **active citizens** entitled to vote - about **4 million** of a population of **28 million**. The remaining men, about three million, and **all women**, were classed as **passive citizens**. To qualify as an elector, and then as a deputy, a man had to belong to the highest bracket of taxpayers.

✅ **Fact:** The assembly elected under this constitution, which sat from **October 1791** until the **Convention** replaced it in **September 1792**, is conventionally called the **Legislative Assembly**; the standard school account refers to it throughout simply as the National Assembly.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the active-and-passive distinction is the single most useful fact in this lesson, because it converts an abstraction into arithmetic. A declaration that men are born free and equal in rights was administered by a franchise that enfranchised roughly one adult in seven of the population and excluded every woman by category. That is not a scandal discovered later; it is printed in the constitution, and it is why the demand of 1792 was already forming while the ink was drying.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a second structural difficulty needs no judgement about the individual monarch. A constitutional monarchy asks a hereditary ruler to exercise reduced powers under a constitution he did not consent to and which abolished the order that justified his position. His compliance is necessary for the settlement's legitimacy, and his interest lies in its failure. The settlement therefore depends for its survival on the sustained good faith of the person with the clearest reason to withhold it.

### 6. Why the sources call this "bourgeois-led constitutionalism"

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the label in the phase table is precise and each word is doing work. *Bourgeois* identifies the leading group; *led* concedes that others were present and pressing; *constitutionalism* names the method, which was to write a binding instrument rather than to seize and redistribute. Compare it with the label for the next phase - Jacobin republicanism resting on sans-culotte Paris - and the change of social base, method and aim is visible in the words themselves. That is why the phase table repays close reading rather than memorisation.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Calling 1789-91 a failure is unhistorical. It abolished privilege, wrote a declaration of rights and established constitutional government - achievements that most European states did not reach for another century. Judging it by whether it lasted two years applies a standard no revolutionary settlement anywhere has met.

**Reply:** The lesson does not call it a failure; the sources record it as the phase in which the old regime was **legally broken**, and that is an unambiguous achievement which survived everything that followed, including Napoleon and the restoration. What is being explained is not failure but **instability** - why a settlement with real achievements could not become the terminus. The explanation is not a defect in its principles but the combination of an inherited fiscal problem it did not solve and two crises its own success generated. An answer should hold both: the settlement was the Revolution's most durable legal achievement and it was, as a political arrangement, unsustainable.

**Residual:** The unresolved question is whether the settlement could have stabilised in the absence of war. The three crises are given together and not ranked against one another, so no claim is made here that war was the decisive solvent. What can be said is that two of the three were generated by the settlement's own achievements and would have pressed on it regardless.

### UPSC application

This lesson owns the *phases* directive. When a question asks you to trace the phases, the mark is not for the dates but for explaining why each phase produced the next, and the phase table's recorded reason - fiscal, religious and war crises unresolved - is the hinge between phase one and phase two. The equality ladder in section 2 is the single most transferable device in this session: it works in a phases answer, a transformation answer, an enduring-relevance answer and any question that touches the nineteenth-century doctrines the overview topic owns. Use it, name its three stages, and attach the groups the source attaches to each.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that the Revolution "ended in 1791 and then restarted"; the sources record continuity of an unstable settlement, not an interruption.
- Attributing the collapse solely to the king's conduct, when the sources record three structural crises.
- Claiming universal suffrage in 1791; equal political rights for the non-propertied are dated by the sources to 1792.
- Collapsing legal, political and social equality into one idea, when a standard text distinguishes all three and attaches each to different actors.
- Using the class critique of bourgeois equality as if it disproved the Declaration, rather than as one recorded interpretation with a recorded counterweight.
- Claiming universal suffrage in 1791; the franchise was restricted to active citizens - men over 25 paying three days' wages in tax, about four million of twenty-eight million - and all women were passive citizens.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1789-91: bourgeois-led constitutionalism with peasant and crowd pressure; achievements - end of legal privilege, the Declaration, constitutional monarchy.
2. ✅ **Constitution of 1791**: separation of powers, a royal **veto**, an indirectly elected assembly; **active citizens** are men over 25 paying three days' wages in tax, about **4 million of 28 million**; the remaining men and **all women** are **passive citizens**. The **Legislative Assembly** sits **October 1791 - September 1792**. The old regime is legally broken but instability continues.
3. ✅ The phase gave way because **fiscal, religious and war crises** remained unresolved.
4. ✅ The three-stage equality ladder: equality before the law (the men of 1789) → political equality or universal suffrage (the Jacobins and later Republicans) → social equality (socialists).
5. ✅ Citizenship was gendered and property-conditioned by turns; it was contested, not conferred once.
6. ✅ Equal political rights for workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes are dated to **1792** in the French context.
7. ✅ Property is named among the rights in the Declaration's second proposition.
8. ✅ Engels, *Anti-Dühring* (1878): "This equality reduced itself to bourgeois equality before the law; ... bourgeois property was proclaimed as one of the essential rights of man".
9. ✅ The middle class invoked equality against the feudal system, then refused to extend it in substantive terms to the working class.
10. ⚠️ Two of the three crises were generated by the settlement's own achievements; only the fiscal one was inherited.
11. ⚠️ A constitutional monarchy depends on the good faith of the person with the clearest interest in its failure - a structural problem, stated without speculating about motives.
12. ⚠️ An order that cannot be restored and a settlement that cannot continue leave only one direction available.
13. ⚠️ The phase labels themselves carry the analysis: "bourgeois-led constitutionalism" against "Jacobin republicanism resting on sans-culotte Paris".

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the settlement of 1791 made its own supersession more likely, using the distinction between legal, political and social equality - and state precisely what 1792 added.

**Model answer:** The settlement completed the first stage of the equality sequence: the men of 1789 sought equality before the law, meaning the elimination of aristocratic legal privileges and feudal obligations, and by 1791 the sources record that the old regime was legally broken. But completing that stage made the next one askable and difficult to refuse. A society which has just abolished distinctions of birth cannot readily justify distinctions of property as the basis of political participation, since the justification it has just destroyed and the justification it now relies on are both distinctions not based on the individual's own claim to be heard; and the Declaration had proclaimed that social distinctions may be based only upon public utility, which is a test the property qualification had to pass rather than an exemption it could claim. The evidence that the settlement had not passed it is that equal political rights for workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes date in the French context to 1792, not to 1789 or 1791. What 1792 added was therefore the second stage - political equality, associated by a standard account with the Jacobins and the Republicans who succeeded them - together with the abolition of the monarchy that had been the institutional expression of the first settlement's compromise. The third stage, social equality, was not added at all; the sources record that workers and artisans still lacked economic equality, which is why the question passes to the nineteenth century rather than being answered by the Revolution.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the property qualification was simple hypocrisy. It was defended by an argument - that independence of judgement requires independence of circumstance - which was widely accepted at the time; the decisive objection is not that the argument was insincere but that its effect was to make political rights track the existing distribution of social power, and that the settlement's own founding text supplied the standard by which that effect could be condemned.

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** Of the three crises that destroyed this settlement, the religious one is the least discussed by candidates and arguably the most damaging, because it gave counter-revolution something no purely political grievance could give it - a popular constituency. The next lesson explains why.

## Lesson 7 - The religious rupture: why the Church question became the unhealable one

Progress: 7/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: clerical privilege and its abolition; the Church's ideological authority; why a settlement of the Church's position was unavoidable and unattainable; secular public authority and its coercive form

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's estate table on clerical privilege and its linkage of the Church to the old order, its evidence bank on secular public authority with the attached record that revolutionary anti-clericalism was itself coercive, and its phase table naming the religious crisis among the three that ended the constitutional monarchy; and a standard political-theory text on ecclesiastical authority and on the conservative defence of customary right
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: why this crisis has no exit

```text
       THE CHURCH UNDER THE OLD REGIME
                     |
      +--------------+--------------+
      |                             |
  A PRIVILEGED ORDER          AN IDEOLOGICAL AUTHORITY
  tax exemptions, property,   teaches obedience; sanctions
  office, corporate status    the existing order; its
      |                       privileges LINK the Church
      |                       to the old regime
      |                             |
      v                             v
 ABOLISHING PRIVILEGE          ABOLISHING PRIVILEGE HERE
 IS THE WHOLE POINT            ALSO TOUCHES AUTHORITY
 OF THE REVOLUTION             OVER BELIEF
      |                             |
      +--------------+--------------+
                     |
                     v
        THE REVOLUTION CANNOT LEAVE THE CHURCH ALONE
        (it is one of the two privileged orders)
                     |
                     v
        BUT IT CANNOT TOUCH THE CHURCH WITHOUT
        REACHING INTO EVERY VILLAGE IN FRANCE
                     |
      +--------------+--------------+
      |                             |
 FOR THE REVOLUTION:          FOR MANY PARISHIONERS:
 removing the ideological     an attack not on privilege
 sanction of the old order    but on their own religious life
      |                             |
      +--------------+--------------+
                     |
                     v
   COUNTER-REVOLUTION ACQUIRES A POPULAR CONSTITUENCY
   - which no purely political grievance could have supplied
```

*The diagram's shape is the argument: the Church occupies two positions at once, and an action necessary against one of them is experienced as an attack on the other. That is why this is the one crisis with no available compromise.*

### 1. The Church's double position

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records the First Estate as the clergy, which **enjoyed privilege and tax exemptions**, and states its historical role in one phrase: it **linked the Church to the old order.**

✅ **Fact:** The same account's evidence bank records the Revolution's action against it: the Revolution **attacked the ideological authority of the Church and the First Estate's privileges**, and the enduring principle this established is **secular public authority** - the argument that **public office and law need no religious sanction.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read those two facts together and the structural trap is complete. The clergy was not a religious body that happened to hold privileges; in the sources' formulation its privileges were what *linked* it to the old order, which means the privileges were part of the political structure rather than incidental to it. Abolishing privilege therefore had to include clerical privilege, or the abolition was not an abolition at all. There was no version of the Revolution's central act that left the Church untouched.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text supplies the long background in a single line: during medieval Europe, **political authority was eclipsed by ecclesiastical authority**, and temporal citizenship was no longer considered important.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that line explains why the revolutionary claim about citizenship and the revolutionary claim about the Church are the same claim viewed from two sides. To make temporal citizenship the primary political identity is necessarily to demote the ecclesiastical one. The Revolution did not attack the Church as an afterthought to its political programme; the political programme required the demotion.

### 2. Why no settlement was available

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the part to think through carefully, because it explains the intensity that the bare facts do not.

A revolution that abolishes the legal privileges of a corporate body must then decide what that body *is* in the new order. Three options exist in principle, and each fails.

| Option | Why it fails |
|---|---|
| Leave the Church's corporate position intact | Contradicts the abolition of privilege; the First Estate survives as an exception, and the exception destroys the principle |
| Dissolve the Church's role in public life entirely | Reaches directly into the observances of the rural population, who did not experience their parish as a privileged order |
| Convert the clergy into officers of the new state | Requires the clergy to accept the state as the source of their standing, which for many is precisely the claim their office denies |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third option is the one a rational reformer would choose, and it contains the fatal difficulty. The moment the state defines the clergy's position, every cleric faces a question of allegiance that has no compromise answer, and every parish acquires a reason to take a side. A political quarrel can be resolved by concession because political demands are divisible. A quarrel about ultimate authority is not divisible, which is why it produced the deepest and most durable division of the decade.

✅ **Fact:** The instrument that attempted the third option has a name and a date. The **Civil Constitution of the Clergy**, enacted on **12 July 1790**, reorganised the Catholic Church in France and placed it under the authority of the state; in **November 1790** the Assembly required all clergy to swear an **oath** supporting it. The requirement split the clergy into those who swore - the **jurors**, or constitutional clergy - and those who refused, the **non-jurors** or refractory clergy, and the split ran through the parishes with them.

✅ **Fact:** The material ground had already been laid the previous August, when the confiscation of Church lands and the abolition of tithes removed the clergy's independent revenue and made some state provision for them unavoidable.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence is what explains the intensity. Confiscating the Church's lands created a problem the state then had to solve by defining the clergy's position; defining their position required an oath; and an oath is the one instrument that cannot be complied with partially. Every priest in France was compelled to choose publicly, and every congregation inherited the choice. That is how a fiscal measure of August 1789 became, within sixteen months, the deepest and most durable division of the decade.

### 3. What the attack on the Church established, and at what cost

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records the principle that resulted as one of the Revolution's six enduring contributions: **secular public authority**, whose content is that **public office and law need no religious sanction**, and whose mechanism of transmission is the argument itself rather than any French institution.

✅ **Fact:** The same entry carries its own built-in qualification, and it is unusually candid: **revolutionary anti-clericalism was itself coercive; secularism has plural later forms.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that qualification is worth a full sentence in any answer on the Revolution's legacy, and it is worth understanding rather than quoting. A state that removes religious sanction from public authority can do so in at least two ways. It can withdraw the state from religion, leaving belief to individuals and associations. Or it can substitute the state's own authority over the domain the Church previously occupied, in which case the result is not neutrality but a transfer of jurisdiction. The revolutionary form tended toward the second, which is what "itself coercive" records; and the observation that **secularism has plural later forms** is the source's way of saying that the French solution is one model among several and should not be treated as the definition of the concept.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** for an Indian-context answer this is the most useful single point in the lesson, and it must be made carefully. The correct claim is comparative and conceptual: that the eighteenth-century French settlement established the argument that public law needs no religious sanction, and that later constitutional orders, including India's, worked out different relationships between state and religion within that broad inheritance. The incorrect claim - and it is a common one - is that Indian secularism derives from the French model. The source's own formulation, that secularism has plural later forms, is the authority for keeping the claim comparative rather than genealogical.

### 4. Why this crisis, more than any other, armed the counter-revolution

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the decisive consequence is sociological rather than doctrinal, and stating it is what turns this lesson into an explanation of the 1792-94 phase rather than a digression.

Every other revolutionary measure divided the population along lines that favoured the Revolution. Abolishing seigneurial dues set the many against the few. Abolishing noble privilege set the many against the few. Attacking the Church's position did something different: it divided the population along a line that ran **through the villages** rather than between the villages and their lords. A peasant who had gained from the annulment of feudal laws might simultaneously experience the reorganisation of his parish as an injury. For the first time, the Revolution had produced opposition among the people whose material interests it had served.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and that is precisely what a counter-revolution needs and had previously lacked. Émigré nobles and hostile foreign courts could supply hostility; they could not supply a popular base. The religious question supplied one. From that point the Revolution faced not only enemies outside and above it but a constituency within the population it claimed to represent - which is the condition that makes an emergency government plausible, and that Lesson 9 will have to weigh when it assesses the Terror.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table records the religious crisis as one of the three that the constitutional monarchy left unresolved, alongside the fiscal and the war crises.

### 5. The conservative critique, in the form a standard text records it

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text records Edmund Burke (1729-97) as the greatest champion of the historical theory of rights, and records that he **criticised the French Revolution (1789) because it was provoked by a conception of abstract rights of man - liberty, equality, fraternity** - while glorifying the English Revolution of 1688, which sought to reassert customary rights that Englishmen had long enjoyed and which had found expression in documents such as **Magna Carta (1215)** and the **Petition of Right (1628)**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Burke's criticism is usually cited in the abstract, and it becomes far sharper when attached to this lesson. His objection is not that liberty is undesirable but that **abstract** rights, deduced from a conception of man rather than inherited from a particular society's practice, licence the demolition of institutions whose functions the demolishers do not understand. The Church question is the strongest case he has. An institution that had for centuries performed registration, charity, education and the moral organisation of village life was reorganised by reference to a principle - that public authority needs no religious sanction - which said nothing about who would perform those functions afterwards. The revolutionaries' reply is equally strong: an institution whose privileges linked it to a structure of legal inequality could not be left in place by a revolution against legal inequality, whatever its useful functions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that exchange is a genuine argument with two good sides, which makes it ideal material for an answer that needs to demonstrate evaluation. Lesson 17 returns to it as the origin of the conservative interpretation of the whole event.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson makes the religious question central on thin evidence. The sources mention it in a five-word phrase in a table. Building a whole lesson on "religious crises unresolved" inflates a passing reference into a pillar.

**Reply:** The phrase is short and the supporting material is not. The sources separately record that clerical privilege *linked the Church to the old order*, which makes the Church structural rather than incidental; that the Revolution *attacked the ideological authority of the Church* as well as its privileges, which makes the action doctrinal rather than merely fiscal; that this established secular public authority as one of the Revolution's enduring contributions, which makes it consequential; and that revolutionary anti-clericalism *was itself coercive*, which makes it costly. A crisis that is structural, doctrinal, consequential and costly is not a passing reference. What this lesson does not do - and the control says so explicitly - is supply the legislative particulars that the sources omit.

**Residual:** The genuine gap is scale. The instrument, the oath and the resulting juror and non-juror division are firm; how much of France divided, how the split mapped onto region or class, and how long it persisted are questions this level of study does not settle. The claim made here - that the religious question gave counter-revolution a popular constituency - is an inference from the structure of the situation, marked as such, and it is the strongest available reading rather than a reported measurement.

### UPSC application

This lesson is worth one sentence in a phases answer, one sentence in a causes-of-radicalisation answer, and a full paragraph in any question on secularism, on the Revolution's legacy, or on why revolutions generate counter-revolutions. The highest-value move is the qualification: state that the Revolution established the argument that public law needs no religious sanction, and in the same breath that revolutionary anti-clericalism was itself coercive and that secularism has plural later forms. That combination demonstrates exactly the balance an examiner is looking for, and it protects an Indian-context answer from the genealogical over-claim.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing about "a measure against the Church" when the Civil Constitution of the Clergy (12 July 1790) and the oath of November 1790 can be named precisely, with the juror and non-juror split they produced.
- Presenting the attack on the Church as anti-religious zeal rather than as a necessary consequence of abolishing privilege.
- Claiming that Indian secularism derives from the French model, when the sources record that secularism has plural later forms.
- Omitting the coercive character of revolutionary anti-clericalism and so writing a one-sided account of the Revolution's secular legacy.
- Treating Burke as a mere reactionary rather than as the author of a serious functional objection to abstract rights.
- Missing the sociological consequence - that this was the first revolutionary measure to divide the villages internally.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The First Estate enjoyed privilege and tax exemptions, and those privileges **linked the Church to the old order**.
2. ✅ The Revolution attacked the ideological authority of the Church and the First Estate's privileges.
3. ✅ The principle established: **secular public authority** - public office and law need no religious sanction.
4. ✅ The qualification recorded with it: revolutionary anti-clericalism **was itself coercive**; secularism has plural later forms.
5. ✅ The religious crisis is one of the three - with fiscal and war - that the constitutional monarchy left unresolved.
6. ✅ In medieval Europe political authority was eclipsed by ecclesiastical authority; temporal citizenship was not considered important.
7. ✅ Burke (1729-97) criticised the Revolution for being provoked by abstract rights of man, and glorified 1688 for reasserting customary rights expressed in Magna Carta (1215) and the Petition of Right (1628).
8. ⚠️ Three possible settlements of the Church's position - leave it, dissolve it, absorb it - and each fails for a different reason.
9. ⚠️ Political demands are divisible and negotiable; claims about ultimate authority are not, which is why this crisis had no compromise.
10. ⚠️ This was the first revolutionary measure to divide the villages internally rather than setting village against lord.
11. ⚠️ Counter-revolution thereby acquired a popular constituency, which émigrés and foreign courts could not supply.
12. ⚠️ For Indian-context answers: claim a shared argument about public authority, never a genealogy of Indian secularism from the French model.
13. ⚠️ Burke's objection is functional - abstract rights licence demolition of institutions whose social functions are not understood; the revolutionary reply is that privilege-linked institutions cannot survive a revolution against privilege.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Church question, unlike the abolition of noble privilege or of seigneurial dues, produced opposition among the very people the Revolution had materially benefited - and say what that fact contributed to the events of 1792-94.

**Model answer:** The abolition of noble privilege and of seigneurial dues divided French society between a large beneficiary population and a small losing one, so opposition to them was confined to those who had lost, and their numbers made them politically manageable. The Church occupied a different position, because the sources record its privileges as what linked it to the old order while the same institution was also the site of ordinary religious life in every parish; an action necessary against the first aspect was therefore experienced by many as an attack on the second. The result was a division that ran through the villages rather than between villages and lords, so that a peasant who had gained decisively from the annulment of feudal laws could simultaneously regard the reorganisation of his parish as an injury. What this contributed to 1792-94 is a popular constituency for counter-revolution, which hostile foreign courts and emigrating nobles could never have supplied by themselves. An external enemy justifies mobilisation; an internal enemy embedded within the population justifies surveillance, suspicion and emergency powers, because the government can no longer identify its opponents by their rank. That is a necessary part of the explanation of the emergency government of the following phase, and it should appear in any account of the Terror that seeks to explain rather than merely to condemn it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the Revolution's religious policy as simple hostility to belief. The sources record an attack on the Church's privileges and on its ideological authority - that is, on its position in a structure of legal inequality and on its power to sanction that structure - and they record that the enduring principle established was that public office and law need no religious sanction, together with the frank qualification that the revolutionary form of this was itself coercive.

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** Two of the three unresolved crises are now on the table. The third, war, converts everything this lesson has described from a domestic division into a question of national survival - and it is under that pressure that the constitutional monarchy is abolished. The next lesson follows it.

## Lesson 8 - War, fear and the abolition of the monarchy, 1792

Progress: 8/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: how external war and counter-revolutionary fear combine; the republic of 1792; the widening of political rights; the regicide of 1793; the mechanism of radicalisation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's timeline entries for 1792 and its chapter-spine section on the abolition of the monarchy, its causal chain linking war and counter-revolutionary fear to radicalisation, its phase table for 1792-94 and its date control separating republic from regicide; and the historiographical companion on the Jacobins' aims and the social base of the republican phase
