CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the radicalisation engine

```text
  THE SOURCES' OWN CHAIN
  ----------------------
  fiscal crisis + unequal estates + bread stress
                      +
            Enlightenment attack on privilege
                      ->
            1789 political rupture
                      ->
    war + counter-revolutionary fear + radicalisation
                      ->
    Jacobin republic  ->  Directory  ->  Napoleon


  THE MECHANISM INSIDE THE THIRD LINE
  -----------------------------------
        EXTERNAL WAR                INTERNAL OPPOSITION
        a hostile Europe            a constituency created
        of monarchies               by the religious and
              |                     political divisions
              |                            |
              +-------------+--------------+
                            |
                            v
              EVERY INTERNAL OPPONENT CAN NOW BE
              REPRESENTED AS AN EXTERNAL AGENT
                            |
        +-------------------+-------------------+
        |                                       |
  DISSENT BECOMES                    EMERGENCY POWERS BECOME
  INDISTINGUISHABLE                  DEFENSIBLE IN PRINCIPLE
  FROM TREASON                       (the nation is in danger)
        |                                       |
        +-------------------+-------------------+
                            |
                            v
        THE MODERATE POSITION BECOMES UNTENABLE:
        to defend a limited constitution is to be
        suspected of protecting the enemy
                            |
                            v
        POWER MOVES TO WHOEVER WILL ACT MOST DECISIVELY
                            |
                            v
        MONARCHY ABOLISHED; REPUBLIC DECLARED, 1792
```

*The second diagram is the reusable part. It is a general mechanism, not a French peculiarity, and stating it as a mechanism is what converts a narrative answer into an analytical one.*

### 1. The sources' causal chain, read closely

✅ **Fact:** The standard account gives the topic's causal chain in a single compact block: fiscal crisis, unequal estates and bread stress, plus the Enlightenment attack on privilege, produce the 1789 political rupture; that in turn produces **war plus counter-revolutionary fear plus radicalisation**; and that produces the Jacobin republic, then the Directory, then Napoleon.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note the composition of the third term. It is not "war caused radicalisation". It is a conjunction of three elements, one external, one psychological and one political, and the middle term is the connective. War by itself can unify a country behind an existing government. What radicalises is war **plus the belief that the enemy has allies inside**, because that belief dissolves the distinction between opposition and treason - and once that distinction has gone, no moderate position is safe to hold.

✅ **Fact:** The chapter spine the standard account follows has a section on France abolishing the monarchy and becoming a republic, whose core point is **radicalisation and war**, and whose significance is recorded as the **shift from reform to revolution**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "shift from reform to revolution" deserves attention because it inverts the usual chronology in candidates' minds. It means that 1789-91, for all its drama, is being classified by the source as *reform* - the abolition of privilege within a monarchical constitutional frame - and that the genuinely revolutionary rupture, the creation of a new form of state, is dated to 1792. That classification is defensible and it is worth adopting: it explains why the sources treat 1792 rather than 1789 as the moment when the non-propertied enter the political nation.

### 2. What 1792 did

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's timeline records **1792: monarchy abolished; republic declared**, with the significance that the **Revolution turns more radical.**

✅ **Fact:** Its date-control list keeps two things apart that candidates routinely merge: the **republic (1792)** and the **regicide (1793)**. They are separate markers in separate years.

✅ **Fact:** The phase table records what the 1792-94 phase achieved: the **republic**, **wider male political rights** and **national defence**.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records the same widening from the older survey's standpoint: **1792** is the moment when workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes first gained equal political rights in the French context.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three achievements, and they are not a list but a package with an internal logic. National defence required mass participation; mass participation was difficult to demand from men who were not full citizens; extending political rights was therefore not only an ideological act but a condition of mobilisation. That connection between **military necessity and the widening of citizenship** is one of the most transferable ideas in world history, and it is worth naming here because it recurs in almost every later case the syllabus covers.

### 3. Who the Jacobins were

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account names the club and its membership. The most successful of the revolutionary political clubs was that of the **Jacobins**, which took its name from the former **convent of St Jacob** in Paris where it met. Its members belonged mainly to the less prosperous sections of society - small shopkeepers, artisans such as shoemakers, pastry cooks, watch-makers and printers, servants and daily-wage workers - and their leader was **Maximilian Robespierre**. A large group among them adopted long striped trousers in place of the knee breeches worn by fashionable society, and so came to be known as the **sans-culottes**, literally "those without knee breeches"; the men also wore the **red cap** that symbolised liberty, which women were not permitted to wear.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory glossary adds the club's trajectory in one line: **founded in 1789**, it became increasingly radical, **instituted the Terror**, and was **closed in 1794**.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records their aims and their record: they wanted the **republic, popular mobilisation and emergency defence**, and they **saved the Revolution militarily but are associated with the Terror.**

✅ **Fact:** The same companion records the doctrinal position from the older survey: **the Jacobins believed in direct democracy and, fearing danger to the Revolution, adopted harsh measures.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence is worth noticing: founded in 1789, increasingly radical, instituted the Terror, closed in 1794. The Jacobins did not begin as the authors of an emergency dictatorship; they began as a club within the constitutional phase and arrived at the Terror through five years of escalating conflict. An answer that presents them as extremists from the outset has removed the explanatory problem rather than solved it - and the explanatory problem is exactly what a Terror question is asking about.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note also the social composition. A club of shoemakers, printers and wage-earners led by a provincial lawyer is not the bourgeois leadership of 1789; the change of personnel between the phases is as real as the change of programme, and it is the concrete form of the phase table's shift from bourgeois-led constitutionalism to Jacobin republicanism resting on sans-culotte Paris.

### 4. Why the republic, specifically

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is worth asking why the crisis of 1792 produced the abolition of the monarchy rather than, say, a stronger ministry or a regency. Three reasons follow from what the previous lessons have established.

| Reason | Basis |
|---|---|
| The constitutional monarchy had already been shown to depend on the good faith of the person with the least interest in its survival | Lesson 6's structural problem |
| War with monarchies abroad made the domestic monarch's position symbolically impossible | The war crisis recorded in the phase table |
| The crowd on which the governing faction now rested had no attachment to the monarchical compromise of 1791 | The phase table's record that 1792-94 rested on sans-culotte Paris |

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account supplies the sequence that turned those pressures into an outcome. Louis XVI, having signed the Constitution, entered into secret negotiations with the King of **Prussia**, and neighbouring rulers planned to send troops; before they could, the Assembly voted in **April 1792** to declare war on **Prussia and Austria**. Volunteers thronged from the provinces, singing among other patriotic songs the **Marseillaise**, composed by **Roget de L'Isle** and first sung by volunteers from Marseilles as they marched into Paris - now the national anthem of France. In the summer of 1792 the Jacobins planned an insurrection of Parisians angered by short supplies and high prices; on the morning of **10 August** they stormed the **Palace of the Tuileries**, massacred the king's guards and held the king hostage for several hours, after which the Assembly voted to imprison the royal family. Elections followed, and from then on **all men of 21 and above, regardless of wealth, got the right to vote**. The newly elected assembly was called the **Convention**, and on **21 September 1792** it abolished the monarchy and declared France a republic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read that sequence as three conversions and it becomes an argument rather than a narrative. The war converts a domestic constitutional quarrel into a question of national survival. The insurrection of 10 August converts the crowd from a pressure on the Assembly into the agency that removes the executive. And the abolition of the property qualification converts the political nation itself - which is why the sources date equal political rights for the non-propertied to 1792 and why the Convention, unlike its predecessor, could claim to speak for the whole male population.

### 5. The regicide and what it foreclosed

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's date control records **regicide (1793)** as a distinct marker between the republic of 1792 and the Terror of 1793-94, and the standard school account supplies the particulars: **Louis XVI** was sentenced to death by a court on the charge of **treason** and executed publicly on **21 January 1793** at the **Place de la Concorde**; **Marie Antoinette** met the same fate shortly afterwards.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical significance of the regicide is not moral but strategic, and stating it that way is what earns marks. The execution of a monarch by judicial process does three things at once. It makes the republic irreversible for those who carried it out, because no restored monarchy could forgive it. It removes any possibility of a negotiated settlement with the monarchies at war with France, since the act is precisely what they are fighting to prevent. And it binds the revolutionary leadership together by a shared liability, which is a powerful unifier and an equally powerful driver of escalation, because every subsequent defeat is now personally fatal to the men making decisions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the charge matters as much as the outcome. Trying a king for **treason** asserts that the monarch is a citizen subject to the nation's law rather than the source of it - so the trial enacts the sovereignty claim of 17 June 1789 in its most literal possible form. That is why the regicide belongs in an answer on popular sovereignty and not only in a narrative of 1793.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third strategic point is the bridge to Lesson 9. A government whose members cannot survive their own defeat behaves differently from one whose members can retire. It is one of the several genuine causes of the Terror, and it is a structural cause rather than a psychological one.

### 6. The mechanism, stated for reuse

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the general form, which can be applied to almost any revolution in the syllabus:

```text
  A revolution creates enemies it cannot reconcile
            +
  A war gives those enemies an external sponsor (real or imagined)
            =
  1. Opposition becomes indistinguishable from treason
  2. Emergency powers become arguable on defensive grounds
  3. Moderation becomes personally dangerous to hold
  4. Authority flows to whoever will act most decisively
  5. The emergency's institutions outlive the emergency
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** step five is the one that generates the Terror question, and it is also the one that generates the standard defence and the standard indictment. Lesson 9 takes all five apart.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This account is exculpatory. It presents radicalisation as the automatic output of a mechanism, which means nobody chose anything and nobody is responsible. Revolutions are made by people who decide to do things, and a model that removes decision removes judgement.

**Reply:** The mechanism explains the *pressure*, not the *choice*, and the distinction matters. What the model establishes is that after 1792 the range of survivable positions narrowed sharply and that moderation carried a personal cost it had not carried before. It does not establish that any particular measure was inevitable, and the sources do not say so either: the older survey's formulation is that the Jacobins, **fearing danger to the Revolution, adopted harsh measures**, which attributes a decision to a motive under a condition. That is precisely the right structure for a historical judgement - condition, motive, decision - and it preserves both explanation and responsibility. Lesson 9 accordingly refuses both "necessary" and "insane" as complete verdicts, which is the rule the sources themselves lay down.

**Residual:** The sources do not allow any assessment of how far the internal threat was real and how far it was constructed by those who benefited from believing in it. That is one of the genuinely open questions of the period, and this session marks it as open rather than adopting either view.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the hinge of a phases answer and the indispensable preliminary to any Terror question. The single most valuable move is to state the three-part composition of the radicalising force - war, counter-revolutionary fear, and the dissolution of the line between dissent and treason - rather than writing that "the Revolution became more extreme". A second high-value move is the military-participation point: national defence required mass mobilisation, mass mobilisation was hard to demand from half-citizens, and the widening of political rights in 1792 therefore had a practical as well as an ideological logic. For questions about the modern nation, that connection is the beginning of the answer.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Merging the republic (1792) and the regicide (1793) into one event or one year.
- Writing that "war caused the Terror" without the intermediate step of counter-revolutionary fear dissolving the line between opposition and treason.
- Treating the Jacobins as extremists from the outset, when the sources record a club founded in 1789 that became increasingly radical.
- Leaving the 1792 sequence vague when it can be dated precisely: war declared April 1792, Tuileries stormed 10 August, Convention elected on a universal male franchise, monarchy abolished 21 September 1792, Louis XVI executed 21 January 1793.
- Missing the source's own classification of 1789-91 as reform and 1792 as the shift to revolution.
- Presenting the widening of political rights in 1792 as purely ideological, without the mobilisation logic.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The sources' causal chain: 1789 rupture → **war + counter-revolutionary fear + radicalisation** → Jacobin republic → Directory → Napoleon.
2. ✅ **1792**: monarchy abolished; republic declared; the Revolution turns more radical.
3. ✅ The date control separates **republic (1792)** from **regicide (1793)**.
4. ✅ The chapter spine describes this as the **shift from reform to revolution**.
5. ✅ Phase achievements, 1792-94: republic, wider male political rights, national defence.
6. ✅ 1792 is when workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes first gained equal political rights in the French context.
7. ✅ Jacobins: a political club **founded in 1789**, named from the former **convent of St Jacob** in Paris; members were shopkeepers, artisans, servants and wage-earners; leader **Maximilian Robespierre**; the striped-trouser group became the **sans-culottes**. Increasingly radical; instituted the Terror; **closed in 1794**.
8. ✅ The 1792 sequence: war on **Prussia and Austria**, **April 1792**; the **Marseillaise** of **Roget de L'Isle**; the **Tuileries** stormed **10 August**; universal male suffrage at **21**; the **Convention** abolishes the monarchy **21 September 1792**; **Louis XVI** executed for **treason** **21 January 1793** at the **Place de la Concorde**.
9. ✅ Jacobin aims: republic, popular mobilisation, emergency defence; record: saved the Revolution militarily, associated with the Terror.
10. ✅ They believed in direct democracy and, **fearing danger to the Revolution, adopted harsh measures**.
11. ⚠️ The radicalisation mechanism in five steps: treason-conflation, defensible emergency powers, dangerous moderation, authority to the decisive, institutions outliving the emergency.
12. ⚠️ Military necessity and the widening of citizenship are connected: mass defence is hard to demand from half-citizens.
13. ⚠️ The regicide made the republic irreversible, foreclosed negotiation, and bound the leadership by shared liability.
14. ⚠️ A government whose members cannot survive defeat behaves differently from one whose members can retire - a structural cause of escalation.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the precise role of war in the radicalisation of the Revolution, distinguishing what war contributed from what it did not - and identify the step at which moderate constitutional politics became personally unsafe.

**Model answer:** War contributed three things and did not by itself contribute radicalism. It supplied an external threat that made the survival of the state and the survival of the Revolution the same question; it supplied a sponsor, real or imagined, for every internal opponent, since anyone resisting the new order could be represented as serving the enemy; and it supplied the practical necessity of mass mobilisation, which made the extension of political rights to the non-propertied a condition of defence as well as a principle, which is why the sources date equal political rights for workers and peasants in the French context to 1792. What war did not supply is the content of radical policy: nothing about a foreign invasion prescribes a republic, a maximum on prices or an emergency tribunal. The transition to those came from the combination the sources record - war plus counter-revolutionary fear plus radicalisation - in which the middle term does the connecting work, because the belief that the enemy has allies within dissolves the distinction between opposition and treason. The step at which moderate politics became unsafe is exactly there: once that distinction has gone, defending a limited constitution, negotiating with opponents or urging restraint is no longer one policy among several but evidence of complicity, so the moderate position carries a personal risk that the intransigent position does not, and authority flows to those willing to act most decisively.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the abolition of the monarchy in 1792 and the execution of the king in 1793 as a single event. They are separate markers in separate years, and their analytical functions differ: the first creates a republic, the second makes it irreversible and forecloses any negotiated peace with the monarchies at war with France.

**Bridge to Lesson 9:** We now have a republic at war, governed by men who cannot survive their own defeat, resting on a capital that wants bread and equality, and facing opponents who are indistinguishable from traitors. The next lesson examines what that government did, what it built, and how a candidate should judge it.

### Visual: one government, three things at once

```text
     |                      |                      |

 insurrection          enemies                       |

 provide work or            |                  executed in some
 livelihood                 |                  14 months
     |                      |                      |
     +----------------------+----------------------+
                            |
                            v
            THESE ARE NOT THREE SEPARATE REGIMES.
            THEY ARE ONE UNSTABLE PACKAGE, AND ANY
            ACCOUNT THAT DROPS ONE OF THEM IS WRONG.
                            |
     +----------------------+----------------------+
     |                                             |
 THE ROMANTIC ERROR                      THE DISMISSIVE ERROR
 keep column 1, drop column 3            keep column 3, drop column 1
 -> "the most democratic                 -> "a bloodbath with no
    moment of the Revolution"               political content"
```

*The visual is built around the sources' own formulation, which is that the Terror joined mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence in one unstable package. The two errors at the bottom are the two ways of failing this question, and they are symmetrical.*

### Part A - What the Jacobin republic built

#### A1. The constitution that is usually forgotten

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records, from the older survey, that under the **Jacobin constitution** three things were highlighted: **universal male political rights**; the **right of insurrection**; and the principle that **government must provide work or livelihood.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** stop and consider how unusual each of those is for 1793. Universal male political rights, without a property qualification, place the phase far ahead of most nineteenth-century European constitutions. A right of insurrection written into a constitution is a genuine conceptual oddity - a legal instrument authorising resistance to the authority it constitutes - and it makes explicit what the Declaration had implied by naming resistance to oppression among the rights. And a duty on government to provide work or livelihood is a **social right**, of a kind that does not become common in constitutions until the twentieth century.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most under-used fact in the topic, and it does three jobs in an answer. It proves that the radical phase had a positive constitutional programme and was not merely an emergency. It supplies the exact point at which the Revolution touches the socialist tradition without being socialist, since a duty to provide livelihood is a claim about social entitlement rather than about ownership. And it converts the Terror question from a moral one into a genuinely difficult political one: the same movement produced the period's most advanced statement of political and social rights and its most extensive coercion, and both facts have to be held together.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it also completes Lesson 6's equality ladder with unusual precision. The men of 1789 achieved equality before the law; the Jacobins pursued political equality - and, in the work-or-livelihood clause, gestured at the third stage that a standard account attributes to socialists. The gesture was not delivered. But the ladder's second and third rungs both appear in this phase, which is why the sources place the Jacobins at the hinge of the sequence.

✅ **Fact:** The document has a name and a date. The **Constitution of 1793**, also called the **Constitution of Year I**, was adopted by the Convention on **24 June 1793** and approved by plebiscite in July - and was then **suspended and never fully implemented**, the war and the emergency government taking its place.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that fact is worth more than any commentary on it. The most democratic constitution the Revolution produced was ratified and shelved in the same summer, and the government that shelved it was the government that had written it. An answer that states this has explained the relationship between the Jacobin programme and the Jacobin emergency in a single sentence: the emergency did not replace a constitution that had failed, it replaced one that had never been allowed to operate.

#### A2. The institutions of revolutionary government

✅ **Fact:** The emergency machinery likewise has names and dates, and an answer should use them. The **Revolutionary Tribunal** was created in **March 1793** to try political offenders. The **Committee of Public Safety** was established on **6 April 1793** as the executive charged with protecting the republic from internal and external threats. The **levée en masse** of **23 August 1793** decreed mass conscription for national defence. The **Law of Suspects** of **17 September 1793** permitted the arrest of virtually anyone suspected of counter-revolutionary activity. From **March 1793** a royalist and Catholic insurgency in the **Vendée**, in western France, opened a civil war against conscription and revolutionary religious policy.

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account describes how the government used that machinery. Those Robespierre regarded as enemies of the republic - ex-nobles and clergy, members of other political parties, and even members of his own party who disagreed with his methods - were arrested, imprisoned and tried by the revolutionary tribunal, and if found guilty were **guillotined**, on a device named after **Dr Guillotin** who invented it. His government issued laws placing a **maximum ceiling on wages and prices**; meat and bread were rationed; peasants were forced to bring grain to the cities and sell at fixed prices; white flour was forbidden and all citizens required to eat the **pain d'égalité**, a wholewheat "equality bread"; the traditional Monsieur and Madame were replaced by **Citoyen** and **Citoyenne**; and churches were shut and converted into barracks or offices.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** set those two paragraphs side by side and the Terror stops being a mood and becomes a **system**, which is what an examiner wants. There is a court, an executive committee, a conscription decree, a detention statute, a price and wage regime, a rationing scheme, a civil war and a programme of enforced civic language - each with a date, each answering a specific problem of a republic at war with enemies inside and outside its borders. Describing it as a system also sharpens the criticism rather than softening it, because a system can be assessed for proportionality in a way that a frenzy cannot.

✅ **Fact:** The Convention itself was divided between the **Girondins**, the moderate republicans, and the **Montagnards**, the radical Jacobins who came to dominate it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that division is the missing term in most accounts of the Terror. The coercion was not simply a republic acting against royalists; it was one republican faction consolidating against another, which is why the machinery consumed revolutionaries as readily as counter-revolutionaries and why the factional cause in the three-cause rule below is not an afterthought.

#### A3. Direct democracy as a doctrine, not a slogan

✅ **Fact:** The older survey, as the companion records it, states that the Jacobins **believed in direct democracy** and, **fearing danger to the Revolution, adopted harsh measures.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two halves of that sentence are causally linked, and seeing the link is what distinguishes an explanation from a juxtaposition. A doctrine of direct democracy holds that the people's will is immediately present in the assembled people, rather than being filtered and delayed through representation and procedure. That doctrine has an enormous strength - it makes the government answerable to the governed continuously rather than periodically - and a specific vulnerability. If the popular will is immediate, then procedures that delay it are obstacles rather than protections; and an opponent who invokes procedure against the popular will is not exercising a right but obstructing the sovereign. Under conditions of war and internal threat, that inference is available and was drawn.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the responsible way to connect the doctrinal tradition to the coercion without either whitewash or slander. It is not a claim that Rousseau's philosophy caused the Terror. The point is, as Lesson 3 established, that the tradition the revolutionaries drew on gave prominence to the common good over private interests, and it records here that the governing faction held a doctrine of direct democracy. Together those make the *justification* of emergency coercion available from within the revolutionary tradition itself, rather than requiring a betrayal of it. That is a stronger and more troubling point than any accusation, and it is one an examiner will reward.

#### A4. What the phase achieved

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table records the achievements of 1792-94 as the **republic**, **wider male political rights** and **national defence**, and records the reason the phase gave way in a single decisive line: **emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that line is the best short verdict on the Terror available in the sources and it should be used as a conclusion rather than paraphrased away. It concedes that there was an emergency, concedes that the emergency justified something, and locates the failure precisely in the persistence of the instrument beyond the conditions that licensed it. It is a judgement with a date attached rather than a moral adjective.

### Part B - The Terror as a problem in historical judgement

#### B1. The recorded figure, and exactly what it counts

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records, from the older survey, that **in 14 months around 17,000 people were tried and executed.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's factual-risk control instructs that no casualty figure for the Terror should be given in a core answer **unless** it is the sourced figure of about **17,000 tried and executed in some 14 months**, and that no totals for the Revolution as a whole should be invented.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two owners of this material type the same figure differently - one as a plain sourced fact, the other as a reported figure to be attributed - and the safer practice is to attribute it rather than assert it as a settled total.

**Scope note - what the number counts.** ⚠️ Standard reference accounts put official death sentences during the Terror at about the same order as the figure above, roughly sixteen to seventeen thousand, and they are explicit that this counts **judicial executions only**. Deaths in prison and killings without formal trial are additional; and the mortality of the civil war in the western provinces is, on the estimates such accounts give, very much larger again and is not included in the judicial figure at all. The consequence for an answer is exact and should be stated in the answer itself: **about 17,000 is a figure for people tried and executed, not a figure for the human cost of the Terror, still less for the Revolution.** A candidate who writes "17,000 died in the Terror" has misreported the standard number.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is also a general lesson in source discipline and worth internalising. The question a number answers is part of the number. Detach the figure from its scope - tried and executed, in fourteen months, by judicial process - and it becomes an assertion the evidence does not support.

#### B2. The three-cause rule

✅ **Fact:** The standard account lays down an explicit **Terror handling rule**: explain it as **war emergency plus ideological logic plus factional struggle - three causes, not one**; refuse both "necessary" and "insane" as complete verdicts; and state the cost explicitly.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three causes do different work and should be written as such.

| Cause | What it explains | What it cannot explain |
|---|---|---|
| **War emergency** | Why extraordinary powers were claimed and why they were widely accepted | Why the coercion continued as the military position improved, and why it was directed inward as much as outward |
| **Ideological logic** | Why coercion could be justified from within revolutionary doctrine rather than despite it - the common good above private interest, the immediate popular will above procedure | Why particular individuals and groups were targeted at particular moments |
| **Factional struggle** | Why the coercion consumed revolutionaries as well as counter-revolutionaries, and why its intensity tracked political conflict rather than military danger | Why an emergency apparatus existed in the first place for factions to capture |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table shows why one cause is never enough. Each explains what the others cannot, and each fails exactly where another succeeds. An answer that presents all three, and shows the gap each fills, has demonstrated the analytical skill the directive is testing.

#### B3. The formulation to write

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion's own conclusion is that the Terror **cannot be dismissed as mere madness, nor can it be romanticised**, and that it joined **mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence in one unstable package.**

✅ **Fact:** The same companion's debate matrix presents the two positions squarely - a **necessary defence in wartime emergency** against **coercive excess and political narrowing** - and states the best synthesis as explaining **both context and cost.**

✅ **Fact:** It also records the corresponding trap: the claim that the **Terror proves the Revolution failed** is answered by the requirement to show **both survival-context and coercive cost.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** combine those with the phase table's verdict and you have a complete, defensible and quotable position:

> The Terror was a genuine emergency government that saved the Revolution militarily, that rested on the most advanced statement of political and social rights the period produced, and that used coercion which outlived the emergency's justification and consumed the revolutionaries themselves. It is explained by war, by a doctrine that ranked the common good above procedure, and by factional struggle - and its cost, about seventeen thousand people tried and executed in fourteen months, is a judicial figure and not a measure of the total violence of the period.

#### B4. Why the Terror is the Revolution's most consequential legacy problem

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one point that the exclusions lesson will reinforce and that belongs here first. The Terror is the event that permanently complicates the Revolution's claim to have founded modern liberty, and it does so in a specific way: the coercion was carried out **in the name of popular sovereignty and by appeal to the rights the Revolution had declared**. That is what makes it an argument rather than an embarrassment. It establishes that popular sovereignty is not a guarantee of liberty - that a doctrine locating authority in the people answers the question of *who* rules without answering the question of *what may be done to whom* - and that is a permanent and genuinely important political lesson, which is why the standard account records that the sovereignty principle "was invoked by the Terror as well as by liberals" and "does not by itself guarantee liberty".

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating the Terror as explicable rather than simply condemnable risks apologetics. A government that executed seventeen thousand people by judicial process in fourteen months, while suspending the constitution it had just written, does not need a nuanced verdict; it needs a plain one.

**Reply:** A plain verdict and an explanation are not alternatives, and the sources demand both. The rule they lay down requires the cost to be stated explicitly - which this lesson does, with the figure and with the correction that the figure counts judicial executions only and is therefore an understatement of total violence. What the rule forbids is *replacing* explanation with condemnation, for a specific reason: the Terror was not carried out by men who rejected the Revolution's principles but by men who believed they were applying them under conditions of national danger. If that is not explained, the most important thing the episode teaches - that a doctrine of popular sovereignty does not by itself protect anyone - is lost, and what remains is a story about wicked individuals which teaches nothing transferable.

**Residual:** The counterfactual cannot be resolved. Whether the Revolution could have survived militarily without the coercive apparatus is an open historical question, and no position is taken on it here. The recorded verdict - that emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification - is a claim about the period after the danger receded, and it holds without resolving the counterfactual.

### UPSC application

The Terror is the single likeliest place in this topic for a question that tests judgement rather than knowledge, and the three-cause rule is the answer's structure. Open by conceding the emergency, establish the positive constitutional content that the phase produced - universal male political rights, the right of insurrection, the duty to provide work or livelihood - then give the three causes with the work each does, then the cost with its exact scope, and close with the phase table's verdict that emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification. For any question on popular sovereignty or on the limits of majority authority, the point in B4 is the one that lifts the answer: the Terror shows that locating sovereignty in the people settles who rules and not what may be done.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing "17,000 died in the Terror"; the figure is for people **tried and executed**, and total violence was greater.
- Inventing any other casualty figure, or a figure for the Revolution as a whole.
- Giving one cause for the Terror when the sources require three.
- Accepting either "necessary" or "insane" as a complete verdict.
- Omitting the Jacobin constitution's provisions, which is what makes the phase a political programme rather than only an emergency.
- Writing about the Terror without naming its machinery, when the Revolutionary Tribunal (March 1793), the Committee of Public Safety (6 April 1793), the levée en masse (23 August 1793) and the Law of Suspects (17 September 1793) can each be dated and each explains a different part of the system.
- Forgetting the Constitution of 1793, adopted on 24 June 1793 and then suspended, which is what makes the Jacobin phase a programme and not only an emergency.
- Treating the Terror as a betrayal of revolutionary principle, when its justification was drawn from within revolutionary doctrine.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Constitution of 1793 (Year I)**, adopted **24 June 1793** and then **suspended**: **universal male political rights**; the **right of insurrection**; government **must provide work or livelihood**.
2. ✅ The machinery: **Revolutionary Tribunal** (March 1793); **Committee of Public Safety** (6 April 1793); **levée en masse** (23 August 1793); **Law of Suspects** (17 September 1793); the **Vendée** insurgency from March 1793; the price and wage maximum, rationing, **pain d'égalité**, **Citoyen** and **Citoyenne**; the **guillotine**, named after **Dr Guillotin**. The Convention divided between **Girondins** and **Montagnards**.
3. ✅ The Jacobins believed in **direct democracy** and, fearing danger to the Revolution, adopted harsh measures.
4. ✅ Recorded figure: in **14 months, around 17,000 people were tried and executed**.
5. ✅ The factual-risk control permits that figure and forbids invented totals for the Revolution.
6. ⚠️ Scope discipline: the figure counts judicial executions; prison deaths, killings without trial and the civil war in the west are not included, and were substantially larger.
7. ✅ Terror handling rule: **war emergency + ideological logic + factional struggle**; refuse both "necessary" and "insane"; state the cost.
8. ✅ The Terror joined **mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence in one unstable package**.
9. ✅ Phase achievements 1792-94: republic, wider male political rights, national defence.
10. ✅ Why the phase gave way: **emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification**.
11. ✅ Debate positions: necessary wartime defence against coercive excess and political narrowing; synthesis explains both context and cost.
12. ✅ Trap: "the Terror proves the Revolution failed" - answer with both survival-context and coercive cost.
13. ✅ Popular sovereignty was invoked by the Terror as well as by liberals, and does not by itself guarantee liberty.
14. ⚠️ Direct democracy makes procedure look like obstruction, which is how coercion is justified from inside the doctrine rather than against it.
15. ⚠️ The work-or-livelihood clause is a social right, rare before the twentieth century, and it completes the equality ladder's second and third rungs in one phase.

### Concept check

**Question:** Construct a verdict on the Terror that satisfies the requirement to explain rather than merely condemn, while stating the cost accurately. Identify the two failure modes your verdict must avoid and the exact scope of the figure you cite.

**Model answer:** The verdict must begin by conceding the emergency: France was at war, the Revolution faced opponents who could be represented as agents of that war, and the government that responded was the same government that produced the period's most advanced constitutional programme, since the Jacobin constitution recorded universal male political rights, a right of insurrection and a duty on government to provide work or livelihood. It must then give three causes rather than one - war emergency, which explains why extraordinary powers were claimed and widely accepted; ideological logic, which explains why coercion could be justified from within revolutionary doctrine, since a belief in direct democracy makes procedural obstruction look like resistance to the sovereign people; and factional struggle, which explains why the coercion consumed revolutionaries themselves and why its intensity tracked political conflict rather than military danger. It must state the cost explicitly: around seventeen thousand people were tried and executed in some fourteen months, and that figure counts judicial executions only, so deaths in prison, killings without trial and the far greater mortality of the civil war in the west lie outside it. And it should close with the recorded verdict that emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification. The two failure modes to avoid are romanticisation, which keeps the democratic content and drops the coercion, and dismissal, which keeps the coercion and drops the political content; the sources refuse both "necessary" and "insane" as complete verdicts.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the Terror was a departure from revolutionary principle. Its justification was drawn from within revolutionary doctrine - the priority of the common good, the immediacy of the popular will and the defence of the nation - which is exactly why it shows that popular sovereignty answers the question of who rules without answering the question of what may be done to whom.

**Bridge to Lesson 10:** An emergency government that has outlived its emergency has a specific problem: it cannot relax without exposing the men who ran it, and it cannot continue without losing the consent that sustained it. The next lesson follows what happened when that contradiction broke, and why the resolution took four years to produce a general.

## Lesson 10 - The retreat: how the emergency ended and why it produced a soldier

Progress: 10/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the closure of the radical phase; the Directory, 1795-99; propertied republicanism; political fatigue; the transfer of the casting vote to the army

Book context: queried - the standard account's timeline entry for 1795 and its four-phase summary, its phase table describing propertied republicans, the retreat from Terror and the shift of power toward the army, and its date control for the Directory and Brumaire; the historiographical companion on the army as a social force; and a standard political-theory text on the club's closure in 1794 and on de facto sovereignty
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: why the vacuum fills from the barracks

```text
   THE EMERGENCY GOVERNMENT'S TRAP
   -------------------------------
   cannot relax ---------> the men who ran it are exposed
   cannot continue -------> the consent that sustained it is gone
                  |
                  v
        THE RADICAL PHASE CLOSES  (club closed, 1794)
                  |
                  v
   WHO GOVERNS NOW? the available options, and why each is blocked
   -------------------------------------------------------------
   the monarchy?        blocked by the regicide of 1793
   the Jacobin left?    blocked by the reaction against the Terror
   the sans-culottes?   blocked - they never had governing machinery
   the propertied
   republicans?         AVAILABLE - and this is the Directory
                  |
                  v
   THE DIRECTORY, 1795-99
   base:      propertied republicans
   achieved:  retreat from Terror
   problem:   threatened from BOTH sides at once, and legitimate
              to neither
                  |
                  v
   EVERY CRISIS MUST BE SURVIVED BY FORCE
                  |
                  v
   THE ONLY RELIABLE FORCE IS THE ARMY
                  |
                  v
   "INSTABILITY SHIFTS POWER TOWARD THE ARMY"
                  |
                  v
   BRUMAIRE, 1799
```

*The middle block is the analytical heart of the lesson: a regime that is legitimate to nobody must be defended by somebody, and whoever defends it repeatedly ends up choosing who governs.*

### 1. How the radical phase ended

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text records the club's fate in one clause: the Jacobin club "became increasingly radical and instituted the Terror. It was **closed in 1794**."

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table records the achievement of the phase that followed as the **retreat from Terror**, and its social base as **propertied republicans**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structural reason the emergency phase closed is worth stating, because it is the same reason emergency governments generally close, and an answer that gives the structure rather than the incident travels further. An emergency government derives its authority from danger. As the danger recedes, the authority recedes with it, while the apparatus does not - and the men who operate the apparatus cannot dismantle it without exposing themselves to the consequences of what they have done with it. The result is a regime that is simultaneously less justified and less able to stop, which is an unstable equilibrium that breaks at the first serious internal challenge. The phase table's verdict names exactly this: **emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification**.

✅ **Fact:** The event has a name, and it is the revolutionary calendar's. Robespierre was overthrown on **9 Thermidor Year II**, which in the Gregorian calendar is **27 July 1794**; the standard school account records that he was convicted by a court in July 1794, arrested, and sent to the guillotine the next day - **28 July 1794**. The political reaction that followed is conventionally called the **Thermidorian Reaction**, and the club itself was closed later that year.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** use the name. "Thermidor" has become a general political term for the stage at which a revolution's own apparatus turns on its authors and the revolution is halted from within by the men who made it, and an answer that names it is placing the French case inside a recognised pattern rather than merely reporting a change of ministry. The two-day sequence also makes the point that the Terror ended by the same instrument it had used: its architect was tried and guillotined, which is the sharpest available illustration of the phase table's verdict that emergency coercion had outlived the emergency's justification.

### 2. The Directory, described exactly

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account describes the new arrangement. The fall of the Jacobin government allowed the wealthier middle classes to seize power; a **new constitution** - conventionally the **Constitution of Year III**, approved in **August 1795** - **denied the vote to non-propertied sections of society**, provided for **two elected legislative councils**, and had those councils appoint a **Directory**, an executive of **five members**, as a deliberate safeguard against the concentration of power in a one-man executive as under the Jacobins. The Directors often clashed with the councils, which then sought to dismiss them, and the resulting political instability paved the way for the rise of a military dictator, **Napoleon Bonaparte**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the design is the most instructive thing about the Directory and it is usually skipped. Five executives instead of one is an anti-Napoleon device adopted five years before Napoleon - an explicit constitutional response to the experience of concentrated emergency power. That it produced precisely the outcome it was built to prevent is the strongest single illustration in this topic of the limits of institutional design against a social and political vacuum.

✅ **Fact:** Its four-phase summary describes **1795-99: Directory**, with the note that **instability and fatigue weaken civilian rule.**

✅ **Fact:** Its phase table gives the fullest version: the dominant coalition is ⚠️ **propertied republicans**; the achievement is ✅ the **retreat from Terror**; and the reason the phase gave way is ✅ that **instability shifted power toward the army.**

✅ **Fact:** Its date control lists **Directory (1795-99)** and **Brumaire (1799)** as distinct markers.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three words in that record carry the analysis, and each is worth unpacking.

**Propertied.** The regime's base was defined by property, which means the widening of the political nation that 1792 had achieved was, in practice, narrowed again. That is not a detail: it means the Directory could not appeal to the constituency that had sustained the republic at its most dangerous moment, because its own social character was a repudiation of that constituency's claims.

**Republicans.** The regime was committed to the republic, which blocked the one solution that would have ended its legitimacy problem - a restoration - and which also ensured that royalist opposition was permanent rather than negotiable. Combined with the regicide of 1793, this made compromise with the right structurally unavailable.

**Instability.** The regime was threatened from both sides at once and was legitimate to neither. A government opposed by the left for abandoning the republic's social promise and by the right for existing at all has no coalition available to it, and must therefore survive each crisis by force rather than by politics.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** put those three together and the sources' own conclusion becomes a deduction rather than an assertion. A regime that must survive by force will, repeatedly, need an institution that can supply it. There was one such institution, and by using it the Directory taught it that civilian authority depended on military consent. That is what "instability shifts power toward the army" means as a mechanism, and it is why the phrase is worth quoting rather than paraphrasing.

### 3. The army as a social force

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion lists the **army under Napoleon** among the social forces of the Revolution, with what it wanted - **order, victory, advancement by merit** - and what it achieved: it **became the vehicle for authoritarian consolidation.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the middle item in that list is the surprising one and the most important. "Advancement by merit" is a revolutionary demand, not a reactionary one: it is the military form of the bourgeoisie's original grievance in Lesson 1, which was wealth and ability without matching status. That is why the army is not an external force that captured the Revolution but a **revolutionary institution** whose own interest pointed toward a strong executive. An officer promoted on ability under the republic had everything to lose from a restoration that would restore promotion by birth, and much to gain from a regime that guaranteed careers open to talent while providing the order in which careers can be pursued.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most useful thing to understand about 1799, and it prevents the standard error of treating Napoleon's rise as a betrayal from outside. The army wanted what the Revolution had promised - merit, order and victory - and it found the Directory unable to supply the second and unreliable at the third. Lesson 13 completes the argument.

### 4. Fatigue as a political factor

✅ **Fact:** The standard account names **fatigue** explicitly: instability and fatigue weaken civilian rule.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** political fatigue is rarely treated seriously by candidates and it belongs in a good answer, because it explains consent rather than force. By 1799 France had passed through a decade in which the form of the state had changed four times, in which participation in politics had at times been lethal, and in which the promised settlement had repeatedly failed to arrive. Under those conditions a population does not need to be coerced into accepting a strong executive; it may accept one gratefully, provided the executive guarantees what has already been won. That is a claim about the *supply of consent*, and it explains why the consolidation of 1799 met so little resistance despite reversing the Revolution's political direction.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text supplies the concept that names the resulting situation. It records that **de facto sovereignty** arises where the actual sovereign differs from the legal one, lists **Napoleon in France** alongside Cromwell in England and the Bolsheviks after 1917 as historical examples, and notes that such a state of affairs is **marked by uncertainty, confusion and irregularity** and continues only temporarily, until the holder **evolves suitable means to legitimise** the authority.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that framework is exactly right for what follows, and it structures Lessons 13 and 14 in advance. Brumaire produces de facto power; the legal settlement, the Code and the imperial title are the means by which it is legitimised. Naming the process this way turns a sequence of events into an argument about how revolutionary regimes stabilise.

### 5. Why the Directory matters more than its reputation suggests

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** candidates often dispose of the Directory in a clause as an interlude of corruption and drift. Three reasons to give it a sentence or two of real content:

| Because | Consequence for the answer |
|---|---|
| It is where the retreat from the Terror actually happened | Without it, the Terror appears to end for no reason |
| It is where the political nation was narrowed again after 1792 | It shows that the widening of citizenship was reversible, which qualifies any triumphal account |
| It is where the army acquired the casting vote | Without it, Napoleon's rise looks like a personal coup rather than a structural outcome |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the examiner's point. The question is never really "how did Napoleon seize power" but "why was there power lying available to be seized", and the Directory is the whole of the answer.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This lesson makes the Directory's collapse look inevitable and therefore excuses the people who ended it. Regimes threatened from both sides survive all the time; what destroyed this one was the ambition of a general and the willingness of politicians to use him.

**Reply:** Ambition is necessary to the outcome and insufficient for it, and the reply is the same one the radicalisation lesson made. Ambitious generals are common; the conditions under which an ambitious general can replace a civilian government are not. Those conditions are what the sources describe: a regime with a narrow propertied base, no available coalition, permanent opposition from both directions, and a population exhausted by a decade of instability - so that force was required regularly and the institution supplying it acquired a veto. This session takes no position on individual motives, for the simple reason that the sources read for it record none. What they record is the structural transfer, in the phrase "instability shifts power toward the army", and that is a claim about the regime rather than about the general.

**Residual:** The regime's successive internal crises - how they arose, how many there were and how each was suppressed - are not reconstructed here. The transfer of power is the point, and it can be explained from the regime's design and social base without the episode-by-episode record.

### UPSC application

The Directory is worth two sentences in a phases answer and a full paragraph in any question about why the Revolution ended in authoritarian rule. The highest-value sentence in the whole lesson is the structural one: a government that is legitimate to neither side must survive by force, and the institution that supplies force acquires the power to choose. That sentence transfers to other cases in the syllabus and shows an examiner that the candidate is reasoning rather than narrating. The de facto sovereignty framework is also worth deploying in any question about legitimacy, because it names the exact problem the Consulate and the Empire were designed to solve.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Dismissing the Directory in a clause and thereby making Napoleon's rise inexplicable.
- Treating the army as an external force that captured the Revolution, when its demand for advancement by merit was a revolutionary demand.
- Calling the end of the radical phase merely "a reaction" when it can be named and dated - 9 Thermidor Year II, 27 July 1794, with Robespierre guillotined the following day.
- Forgetting that the Directory narrowed the political nation again after 1792, which qualifies any straight-line story of expanding citizenship.
- Omitting fatigue, and so explaining acquiescence in 1799 entirely by coercion.
- Merging the Directory's establishment in 1795 with Brumaire in 1799; the date control keeps them apart.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **9 Thermidor Year II = 27 July 1794**: Robespierre overthrown, guillotined **28 July 1794**; the **Thermidorian Reaction** follows; the Jacobin club is **closed in 1794**.
2. ✅ **Constitution of Year III**, **August 1795**: no vote for the non-propertied, **two elected legislative councils**, a **Directory** of **five**; instability shifts power toward the army.
3. ✅ **1795-99**: instability and fatigue weaken civilian rule.
4. ✅ Phase base: **propertied republicans**; achievement: **retreat from Terror**; reason it gave way: instability shifted power toward the army.
5. ✅ Date control keeps **Directory (1795-99)** and **Brumaire (1799)** distinct.
6. ✅ The army under Napoleon wanted **order, victory and advancement by merit**, and became the vehicle for authoritarian consolidation.
7. ✅ **De facto sovereignty**: the actual sovereign differs from the legal one; Napoleon in France is a recorded example; the condition is temporary until the holder evolves means of legitimisation.
8. ⚠️ Emergency governments close because authority recedes with the danger while the apparatus does not, and its operators cannot dismantle it safely.
9. ⚠️ *Propertied* narrowed the political nation; *republicans* foreclosed restoration; *instability* meant no available coalition.
10. ⚠️ A regime legitimate to neither side must survive by force, and the supplier of force acquires the casting vote.
11. ⚠️ The army's demand for advancement by merit is a revolutionary demand, which is why 1799 is a structural outcome rather than an external capture.
12. ⚠️ Fatigue explains consent, not merely coercion, in the acceptance of a strong executive.
13. ⚠️ The real question is not how Napoleon seized power but why power was lying available; the Directory is the answer.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Directory, rather than the Terror, is the phase that explains Napoleon - and state the mechanism by which a civilian regime transfers the effective power of decision to its own army.

**Model answer:** The Terror explains why the radical phase could not continue, since emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification and its operators could neither relax nor persist safely; but it does not explain why the successor regime was replaced by a soldier rather than by another civilian arrangement. The Directory supplies that explanation through three recorded features. Its base was propertied, which narrowed the political nation that 1792 had widened and cut it off from the constituency that had sustained the republic at its most dangerous moment. It was republican, which, together with the regicide of 1793, made compromise with royalist opposition structurally unavailable. And it was unstable, being opposed from the left for abandoning the republic's social promise and from the right for existing at all, so that no governing coalition was available to it. A regime with no available coalition must survive each crisis by force rather than by politics, and the only institution capable of supplying force repeatedly is the army; each use of that institution teaches it that civilian authority depends on its consent, until the body that has repeatedly saved the government acquires the power to choose it. That is the mechanism the sources compress into the phrase that instability shifts power toward the army, and it is completed by two further facts: that the army's own demand was for order, victory and advancement by merit, which are revolutionary rather than reactionary aims, and that a decade of instability and fatigue had left the civilian population willing to accept a strong executive that guaranteed what had already been won.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Napoleon's rise as the army invading politics from outside. The army was a revolutionary institution whose interest in careers open to talent aligned it with the Revolution's social settlement and against restoration; what it rejected was not the Revolution but the Directory's inability to provide order and reliable victory.

**Bridge to Lesson 11:** Before following power to the barracks, there is an accounting to be done. The Revolution had declared rights universally and administered them through a citizenship that excluded several categories of person entirely. The next lesson sets out who was excluded, how the exclusions were defended, and whether they change the verdict.

## Lesson 11 - The boundaries of the new citizenship: women, the enslaved, the colonies and the wage-earner

Progress: 11/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the gendered limits of citizenship; the collision of universal rights with a colonial slave economy; abolition, reversal and 1848; political voice without economic equality; how to evaluate the exclusions without anachronism

### Visual: the universal declaration and the administered citizenship

```text
 to        rights      governed    without           1792, then

 ionary    with a      consent     equality          under the
 action;   colonial                                  Directory
 denied    slave
 equal     economy
 political      |

 ship       ABOLISHED in the revolutionary period
            REVERSED under Napoleon
```
