   |         |            |           |                  |
   +---------+------------+-----------+------------------+
                          |
                          v
        A UNIVERSAL CLAIM ADMINISTERED THROUGH A
        BOUNDED CITIZENSHIP
                          |
                          v
        AND - decisively - the claim could be turned against
        the boundary, because it had been stated universally
```

*The final line is what stops this lesson from being a list of failures. The exclusions are real; so is the fact that the excluded used the excluders' own text, and the reversal-and-1848 sequence proves that the argument outlived the defeat.*

### 1. Why the exclusions belong in the main argument, not in a footnote

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there are two lazy ways to handle this material and both lose marks. The first treats the exclusions as an appendix of regrettable failures attached to an otherwise triumphant story. The second treats them as proof that the whole thing was a fraud. Neither is what the sources support.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's own formulation is the one to adopt: the Revolution's relevance is **double** - it supplied the universal claim and **simultaneously demonstrated how such claims get bounded in practice.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that is a claim about the structure of the settlement rather than a moral verdict on it. A universal declaration administered through a bounded citizenship is one fact, not two opposed facts; and understanding the relationship between the two halves is exactly what an "explain" or "critically examine" directive is testing.

### 2. Women: the question the sources themselves ask

✅ **Fact:** The chapter spine the standard account follows contains a section titled **"Did women have a revolution?"**, whose core point is the **gendered limits of citizenship** and which the account calls an excellent analytical addition.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's exclusions block states the position directly: women were **central to revolutionary action yet were denied equal political citizenship.**

✅ **Fact:** The same account records that citizenship **"was gendered and property-conditioned by turns"** and was **contested, not conferred once.**

✅ **Fact:** It records one further and particularly sharp point, which belongs to the Napoleonic settlement and is previewed here: the **Napoleonic Code narrowed married women's legal position**, and the source attaches the general lesson that **transmission was selective**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence those four facts describe is worse than simple exclusion, and it should be written as a sequence. Women were participants in the revolutionary action; they were denied equal political citizenship in the settlement that action produced; and the codification that exported the Revolution's legal achievements across Europe **narrowed** their legal position further. That is not a story of a right not yet extended. It is a story of a right withheld and then, in the very instrument that made the rest of the settlement permanent and portable, of a position made worse. The word "selective" in the source is doing precise work: the Code carried some of the Revolution's equality abroad and left some of it behind, and which parts travelled was a political choice.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reason this is analytically important rather than merely regrettable is that it tests the transmission thesis on which the whole enduring-relevance argument rests. If codification is the mechanism by which revolutionary principles spread, then whatever codification excluded did not spread, and the exclusion was exported along with the inclusion. An answer that makes that point has moved from describing a legacy to analysing how legacies actually work.

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account names the actors. Women started their own political clubs and newspapers, and **about sixty women's clubs** appeared in French cities, of which the **Society of Revolutionary and Republican Women** was the most famous. Their central demand was that women enjoy the same political rights as men; they were disappointed that the **Constitution of 1791 reduced them to passive citizens**, and they demanded the right to vote, to be elected to the Assembly and to hold political office.

✅ **Fact:** The most prominent of them was **Olympe de Gouges (1748-1793)**. She protested that the Constitution and the Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen excluded women from rights every human being was entitled to, and in **1791** wrote a **Declaration of the Rights of Woman and Citizen**, addressed to the Queen and to the members of the National Assembly and demanding that they act on it. In **1793** she criticised the Jacobin government for forcibly closing women's clubs; she was tried by the **National Convention**, charged with **treason**, and executed.

✅ **Fact:** The record is not uniformly negative, and an answer should say so. In the early years the revolutionary government created state schools and made **schooling compulsory for all girls**; fathers could no longer force daughters into marriage; marriage became a contract entered into freely and registered under civil law; **divorce was made legal** and could be sought by women as well as men; and women could train for jobs, become artists or run small businesses. During the **Reign of Terror**, however, the government ordered the **closure of women's clubs** and banned their political activities, and many prominent women were arrested and a number executed. **French women finally won the vote in 1946.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the shape of that record is the argument. Civil rights within the family and access to education and work were granted; political rights were refused; and when women organised to press for the political rights, the organisations themselves were suppressed by the most radical phase of the Revolution. The exclusion was therefore not an oversight awaiting correction but a boundary actively policed - and the fact that the author of the counter-declaration was executed by the Convention for treason is the sharpest single illustration available of how the universal language was administered.

### 3. Slavery and the colonies: a collision, not an oversight

✅ **Fact:** The chapter spine contains a section on **the abolition of slavery**, whose core point is **universal rights meeting empire**, and which the standard account describes as useful for contradiction analysis.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's exclusions block states the mechanism: **universal rights collided with a colonial slave economy**, and **abolition in the revolutionary period was contested and later reversed under Napoleon before final abolition came in 1848.**

✅ **Fact:** Its factual-risk control converts this into a writing rule: **do not say the Revolution "abolished slavery permanently"**; abolition was reversed under Napoleon, and **final French abolition is 1848.**

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records the same episode as a **vital contradiction-check on any simplistic narrative.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account supplies the particulars. The French Caribbean colonies - **Martinique, Guadeloupe** and **San Domingo**, the island more usually written **Saint-Domingue** - were major suppliers of tobacco, indigo, sugar and coffee, worked by enslaved Africans carried across the Atlantic in a triangular trade from ports such as **Bordeaux** and **Nantes**. The **National Assembly** debated whether the rights of man extended to all French subjects including those in the colonies, but **passed no law**, fearing opposition from businessmen whose incomes depended on the trade. It was finally **the Convention which, in 1794, legislated to free all slaves in the French overseas possessions**. The measure proved short-lived: **Napoleon reintroduced slavery**, by a law of **1802**, and slavery was **finally abolished in the French colonies in 1848**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one point of detail should be flagged rather than smoothed. The standard school account dates the reinstatement only loosely, as "ten years later" than 1794, which would give 1804; reference accounts date the restoring law precisely to **20 May 1802**. Use 1802, and note that the loose formulation exists - it is a small instance of the larger discipline this topic teaches, which is that a date carried casually becomes an error when it is repeated.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the ownership of each act is what makes the sequence examinable. The Assembly of the constitutional monarchy debated and did nothing; the **Convention**, the most radical body of the Revolution, abolished; the consolidating regime restored; and a later republic abolished again. Emancipation therefore tracks the radicalism of the governing body almost exactly, which is a far more precise finding than any general claim about the Revolution's attitude to slavery.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word **collided** is doing exact work and should be preserved in an answer. This was not a case in which a principle was accidentally not applied. It was a direct conflict between a universal claim and a system of production whose profitability depended on denying that claim to the people it enslaved - and the sequence records what happened when the two met: the principle won first, and then lost.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three-stage sequence - abolition, reversal, 1848 - is one of the most valuable pieces of evidence in the entire topic, for a reason that goes well beyond slavery. It is a **controlled demonstration** of how the Revolution's principles actually travelled through time. They were not adopted once and then held. They were asserted, defeated, and asserted again more than half a century later; and the second assertion was possible because the first had established the claim in the society's own law. That is the honest model of the Revolution's legacy, and it is why the standard account insists on a non-linear ladder rather than a straight line from 1789 to modern rights.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the colonial dimension generalises the point. A colonial subject stands in the same relation to the founding logic as an enslaved person: governed by an authority to which no consent was given, by a state whose own charter declares the preservation of natural rights to be the aim of every political association and names resistance to oppression among them. That is not a modern imposition on the texts; it is the texts' internal logic, and it is the reason anti-colonial movements continued to find them useful for two centuries.

### 4. The wage-earner: voice without security

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's exclusions block records the older survey's finding: **workers and artisans obtained political voice but not economic equality.**

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion attaches the corresponding trap: **never say the Revolution achieved full social equality**, because the older survey itself records that workers and artisans still lacked economic equality.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this exclusion differs in kind from the other three and the difference matters. Women, the enslaved and colonial subjects were excluded from the category of full citizen. Wage-earners were admitted to it - in 1792, as Lesson 5 recorded - and found that admission insufficient. Theirs is therefore not an exclusion from citizenship but a demonstration of its **limits**, and it is the origin of the argument that legal and political equality without economic security is formal rather than real. Lesson 6's three-stage ladder ends exactly here.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text records the sharpest available statement of the point, from Engels in *Anti-Dühring* (1878): "This equality reduced itself to bourgeois equality before the law; ... bourgeois property was proclaimed as one of the essential rights of man".

### 5. How the boundaries were defended - and why each defence fails

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** candidates usually stop at "hypocrisy", which is a verdict rather than an analysis. The exclusions were defended by arguments, and setting out the arguments and their answers is what an examiner means by evaluation.

| Defence | How it worked | Why it fails |
|---|---|---|
| **Redefinition** | Place the excluded outside the category of rights-bearer - as property, as dependants, as subjects rather than citizens | It assumes the conclusion; nothing in "men are born and remain free and equal in rights" supports the exception, and no independent ground for it was supplied |
| **Deferral** | Concede the principle, postpone the application to a safer time | A right that may be postponed indefinitely is a permission; and the postponement here lasted from the revolutionary abolition to 1848 |
| **Necessity** | Argue that application would ruin colonial trade, destabilise the family or endanger the state | It converts a claim of right into a cost calculation, which is precisely the move rights-language exists to block |
| **Capacity** | Assert that the excluded lack the independence of judgement that citizenship requires | It was the same argument used for the property qualification, and it fails for the same reason: it makes rights track existing social power rather than test it |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second row explains something the moral verdict cannot - why the contradiction was survivable for so long. Outright denial of the principle would have destroyed the legitimacy of the new order, because the principle was its foundation. Deferral preserved the legitimacy while suspending the obligation, which is how a regime can hold both positions simultaneously without visibly collapsing. The reversal under Napoleon and the long wait to 1848 are deferral in operation.

### 6. Evaluating without anachronism - the rule

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is a genuine methodological question here and an examiner rewards candidates who notice it. Judging the eighteenth century by twenty-first-century standards is anachronistic; refusing to judge it at all is evasive. The workable rule is to **judge a period by the standard it declared for itself**. The exclusions were not invisible at the time: the sources record that women were central to revolutionary action, and that abolition was attempted in the revolutionary period and then reversed - which means both the claim and its defeat were contemporary events, not retrospective discoveries. Criticism that uses the period's own stated premise is historical rather than anachronistic, and it is also far more damaging, because it cannot be answered by observing that people then thought differently.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text supplies the general warning that makes this rule necessary: natural rights "are claimed to be eternal and immutable. But, in actual practice, they are capable of immense variations", and the doctrine "can be interpreted in order to uphold the values of the particular class which invokes these rights."

### 7. The graded verdict

✅ **Fact:** The standard account supplies a verdict scaffold that grades the transformation by group, and it is the most efficient single sentence in the topic: the Revolution transformed society **"completely at the level of legal status and political legitimacy; substantially for the peasantry through the destruction of feudal dues; only partially for urban workers; and least of all for women and colonial subjects."**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** learn it as written. It answers the "how far" directive with a gradient rather than a verdict, it names the group at each level, and it is the reason this lesson exists inside the main sequence instead of at the end.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Emphasising exclusion turns the Revolution into a failure and obscures what it achieved. Legal equality replacing privilege by birth was an enormous advance; measuring an eighteenth-century settlement against universal suffrage and abolition is a category error that judges the past for not being the present.

**Reply:** The achievement is real and these lessons record it repeatedly: legal privilege was abolished, feudal laws were annulled, and the settlement survived both Napoleon and the restoration. But the exclusions are not a separate ledger to be set against the achievements. They are the **administered form** of the achievement - a universal claim delivered through a bounded citizenship - and that combination is one fact about the settlement, not two opposed ones. The reply to the anachronism charge is the rule in section 6: the criticism here uses the period's own declared standard, and the exclusions were contested at the time by people who had heard the declaration.

**Residual:** The honest residual is that the relationship between the universal claim and its boundaries was not fixed in advance. The same language that licensed exclusion by deferral supplied the excluded with their most effective weapon, and nothing in 1789 determined which use would prevail. The sequence from revolutionary abolition through reversal to 1848 shows both uses operating on the same question within sixty years. That indeterminacy is the truthful verdict, and it is more interesting than either celebration or indictment.

### UPSC application

Any directive inviting assessment - *critically examine*, *how far*, *enduring relevance* - should reach this lesson by the second body paragraph. Use the graded verdict scaffold, then the abolition-reversal-1848 sequence as the single best piece of evidence for a non-linear legacy, then the four-defence table to show that the contradiction was argued rather than merely ignored. The 2025 verified question on enduring relevance is directly owned by this topic, and this lesson supplies the qualification without which that answer is a list of principles; the point that the Code narrowed married women's legal position while exporting the rest is the sharpest single sentence available for it.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that the Revolution abolished slavery, or abolished it permanently; abolition was reversed under Napoleon and final French abolition is 1848.
- Claiming the Revolution achieved full social equality.
- Presenting the exclusions as oversights rather than as the administered form of the settlement.
- Reducing the contradiction to hypocrisy instead of setting out redefinition, deferral, necessity and capacity.
- Forgetting that the Code narrowed married women's legal position while exporting the rest of the settlement.
- Leaving the women's side unnamed when Olympe de Gouges, her Declaration of the Rights of Woman and Citizen of 1791, the Society of Revolutionary and Republican Women and the closure of the women's clubs during the Terror are all available and decisive.
- Judging the period by present standards rather than by the standard it declared for itself.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The chapter spine asks **"Did women have a revolution?"** and treats the gendered limits of citizenship as a major analytical theme.
2. ✅ Women were **central to revolutionary action yet denied equal political citizenship**: about **sixty clubs**, the **Society of Revolutionary and Republican Women**, **Olympe de Gouges (1748-1793)** and her **Declaration of the Rights of Woman and Citizen (1791)**, tried by the **Convention** for **treason** and executed; clubs **closed during the Terror**; girls' schooling, free marriage and **legal divorce** granted; the vote only in **1946**.
3. ✅ Citizenship was **gendered and property-conditioned by turns**; contested, not conferred once.
4. ✅ The **Napoleonic Code narrowed married women's legal position**; transmission was **selective**.
5. ✅ Slavery: **universal rights collided with a colonial slave economy**.
6. ✅ The colonies were **Martinique, Guadeloupe** and **San Domingo (Saint-Domingue)**; the **National Assembly debated and passed nothing**; the **Convention abolished slavery in 1794**; **Napoleon restored it in 1802**; **final French abolition came in 1848**. Some school accounts date the restoration loosely as "ten years later"; use **1802**.
7. ✅ Writing rule: never say the Revolution abolished slavery permanently.
8. ✅ The episode is a **vital contradiction-check** on any simplistic narrative.
9. ✅ Workers and artisans obtained **political voice but not economic equality**; never claim full social equality.
10. ✅ Engels, *Anti-Dühring* (1878): equality "reduced itself to bourgeois equality before the law".
11. ✅ Natural rights are claimed to be eternal but are "capable of immense variations" and can uphold the values of the class invoking them.
12. ✅ Graded verdict: complete for legal status and political legitimacy; substantial for the peasantry; partial for urban workers; least for women and colonial subjects.
13. ✅ The relevance is **double**: the universal claim, and the demonstration of how such claims get bounded in practice.
14. ⚠️ Four defences of the boundary - redefinition, deferral, necessity, capacity - and deferral is what made the contradiction survivable.
15. ⚠️ Abolition → reversal → 1848 is a controlled demonstration that the principles travelled by defeat and re-assertion, not by continuity.
16. ⚠️ Judge the period by the standard it declared for itself; the exclusions were contested at the time.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Declaration of the Rights of Man was hypocrisy, since France kept slaves and denied women the vote." Convert this into an examiner-grade evaluation and state what analytical work your version does that the original does not.

**Model answer:** The improved version runs: the revolutionary settlement proclaimed rights universally and administered them through a bounded citizenship, and the relationship between the two is the settlement's defining feature rather than a lapse within it. Citizenship was gendered and property-conditioned by turns, and women were central to revolutionary action yet denied equal political citizenship, with the Napoleonic Code subsequently narrowing married women's legal position even as it exported the rest of the legal settlement across Europe; universal rights collided directly with a colonial slave economy, abolition in the revolutionary period was contested, was reversed under Napoleon, and final French abolition came only in 1848; workers and artisans obtained political voice without economic equality; and colonial subjects were governed without consent by a state whose founding text made the preservation of natural rights the aim of every political association and named resistance to oppression among them. The boundaries were not unnoticed but defended - by redefining the excluded out of the rights-bearing category, by deferring application to a safer time, by pleading economic or social necessity, and by denying the excluded the independence of judgement citizenship was said to require - and deferral is what made the contradiction survivable, because outright denial of the principle would have destroyed the legitimacy the new order rested on. The analytical work this does is threefold. It explains a mechanism instead of pronouncing a verdict; it shows the exclusions were contested at the time, since abolition was actually attempted and then reversed, which makes the criticism historical rather than anachronistic; and it preserves the decisive legacy, which is that a universal claim can be turned against its own administrators, so that the sixty years from revolutionary abolition to 1848 are evidence of how the Revolution's principles actually travelled - by defeat and re-assertion rather than by unbroken continuity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that establishing the exclusions diminishes the Revolution. It locates its significance more precisely: a settlement that proclaimed a standard it did not meet handed its opponents a weapon that a settlement proclaiming nothing would never have produced, and the sources describe the resulting relevance as double for exactly that reason.

**Bridge to Lesson 12:** The accounting so far has been in the currency of rights and citizenship. Before turning to Napoleon it is worth asking a blunter question, and one that decides the "how far did it transform society" directive: strip away the declarations, and what had actually changed in France by the end of the decade?

### Visual: the transformation, graded by layer

  and forms of address

  OF WAGE-EARNERS         political voice         business handed to
                          without economic        the nineteenth

  AND COLONIAL            and, in the Code,       by the instrument
  SUBJECTS                reversed                that exported the rest
```

*The table is the answer to the "how far did it transform society" directive, and the third column is what converts it from a description into a verdict. Notice that the two layers with permanent durability are the two that took the form of abolitions.*

### 1. The largest single change

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records, from the older survey, that **feudal laws were annulled and many revolutionary gains survived even after Napoleon seized power.**

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records the peasantry's achievement in the same terms: **major anti-feudal gains.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the change that makes the Revolution a social event rather than a constitutional one, and its importance is best seen through what it made impossible. After the annulment, the relation between landholder and cultivator in France was a relation of property and contract, not of status and obligation. A lord could still be rich and a cultivator poor; what he could no longer be was a **lord** in the legal sense - a person entitled to dues by virtue of what he was. The distinction is precisely Lesson 1's, applied at the end of the decade instead of the beginning: the legal categories are gone, and the inequalities that remain are of a different kind.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and this is why the restoration after 1815 could not undo 1789, which is the point the standard account's verdict scaffold makes in a single clause - the Restoration could not simply undo 1789. A restored monarchy can restore a throne, a court and a flag. It cannot restore an obligation that has gone unpaid for a generation, in thousands of villages, from a population that has been armed and mobilised in the interval.

### 2. Property, office and status

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records the bourgeoisie's aims - **end of privilege, office by merit, property rights** - and its achievement: **gains in law, status and institutional power.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table for 1799-1815 records **careers open to talent expanded in practice**, and the historiographical companion states the same in its table of what the Revolution preserved.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "careers open to talent" is a phrase worth pausing on, because it is the precise institutional content of the abolition of privilege. It means that access to office, rank and professional advancement is determined by demonstrated capacity rather than by birth. That is a modest-sounding principle with enormous consequences: it converts the state from a structure of inherited positions into a structure of appointments, and it creates a permanent constituency - everyone who holds a position they could not have held before - with a direct interest in the settlement's survival. Lesson 14 shows that constituency being consolidated deliberately.

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text records the political consequence in the formulation of a standard history of political theory: "The abolition of feudalism meant for Marx the rise to power of the middle class and the creation of a political system which made its power effective."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** treat that as one recorded interpretation rather than as a settled finding, because Lesson 17 will show it being contested. What it usefully identifies is that the abolition of privilege did not leave a vacuum where the old hierarchy had been: it left a society in which wealth, education and office were the operative sources of social power, and the group best placed to convert those into political influence was the one that already possessed them.

### 3. Everyday life: the change that is easiest to underrate

✅ **Fact:** The chapter spine the standard account follows contains a section on **the Revolution and everyday life**, whose core point is **new language, symbols and citizenship**, and which the account classifies as **social transformation beyond high politics.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's evidence bank repeats it with more detail under the heading of citizenship replacing subjecthood: **new language, symbols and forms of address remade everyday life.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** forms of address are not a decorative detail; they are the point at which a constitutional principle becomes a daily practice. A society in which the way one person addresses another encodes their relative rank is a society that rehearses hierarchy in every transaction. Changing the form of address does not by itself redistribute anything, and it changes the experience of everything: it converts an unspoken assumption into a contestable claim. That is why the sources treat it as transformation rather than symbolism, and it is a genuinely good point to have available for a question on how a revolution reaches people who never read its documents.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it also supplies the mechanism by which the Revolution reached a largely non-reading population - not through pamphlets, which Lesson 3 ruled out as a direct channel, but through changed practice in the ordinary conduct of life.

### 4. The modern nation

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records, from the older survey, that the Revolution **gave the term "nation" its modern political meaning and relocated sovereignty in the people.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account carries the same point in its must-know set: the Revolution gave the term **nation** a modern political meaning.

✅ **Fact:** Its evidence bank adds the consequence and the qualification together. The modern nation **supplies the vocabulary later used by nineteenth-century unifications and twentieth-century anti-colonial movements**; and the qualification is that **nationalism derived from it was also exclusionary and war-making.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the Revolution's most powerful and most double-edged export, and the shift it performs is precise. Before, the nation was a population under a ruler - a descriptive term for the governed. After, the nation is the **source of authority** - the body in whose name a government must act and to which it is answerable. That change is what makes the nineteenth century's central political demand possible: if authority derives from the nation, then a nation without its own state has a grievance that no amount of good government by a foreign or dynastic ruler can answer.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the qualification must be written with it, because it is the difference between an answer and an advertisement. The same principle that licensed self-determination also defined who belonged and who did not, and it supplied the justification for wars fought in the nation's name. The unifications and the imperial rivalries of the nineteenth century both descend from it, and both belong to the topics that own them.

### 5. What did not change

✅ **Fact:** The older survey records that **workers and artisans obtained political voice but not economic equality**, and the historiographical companion converts this into an explicit prohibition: **never say the French Revolution achieved full social equality.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's graded verdict states the whole result in one sentence: the transformation was **complete at the level of legal status and political legitimacy; substantial for the peasantry through the destruction of feudal dues; only partial for urban workers; and least of all for women and colonial subjects.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the gradient is the answer, and the reason for the gradient is Lesson 1's distinction applied consistently. Where the demand was for an abolition, delivery was complete and durable. Where the demand was for a provision, delivery was partial at best. Where the demand required admitting a new category of person to the status of full citizen, delivery was least of all - because that is the one change that the group holding power had a direct interest in refusing, and no fiscal or military emergency compelled it.

### 6. The distinction that governs the rest of the topic

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** everything in Lessons 13 to 16 depends on separating two settlements that are usually merged.

```text
  THE SOCIAL SETTLEMENT                  THE POLITICAL SETTLEMENT
  ---------------------                  ------------------------
  privilege abolished                    sovereignty relocated in
  feudal law annulled                      the people
  equality before the law                a republic
  careers open to talent                 representative institutions
  property secured                       liberty of person and press
           |                                      |
           v                                      v
  SURVIVES 1799                          DOES NOT SURVIVE 1799
  SURVIVES 1815                          RETURNS ONLY THROUGH
  IS EXPORTED BY CODIFICATION            REPEATED LATER STRUGGLE
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that divergence is the single most examinable idea in the second half of this topic. It is the basis of the verdict on Napoleon, the basis of the answer on enduring relevance, and the reason the Revolution's legacy is non-linear rather than cumulative. Hold it; Lesson 14 uses it directly.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The list of changes is impressive on paper and thin in substance. Legal equality without economic equality leaves the poor exactly as poor; careers open to talent benefit those who already have education; and changed forms of address cost nothing. On this account the Revolution redistributed status among the comfortable and left the structure of wealth untouched.

**Reply:** The objection is the strongest available and the sources concede a great deal of it - which is why the graded verdict grades. But two things resist it. First, the annulment of feudal law was not a change of status among the comfortable: it removed real, recurring extractions from a rural population that constituted the great majority, and it did so permanently. Second, legal equality is not nothing even where economic inequality persists, because it changes what inequality can be *justified by*. Once distinction must demonstrate public utility, wealth has to argue for its privileges rather than inherit them - and every subsequent attack on inequality in France, including the socialist one the objection implicitly voices, was conducted in terms the Revolution made available. The honest formulation, which the sources supply, is that the Revolution transformed the basis of legal and political authority far more completely than it transformed the distribution of wealth and power.

**Residual:** The annulment of feudal law and the peasantry's major gains are established; what happened to landholding afterwards - who acquired land, on what terms, and with what effect on rural inequality - is a substantial separate question and is not answered here. The claim made is about obligations extinguished, not about land redistributed, and those are different claims.

### UPSC application

This lesson owns the *how far did it transform society* directive outright, and the graded verdict scaffold is the answer's closing sentence. The structure to use is the layer table in the visual: take three or four layers, give the extent and the durability of each, and close with the gradient. For a 20-mark answer, add the social-against-political settlement distinction in section 6, because it converts a description of changes into a thesis about which changes survived and why. The point about forms of address is a cheap and distinctive inclusion that most answers will not have.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Claiming full social equality, against the explicit prohibition in the sources.
- Treating "careers open to talent" as a slogan rather than as the institutional content of the abolition of privilege.
- Asserting a redistribution of land; what was extinguished was seigneurial obligation, and the transfer of property is a separate claim with separate evidence.
- Writing that the Revolution created nationalism without the recorded qualification that nationalism derived from it was also exclusionary and war-making.
- Omitting everyday life, language and forms of address, and so describing a transformation that never reached ordinary people.
- Merging the social and political settlements, which makes the verdict on Napoleon impossible to state.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Feudal laws were annulled**, and many revolutionary gains survived Napoleon's seizure of power.
2. ✅ Peasantry: **major anti-feudal gains**; bourgeoisie: gains in **law, status and institutional power**.
3. ✅ **Careers open to talent** expanded in practice.
4. ✅ The chapter spine's section on everyday life: **new language, symbols and citizenship** - social transformation beyond high politics.
5. ✅ New language, symbols and **forms of address** remade everyday life.
6. ✅ The Revolution gave the term **nation** its modern political meaning and **relocated sovereignty in the people**.
7. ✅ The modern nation supplied the vocabulary of nineteenth-century unifications and twentieth-century anti-colonial movements.
8. ✅ Qualification: nationalism derived from it was also **exclusionary and war-making**.
9. ✅ Workers and artisans obtained political voice but **not economic equality**; never claim full social equality.
10. ✅ Graded verdict: complete for legal status and political legitimacy; substantial for the peasantry; partial for urban workers; least for women and colonial subjects.
11. ✅ "The abolition of feudalism meant for Marx the rise to power of the middle class and the creation of a political system which made its power effective."
12. ⚠️ Abolitions were delivered completely and durably; provisions partially; admissions to citizenship least of all.
13. ⚠️ The Restoration could not simply undo 1789, because extinguished obligations cannot be re-collected village by village.
14. ⚠️ The social settlement and the political settlement diverge after 1799; that divergence governs every later verdict in this topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the layer table, answer the question "how far did the French Revolution transform French society?" in a way that grades the answer rather than delivering a single verdict - and state the general principle that explains why the grading falls as it does.

**Model answer:** The transformation was complete at the level of legal status and political legitimacy, since privilege was abolished as a legal category, equality before the law replaced distinction by estate, and sovereignty was relocated in the people, with the term nation acquiring its modern political meaning. It was substantial for the peasantry, because feudal laws were annulled and the annulment survived even Napoleon's seizure of power, converting a relation of status and obligation into one of property and contract. It was real but narrower at the level of office and everyday life, where careers open to talent expanded in practice and new language, symbols and forms of address remade daily conduct, so that the Revolution reached people who never read its documents. It was only partial for urban workers, who obtained political voice without economic equality. And it was least of all for women and colonial subjects, since women were central to revolutionary action yet denied equal political citizenship and had their legal position narrowed further by the Code, while slavery was abolished, reversed under Napoleon and only finally ended in 1848. The principle that explains the grading is the difference between an abolition and a provision, extended by a third case. An abolition can be legislated once and, once obligations have lapsed in practice, is almost impossible to reverse; a provision must be delivered continuously by a state with the resources and will to do so; and an admission of a new category of person to full citizenship requires the group already holding power to concede voluntarily, which no fiscal or military emergency compelled it to do.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from the annulment of feudal law that land was redistributed. The sources record the extinction of seigneurial obligations, not a transfer of landholding, and the two are different claims with different evidence; asserting the second where only the first is supported is a common and visible error.

**Bridge to Lesson 13:** France at the end of the decade has a completed social settlement and a political settlement that has failed four times. The next lesson asks the question that follows from that combination: why was the regime that finally stabilised the country a military one, and why in 1799?

## Lesson 13 - Brumaire, 1799: why a general, and why then

Progress: 13/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the coup of 1799; de facto power and the problem of legitimising it; what the army wanted; the Consulate as a phase; why the social settlement made the political reversal acceptable

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's timeline entry for 1799 and its date control for Brumaire, its phase table for 1799-1815 naming the army, the notables and the codifying state, and its record that the Revolution enters a new authoritarian-consolidating phase; the historiographical companion on the army's aims and on Napoleon as both heir and gravedigger; and a standard political-theory text on de facto sovereignty and its legitimisation
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the offer that could be accepted, and by whom

```text
                     FRANCE, 1799
        social settlement COMPLETE and popular
        political settlement FAILED four times
                          |
                          v
        WHAT WOULD A STABILISING REGIME HAVE TO OFFER?
                          |
   +------------------+---+--------------+----------------+
   |                  |                  |                |
 PEASANTS         BOURGEOISIE          ARMY            GENERAL
 guarantee        secure property,     order,          PUBLIC
 that feudal      careers open to      victory,        an end to
 dues never       talent, no           advancement     a decade of
 return           return of            by merit        instability
   |              privilege              |                |
   |                  |                  |                |
   +------------------+---+--------------+----------------+
                          |
                          v
        NOTICE WHAT IS ABSENT FROM EVERY COLUMN:
        nobody's price is the REPUBLIC
                          |
                          v
        A REGIME CAN THEREFORE BUY BROAD CONSENT BY
        GUARANTEEING THE SOCIAL SETTLEMENT WHILE
        DISMANTLING THE POLITICAL ONE
                          |
                          v
        BRUMAIRE, 1799: de facto power
                          |
                          v
        the remaining task is LEGITIMISATION
        -> law (1804 Code)   -> title (1804 Empire)
        -> administration    -> victory
```

*The three words at the centre - "nobody's price is the republic" - are the lesson. This is not cynicism; it is an account of what each group had actually obtained by 1799 and what each therefore stood to lose.*

### 1. Brumaire, precisely dated

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's timeline records **1799: Napoleon's coup**, attributing the point to a standard reference work, and states its significance as the moment the **Revolution enters a new authoritarian-consolidating phase.**

✅ **Fact:** Its date control names the event **Brumaire (1799)** and keeps it distinct from the Directory of 1795-99 and from the Code of 1804.

✅ **Fact:** Its phase table describes the dominant coalition of **1799-1815** as ⚠️ the **army, notables and codifying state**, records the achievements as ✅ the **Napoleonic Code, careers open to talent, and anti-feudal reform exported**, and records the reason the phase ended as ✅ **empire, censorship and conquest ending republican liberty.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three-part description of the coalition is worth more than its brevity suggests. **Army** supplies force and a constituency of promoted officers. **Notables** is the sources' term for the propertied and office-holding groups who had gained from the Revolution and wanted the gains secured. **Codifying state** names the method - an administration whose characteristic activity is the reduction of the settlement to written, uniform, enforceable law. The three fit together: force secures the settlement, notables benefit from it, and codification makes it permanent. That is a complete description of the regime's social basis in three words, and it is the best short answer to any question about the nature of the Napoleonic state.

✅ **Fact:** The coup has a name and a precise date. The **coup of 18 Brumaire**, on **18-19 Brumaire Year VIII** - **9-10 November 1799** - overthrew the **Directory** and established the **Consulate**, with Napoleon as **First Consul**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two-day span is worth carrying because it marks what kind of event this was. A coup executed against a sitting executive by a general acting with civilian collaborators, completed inside forty-eight hours and followed by a new constitutional form, is not a popular rising like 14 July or 10 August. No crowd decided this, and that absence is the clearest possible sign of how far the political nation of 1792 had already been demobilised by 1799.

### 2. Why the army, and not another faction

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion lists the **army under Napoleon** as a social force whose aims were **order, victory and advancement by merit**, and which became the **vehicle for authoritarian consolidation.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records that instability **shifted power toward the army** as the Directory's defining failure.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lesson 10 established the mechanism; here is the additional point that completes it. Every other candidate for power in 1799 was disqualified by the decade itself. A royalist restoration was blocked by the regicide of 1793 and by the peasantry's fear for the annulment of feudal law. A return to Jacobin government was blocked by the reaction against the Terror. The sans-culotte movement had never possessed governing machinery. The propertied republicans were the Directory, and their failure was the problem to be solved. The army was the only institution left that was **national in reach, revolutionary in its own interest, and capable of enforcing a decision** - and its interest in careers open to talent aligned it with the social settlement rather than against it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the answer to "why a general" and it does not require any claim about the general. It is a claim about the elimination of alternatives, which is both safer and more analytical.

### 3. De facto power and the problem it creates

✅ **Fact:** A standard political-theory text supplies the concept precisely. Where the legal or formal sovereign is displaced, the legal sovereign retains **de jure** sovereignty while the actual holder exercises **de facto** sovereignty; the text lists **Napoleon in France** among its historical examples, alongside Cromwell in England and the Bolsheviks after 1917; it records that such a state of affairs is **marked by uncertainty, confusion and irregularity**, that it can continue only temporarily, and that its holder must **evolve suitable means to legitimise** the authority. It adds that de facto authority is obeyed **either because of sheer force or a strong following among the people.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that framework organises everything from 1799 to 1804 and should be used explicitly in an answer. A coup produces power without title. Power without title is unstable because it invites the same treatment it used. The holder therefore has three instruments available, and Napoleon used all three: **law**, which makes the regime's benefits concrete and portable; **title**, which supplies formal standing; and **victory**, which supplies the consent that comes from success. Lesson 14 is the first, Lesson 15 the third, and the imperial title of 1804 is the second.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the text's final clause is the one to notice - obedience rests on sheer force **or a strong following among the people**. The whole argument of this lesson is that the second was genuinely available, because of what the regime guaranteed. An answer that explains Napoleon's domestic position purely by coercion has missed the settlement.

### 4. Why the political reversal met so little resistance

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the question candidates most often fail to ask, and asking it is what distinguishes a strong answer. A country that had executed a king for being above the law accepted, within a decade, a regime that ended representative government and, within five years, an emperor. Why?

| Group | What it had obtained by 1799 | What a restoration or renewed instability threatened | What the new regime offered |
|---|---|---|---|
| Peasantry | Annulment of feudal laws | The return of dues and charges | A guarantee that the annulment stands |
| Bourgeoisie and notables | Property rights, office by merit, status | Both Jacobin levelling and aristocratic restoration | Security of property and career, with order |
| Army officers | Promotion on ability | Restoration of promotion by birth | Careers open to talent, and victories |
| Population generally | An end to privilege | Another decade of instability and coercion | Stability, with the social gains intact |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read down the final column and the political settlement is missing from every row. That is not because anyone had ceased to value liberty; it is because, for each group, the thing most at risk in 1799 was a **social gain** and the regime on offer guaranteed it. The republic's constituency had been the sans-culotte movement and the Jacobin left, and both had been defeated in the reaction. A political settlement with no constituency willing to fight for it does not survive a determined challenge.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and this is the deep reason the sources describe Napoleon as both heir and limiter. He was able to end the republic **because** he guaranteed the Revolution's social achievements; the two facts are connected, not merely coincident. An answer that states the connection has explained the paradox instead of merely noting it.

### 5. The formulation to carry forward

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion's recommended formulation is: **Napoleon stabilised the social revolution while reversing much of its political republicanism.**

✅ **Fact:** Its debate matrix states the two sides - he **consolidated legal gains** against he **ended republican liberty and launched conquest** - and gives the synthesis as **both heir and gravedigger.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that formulation is only earned if the connection in section 4 has been made. Otherwise it is a balanced-sounding sentence with nothing behind it. The connection is that the stabilisation of the social revolution is precisely what made the reversal of the political revolution acceptable to the people who had made it.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This account is too structural. Coups are made by people who plan them, and the outcome of 1799 depended on the abilities and ambition of one individual who might have failed, died or chosen otherwise. Explaining Brumaire by the elimination of alternatives is a way of avoiding the historical actor.

**Reply:** The reply concedes the individual and defends the structure's role. The coup, its date, the phase it opened and the social coalition on which the new regime rested are all firm; the planning and the personal calculations behind it are not, and nothing here turns on them. What the structure explains is not that the coup succeeded but that **an opening existed** - that every alternative claimant was disqualified, that the institution with force had a revolutionary interest, and that the social settlement had created a broad constituency indifferent to the republic's survival. Individual ability determines who walks through an open door; it does not open the door. The two explanations are complementary, and only the structural one can be stated with confidence.

**Residual:** The genuine gap is the popular response. The sources record the coalition on which the regime rested but not the extent or distribution of popular support, and the plebiscitary mechanisms by which the regime later claimed it are not recorded here. The claim made in this lesson is about interests, not about measured consent, and it is marked as an inference throughout.

### UPSC application

This lesson answers the question that any Napoleon essay must answer before it can evaluate him: why was there an opening. Use the four-row table in section 4 - it is the fastest way to show that the political reversal was acceptable because the social settlement was guaranteed, which is the thesis of the whole second half of the topic. The de facto sovereignty framework is worth naming explicitly in any question that touches legitimacy, because it identifies what the Code, the imperial title and the victories were each *for*. Do not narrate the coup; the sources do not support it and the marks are not there.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Narrating the coup at length; 18 Brumaire, 9-10 November 1799, the fall of the Directory and the Consulate are enough, and the marks are in why the opportunity existed.
- Explaining Napoleon's rise by personal ambition alone, which leaves the opening unexplained.
- Treating the army as counter-revolutionary, when its demand for advancement by merit was a revolutionary demand.
- Merging Brumaire (1799) with the Code and the imperial title (both 1804).
- Explaining domestic acceptance by coercion alone, when the recorded framework allows obedience to rest on a strong following among the people.
- Stating "heir and gravedigger" as a balanced phrase without the connection that makes it true.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **1799: Napoleon's coup**; the Revolution enters a new authoritarian-consolidating phase.
2. ✅ The **coup of 18 Brumaire**, **9-10 November 1799**: the Directory falls and the **Consulate** is established with Napoleon as **First Consul** - distinct from the Directory of 1795-99 and from 1804.
3. ✅ The 1799-1815 coalition: **army, notables and codifying state**.
4. ✅ Achievements of the phase: Napoleonic Code, careers open to talent, anti-feudal reform exported.
5. ✅ Why the phase ended: **empire, censorship and conquest ended republican liberty**.
6. ✅ The army wanted **order, victory and advancement by merit**, and became the vehicle for authoritarian consolidation.
7. ✅ **De facto sovereignty**: actual against legal sovereign; Napoleon in France is a recorded example; it is temporary until legitimised; obedience rests on sheer force **or** a strong following among the people.
8. ✅ Recommended formulation: Napoleon **stabilised the social revolution while reversing much of its political republicanism**.
9. ✅ Debate synthesis: **both heir and gravedigger**.
10. ⚠️ Every alternative in 1799 was disqualified: restoration by the regicide and rural fear; Jacobinism by the reaction; the crowd by lack of machinery; the propertied republicans by their own failure.
11. ⚠️ Nobody's price in 1799 was the republic - which is why the political settlement fell with so little resistance.
12. ⚠️ Three instruments of legitimisation: law, title and victory - the Code, the Empire and the campaigns.
13. ⚠️ Napoleon could end the republic **because** he guaranteed the social settlement; the two facts are causally connected.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why a country that had abolished its monarchy and executed its king in the name of popular sovereignty accepted, within a decade, a regime that ended representative government - and identify what the new regime had to supply that the Directory could not.

**Model answer:** The explanation lies in what each major group had actually obtained by 1799 and what it stood to lose. The peasantry had secured the annulment of feudal laws and feared above all their restoration; the bourgeoisie and notables had secured property rights, office by merit and status, and were threatened from both the Jacobin and the royalist directions; army officers had secured promotion on ability and stood to lose it in any restoration; and the population generally had gained the end of privilege and had endured a decade of instability and coercion. For none of these groups was the republic itself the thing most at risk, because the republic's committed constituency had been the sans-culotte movement and the Jacobin left, and both had been defeated in the reaction against the Terror. A political settlement without a constituency prepared to fight for it does not survive a determined challenge. What the new regime had to supply, and the Directory could not, was therefore a credible guarantee of the social settlement combined with order: a promise that feudal dues would not return, that property and careers open to talent were secure, and that the cycle of crises resolved by force was over. The Directory failed on every count, since it was propertied and therefore alienated from the republic's popular base, republican and therefore unable to compromise with royalism, and unstable and therefore dependent on the army for each successive rescue, which is exactly how instability shifted power toward the army. Having taken power by that route, the new regime held only de facto sovereignty, which the sources describe as temporary until legitimised, and it therefore had to supply law, title and victory in order to convert force into authority.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading 1799 as the army imposing a counter-revolution on an unwilling nation. The regime's coalition was army, notables and codifying state; its offer was the guarantee of the Revolution's social achievements; and the recorded framework for de facto authority explicitly allows obedience to rest on a strong following among the people rather than on force alone.

**Bridge to Lesson 14:** De facto power must be legitimised or it decays. The next lesson examines the first and most durable of the three instruments used to legitimise it - the reduction of the entire revolutionary settlement to a written code - and asks how much of it was the Revolution's and how much was new.

## Lesson 14 - The Napoleonic settlement at home: the Code, the state and the closing of the revolutionary questions
