```text
  nationalism and anti-feudal                popular sovereignty was
  reform spread across Europe                subordinated to military-
                                             political authority
     |                                             |
     +----------------------+----------------------+
                            |
                            v
        "NAPOLEON STABILISED THE SOCIAL REVOLUTION WHILE
         REVERSING MUCH OF ITS POLITICAL REPUBLICANISM"
                            |
                            v
        AND ONE ITEM CROSSES THE LINE IN THE WRONG DIRECTION:
        the Code NARROWED married women's legal position
        -> transmission was SELECTIVE
```

*The two columns are reproduced from the historiographical companion's own table and are the skeleton of any Napoleon answer. The line at the bottom is what prevents the answer from becoming a tidy balance sheet.*

### Part A - What the settlement preserved

#### A1. The Code of 1804

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's timeline records **1804: Napoleonic Code / Napoleon as emperor**, attributing the point to a standard reference work, and states the significance in five words: **legal equality survives, the republic does not.**

✅ **Fact:** The same account records that the older survey and the standard reference work **both connect Napoleon with the Napoleonic Code, which preserved important elements of legal equality and modern civil order.**

✅ **Fact:** Its must-know set states that the **Napoleonic Code is a major legal legacy of the revolutionary era.**

✅ **Fact:** Its factual-risk control is explicit and frequently tested: **do not date the Napoleonic Code to the 1790s; it is 1804.**

✅ **Fact:** Its evidence bank records the analytical point that matters most: the Code **preserved legal equality and anti-feudal reform**, and it shows **the mechanism of transmission - codification carried the settlement beyond France.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last sentence is the single most important idea in the second half of this topic, and it deserves to be unpacked rather than quoted. A revolution's achievements are, at first, embodied in the institutions and personnel of the state that made them. Institutions can be dismantled and personnel replaced; a restored regime can undo a revolution by undoing its government. What codification does is convert the settlement into a **portable object**: a written, systematic, internally consistent body of civil law that can be adopted by any state that finds it useful, including states with no revolution of their own and no sympathy for the one that produced it. That is why the Revolution's social settlement outlived the Revolution's politics, and it is the answer to the enduring-relevance question at the level of mechanism rather than sentiment.

#### A2. Why the anti-feudal settlement was safe

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion's preservation column records that the **abolition of feudal remnants stayed**, that the **Code preserved legal rationalisation**, that **careers open to talent expanded in practice**, and that **nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account repeats the point from the older survey: **feudal laws were annulled and many revolutionary gains survived even after Napoleon seized power.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lesson 13's argument completes here. The regime did not preserve the social settlement out of sentiment; it preserved it because the settlement was the basis of its own support. A government resting on the army, the notables and a rural population whose overriding fear was the return of feudal dues could not have attacked those gains without destroying itself. The preservation was structural, which is why it was reliable - and which is also why it should be presented as an interest rather than as a virtue.

#### A3. The state that carried it

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table names the regime's character in three words: the **army, notables and codifying state.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "codifying state" is a description of method, and the method had consequences beyond civil law. A state whose characteristic activity is reduction to uniform written rule tends toward centralisation, standardisation and administrative regularity - because a uniform rule requires a uniform administration to apply it, and a uniform administration requires appointment by capacity rather than by local standing. That is how the abolition of privilege, careers open to talent and administrative centralisation turn out to be three aspects of one process rather than three separate policies.

✅ **Fact:** The settlement's dates are close together and should be kept apart. The **Civil Code** was promulgated on **21 March 1804**; Napoleon was proclaimed **Emperor** on **18 May 1804** and crowned on **2 December 1804**. Three years earlier, the **Concordat of 1801**, signed with **Pope Pius VII** in July 1801 and promulgated in 1802, had settled the religious question that Lesson 7 opened, restoring public Catholic worship while retaining state authority over the Church and leaving the revolutionary sale of Church lands undisturbed.

✅ **Fact:** The standard school account adds the Code's companion measures and the regime's self-image. Napoleon **saw his role as a moderniser of Europe**; he introduced laws such as the **protection of private property** and a **uniform system of weights and measures provided by the decimal system**; and, having crowned himself Emperor in 1804, he conquered neighbouring countries, **dispossessing dynasties and creating kingdoms in which he placed members of his family**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Concordat is the item most often missed and it completes the argument of these lessons about the two settlements. The religious rupture was the one revolutionary wound that had produced a popular counter-revolutionary constituency; closing it was therefore not piety but statecraft, and the terms show exactly which revolution was being kept. Catholic worship returns - a concession to the population; the Church remains under state authority and does not recover its confiscated lands - a refusal to return the material settlement of 4 August 1789. That is the social revolution being guaranteed and the ideological quarrel being retired, in one instrument.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the decimal system of weights and measures deserves a clause of its own in a transformation answer. It is the least dramatic item in the settlement and among the most durable: a uniform, rational, nationally enforced standard that replaced a patchwork of local measures is the same abolition-of-particularism that the Code performs for civil law, and it outlived every regime that followed.

### Part B - What the settlement curtailed, and how to judge it

#### B1. The political reversal, stated from the sources

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion's curtailment column records four items: the **republic was ended**; **empire and censorship returned concentration of power**; **war became imperial conquest**; and **popular sovereignty was subordinated to military-political authority.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table records the same outcome as the reason the phase ended: **empire, censorship and conquest ended republican liberty.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that censorship appears in both records, and it is the item candidates most often omit. Its inclusion is analytically important because it identifies the precise mechanism of the political reversal. Ending a republic does not require abolishing every revolutionary principle; it requires removing the conditions under which the principles can be **invoked against the government**. A settlement that guarantees equality before the law while suppressing the public criticism through which unequal application could be exposed has kept the substance of the social revolution and removed the instrument of the political one. That is a more precise description of the Napoleonic state than any adjective.

#### B2. The item that crosses the line

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records, in its enduring-principles table, that **the same Code narrowed married women's legal position; transmission was selective.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this belongs in Part B rather than Part A because it is not a neutral omission but an active narrowing, and it is the strongest single objection to a tidy two-column reading of the settlement. The instrument that carried legal equality across Europe simultaneously carried a diminished legal position for married women across the same territory. The preservation and the curtailment were performed by the **same act**, which means the two ledgers are not independent. An answer that presents Napoleon as preserving the social revolution without qualification has missed the sharpest available criticism of that claim - and it is a criticism the sources themselves supply.

#### B3. The misconception set

✅ **Fact:** The standard account records the misconception that **Napoleon simply restored the old order**, and the correction: **he preserved important anti-feudal legal changes even while ending the republic.**

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records the complementary misconception that **Napoleon ended all revolutionary change**, and the correction: **he ended the republic, not the anti-feudal and legal transformation.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** both corrections point the same way, and it is worth noticing that the sources bother to state the point twice. The error they are guarding against is the assumption that a political reaction must be a social reaction. It need not be, and in this case was not - which is the structural discovery of the whole topic and the reason Lesson 12's distinction between the two settlements had to be established before this lesson could be written.

#### B4. The verdict

✅ **Fact:** The compact formulation the historiographical companion recommends is: **Napoleon stabilised the social revolution while reversing much of its political republicanism.**

✅ **Fact:** Its debate matrix gives the synthesis as **both heir and gravedigger**, and the standard account's verdict scaffold gives the same result in answer-ready form: **"Heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political revolution - which is precisely why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789."**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the clause after the dash is what makes the scaffold worth more than its two halves. It supplies the *consequence* of the split verdict: because the social revolution had been stabilised, codified and exported, a restoration could recover the throne without recovering the society. That is the sentence with which to close any Napoleon answer, and it is also the hinge into Lesson 16.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Crediting Napoleon with preserving the Revolution's social settlement inverts the relationship. He inherited a settlement that was already irreversible - feudal obligations had lapsed, privilege had been abolished, and no regime could have restored either without civil war. Preserving what cannot be destroyed is not an achievement; it is a description of his constraints.

**Reply:** The objection is largely right about the constraint and wrong about the conclusion, and the reply identifies what the regime actually added. Irreversibility in practice is not the same as security in law. A settlement resting on lapsed obligations and defeated claimants is secure only so long as no government attempts restoration, and the previous decade had shown how quickly governments changed. What codification supplied was not the settlement but its **durability and its portability**: a written, systematic body of law which made the social order enforceable by ordinary courts, intelligible to officials who had never heard a revolutionary speech, and adoptable by states elsewhere. The sources make exactly this point when they identify codification as the mechanism of transmission. Napoleon's contribution is therefore best described not as preserving the settlement but as converting it from an accomplished fact into an institution.

**Residual:** The Code's article-by-article content, and how completely the regime's administrative and educational measures achieved their aims, are beyond what this level of study can assess. What is evaluated here is the codification mechanism and the two effects that are firmly established - the preservation of legal equality and anti-feudal reform, and the narrowing of married women's legal position.

### UPSC application

This lesson answers the most likely Napoleon question in this part of the syllabus, and the structure is given: two columns, then the item that crosses the line, then the verdict with its consequence clause. Two moves distinguish a strong answer. The first is explaining codification as a **mechanism of transmission** rather than listing the Code as an achievement - this is what shows the examiner that the candidate understands how legacies travel. The second is the selective-transmission point about married women, which converts a balanced answer into a critical one. The date control is worth checking before you write: the Code and the imperial title are both 1804, not the 1790s.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Dating the Code to the 1790s; it is 1804.
- Writing that Napoleon restored the old order; he preserved important anti-feudal legal changes while ending the republic.
- Writing that Napoleon ended all revolutionary change; he ended the republic, not the legal transformation.
- Listing the Code as an achievement without explaining codification as the mechanism by which the settlement travelled beyond France.
- Omitting censorship, which is the precise mechanism of the political reversal.
- Presenting the two ledgers as independent, when the Code performed a preservation and a narrowing in the same act.
- Omitting the Concordat of 1801, which is what closed the religious rupture of 1790 and shows precisely which half of the Revolution the regime was keeping.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **1804**: Napoleonic Code and Napoleon as emperor; legal equality survives, the republic does not.
2. ✅ The Code, promulgated **21 March 1804**, preserved **legal equality, anti-feudal reform and modern civil order**; Napoleon was proclaimed Emperor **18 May 1804** and crowned **2 December 1804**. The **Concordat of 1801** with **Pope Pius VII** restored public Catholic worship while keeping the Church under state authority and leaving the confiscated lands unreturned. Companion measures: **protection of private property** and a **uniform decimal system of weights and measures**.
3. ✅ Factual control: the Code is **1804**, never the 1790s.
4. ✅ Codification **carried the settlement beyond France** - the recorded mechanism of transmission.
5. ✅ Preserved: abolition of feudal remnants; legal rationalisation; careers open to talent expanded in practice; anti-feudal reform and nationalism spread across Europe.
6. ✅ Curtailed: the republic ended; empire and **censorship** returned concentration of power; war became imperial conquest; popular sovereignty subordinated to military-political authority.
7. ✅ The regime's character: **army, notables and codifying state**.
8. ✅ The same Code **narrowed married women's legal position**; transmission was **selective**.
9. ✅ Correction: Napoleon did not simply restore the old order.
10. ✅ Correction: Napoleon did not end all revolutionary change; he ended the republic, not the anti-feudal and legal transformation.
11. ✅ Formulation: **Napoleon stabilised the social revolution while reversing much of its political republicanism**; synthesis - **both heir and gravedigger**.
12. ✅ Verdict scaffold: heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political revolution - which is why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789.
13. ⚠️ Censorship identifies the mechanism: keep equality before the law, remove the means of invoking it against the government.
14. ⚠️ Codification converts an accomplished fact into an institution - durable, enforceable by ordinary courts, and adoptable abroad.
15. ⚠️ A political reaction need not be a social reaction; that is the structural discovery of the topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why codification, rather than conquest, is the more important mechanism by which the French Revolution's achievements reached the rest of Europe - and state the qualification that prevents this from becoming a triumphal account.

**Model answer:** Conquest can impose arrangements on a territory only for as long as the conquering power occupies it, and the Napoleonic empire was defeated within a decade of its height, so any achievement that depended on occupation should have been reversed in 1815 along with the empire itself. Codification works differently. It converts a political settlement into a written, systematic and internally consistent body of civil law, which the sources identify as the mechanism that carried the settlement beyond France; because such a body of law is portable, enforceable by ordinary courts and intelligible to officials with no revolutionary commitment, it can be retained or adopted by states that reject the revolution which produced it. That is why the abolition of feudal remnants, legal rationalisation and equality before the law survived both Napoleon's fall and the conservative restoration, while the republic did not. The qualification is the sources' own and it is decisive: transmission was selective. The very Code that carried legal equality across Europe also narrowed married women's legal position and carried that narrowing with it, so the mechanism transmitted the settlement's exclusions as faithfully as its emancipations. The honest formulation is therefore that codification made the social revolution durable and portable while determining, by political choice rather than by legal necessity, which parts of it would travel.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the preservation of the social settlement as evidence of Napoleon's revolutionary sympathies. The regime rested on the army, the notables and a rural population whose overriding fear was the return of feudal dues, so preserving the settlement was a structural interest rather than a conviction - which is precisely why the preservation was reliable and why the political liberty that no such constituency defended was not.

**Bridge to Lesson 15:** The settlement has been secured at home by law and by title. The third instrument of legitimisation was victory, and it is the one that destroyed the regime - not by failing, at first, but by succeeding so widely that it taught the rest of Europe the very principle it had suppressed in France.

## Lesson 15 - Empire, conquest and the nationalism the Revolution created against itself

Progress: 15/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: war as imperial conquest; the export of anti-feudal reform; how the modern nation became a weapon against its exporter; the limits of what the sources record about the imperial economy

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the historiographical companion's record that nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe, that war became imperial conquest and that popular sovereignty was subordinated to military-political authority, and its section on world linkages recording inspiration across Europe and South and Central America; and the standard account's evidence-bank row on the modern nation with its exclusionary and war-making qualification
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 16
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the boomerang

```text
  FRANCE EXPORTS, BY CONQUEST AND BY LAW:
  ---------------------------------------
    anti-feudal reform          the modern idea of the NATION
    legal rationalisation       as the source of authority
    careers open to talent
              |                              |
              v                              v
  IN THE CONQUERED TERRITORIES:      AND THE SAME IDEA SAYS:
  privilege and seigneurial          "a people is the source of
  obligation are attacked;           authority over itself"
  administration is rationalised                |
              |                                 v
              |                    WHICH, ADDRESSED TO A POPULATION
              |                    UNDER FRENCH RULE, READS:
              |                    "then French rule over us has
              |                     no authority"
              |                                 |
              +----------------+----------------+
                               |
                               v
             RESISTANCE TO FRENCH RULE IS CONDUCTED
             IN THE FRENCH REVOLUTION'S OWN LANGUAGE
                               |
                               v
             THE EMPIRE IS DEFEATED PARTLY BY THE
             PRINCIPLE IT WAS SPREADING
                               |
                               v
             BUT THE REFORMS DO NOT GO HOME WITH THE ARMIES
             -> anti-feudal reform and nationalism remain
```

*This is the topic's finest irony and one of the most reusable arguments in world history: a principle that legitimises a regime at home can delegitimise the same regime abroad, because the principle does not know where the border is.*

### 1. The empire's double record

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion's curtailment column records that under Napoleon **war became imperial conquest** and that **popular sovereignty was subordinated to military-political authority.**

✅ **Fact:** Its preservation column records, in the same table, that **nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two entries sit in opposite columns of the same table and describe the same campaigns. That is not an inconsistency in the source; it is the structure of the phenomenon. The armies that subordinated popular sovereignty to military authority were the same armies that carried anti-feudal reform and the national idea into territories that had neither. An answer that reports only one column has reported half of an event.

✅ **Fact:** The standard account's phase table records the same duality in its own terms: the phase's achievements include **anti-feudal reform exported**, and the reason it ended is that **empire, censorship and conquest ended republican liberty.**

### 2. The export of reform, and what it actually meant

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** consider what "anti-feudal reform exported" means for a territory that receives it at the point of a bayonet. Three consequences follow, and they are not all in the same direction.

| Consequence | Effect |
|---|---|
| Existing privileged orders lose legal standing | A permanent constituency is created against any future restoration of their position |
| Cultivators are relieved of seigneurial obligation | A population acquires a material interest in the reform surviving the regime that brought it |
| The reform arrives with a foreign occupation | The same population may resent the occupier while wishing to keep the reform |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the mechanism that decides the outcome of 1813-15 and beyond. A reform can be separated from its author. Populations across Europe could, and did, resist French rule while retaining what French rule had introduced - which is exactly why the sources record anti-feudal reform and nationalism as having spread across Europe, in the permanent column, while recording the empire's collapse in the other.

### 3. The national idea as a weapon against its exporter

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records, from the older survey, that the French Revolution **inspired movements in almost every country of Europe and in South and Central America**, and that it **gave the term "nation" its modern meaning and relocated sovereignty in the people.**

✅ **Fact:** It records the consequence: this is **the strongest bridge to later nineteenth-century nationalism, constitutionalism and anti-feudal reform.**

✅ **Fact:** The standard account attaches the qualification in its evidence bank: **nationalism derived from it was also exclusionary and war-making.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the logical structure of the boomerang is worth setting out step by step, because it is the reusable part.

```text
1. The Revolution asserts: authority derives from the nation.
2. That assertion legitimises the revolutionary state in France,
   because the French state can claim to act for the French nation.
3. The assertion contains no geographical limit: it is a claim
   about nations as such, not about one nation.
4. Exported to a conquered territory, the same assertion says that
   authority over that territory derives from ITS nation.
5. French authority over that territory therefore fails the test
   that French authority at home passes.
6. Resistance to French rule is now available in the language of
   the French Revolution, and cannot be answered in it.
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** step five is where the empire's ideological position becomes untenable. A conqueror who rules by traditional dynastic right can be resisted only by another traditional claim or by force. A conqueror who rules in the name of popular sovereignty has supplied his subjects with a principle that condemns him, and he cannot repudiate it without repudiating the basis of his own regime at home. That is the precise sense in which the empire was defeated partly by its own doctrine - and it is a general mechanism, not a French curiosity.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the qualification in the sources is essential to include. The nationalism that resulted was not a uniformly liberating force. It was **also exclusionary and war-making** - the same principle that licensed a people's claim to govern itself also defined who was inside the people and who was not, and supplied the justification for wars fought in the nation's name. The nineteenth-century unifications and the imperial rivalries that followed both descend from this moment, and both belong to the topics that own them.

✅ **Fact:** The empire's instruments of pressure can be named and dated. The **Continental System** was inaugurated by the **Berlin Decree of 21 November 1806**, which barred continental Europe from trading with Britain and was Napoleon's answer to British naval blockade. Enforcing it drew France into the **Peninsular War** in Spain and Portugal from **1808 to 1814**, and into the invasion of **Russia in 1812**, whose army crossed the Niemen in June and retreated in December.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** those three items belong together because they are one causal chain, and stating it as a chain is where the marks are. Economic warfare against Britain required every European coastline to comply; compliance could not be obtained by agreement from states with their own trade interests, so it had to be imposed; imposition meant occupation and war in the Iberian peninsula and, when Russia withdrew from the system, invasion in the east. The Continental System is therefore not a commercial footnote but the mechanism that converted a dominant land empire into an over-extended one - and it did so by the internal logic of the policy rather than by any single miscalculation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Peninsular War also supplies the clearest case of the boomerang this lesson is about. Resistance there was popular and sustained rather than merely dynastic, and it was conducted against a regime that presented itself as the carrier of modern reform - which is precisely the position a universalist empire cannot argue its way out of.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a word on proportion, since this is where answers most often go wrong in the opposite direction. Questions in this part of the syllabus ask about causes, consequences, transformation and legacy, so the campaigns earn marks only as evidence for a mechanism. Name the Continental System, the Peninsular War and the Russian campaign, give each one clause showing what it demonstrates - imposed compliance, popular resistance, over-extension - and move on. A paragraph of battles and coalitions displaces the analysis that is actually being marked; a chain of three dated instruments supporting an argument does not.

### 4. Why expansion was structurally difficult to stop

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one further point, because it completes Lesson 13's account of legitimisation. Victory was one of the three instruments by which de facto power was converted into authority. An instrument of that kind has a property that law and title do not: it must be **renewed**. A code, once promulgated, continues to operate; a title, once assumed, persists; but prestige derived from victory decays unless victories continue. A regime that has made military success part of its claim to rule therefore has a standing incentive to seek further success, and a single serious defeat threatens not merely a province but the basis of the regime's authority.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that is the structural reason an empire of this kind expands until it fails, and it is the bridge to the next lesson. It also explains why the same regime that secured its position at home by guaranteeing stability was unable to provide stability abroad: the two were supplied by different instruments, and only one of them was self-sustaining.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The boomerang argument is too neat. Resistance to French rule across Europe was largely conservative - dynastic, religious and local - and was organised by exactly the privileged orders whose position French reform had attacked. Calling it revolutionary nationalism reads a later idea backwards into the period.

**Reply:** The objection is a genuine corrective and the lesson should absorb rather than resist it. What the sources support is that **nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe** and that the Revolution **inspired movements in almost every country of Europe and in South and Central America**; they do not claim that every act of resistance to France was nationalist in the later sense, and neither does this session. The defensible position is that the period made the national principle available as a language of political claim, that this language was used by some resisters and later by many more, and that the conservative and the national forms of opposition coexisted. The strongest evidence for the argument is not the resistance of 1813 but the nineteenth century, which the sources identify as the strongest bridge: the movements that made the national principle into the century's organising political demand drew on the vocabulary this period generalised.

**Residual:** The composition of particular resistance movements, their relative strength and the depth of popular participation in them are not assessed here. The boomerang mechanism is a claim about what the exported principle made available, supported by the established fact that nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread; it is not a report of how any one resistance was constituted, and it is marked as inference throughout.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the bridge from this topic to the nineteenth century, and it supplies the strongest closing paragraph for any question on the Revolution's world-historical effect. The move that earns marks is the boomerang mechanism stated as a general principle - a doctrine of popular sovereignty cannot be confined to the state that proclaims it - followed immediately by the recorded qualification that the resulting nationalism was also exclusionary and war-making. For questions on the modern nation, on the origins of nineteenth-century nationalism, or on why empires founded on universal principles fail, this is the material. Resist the temptation to narrate campaigns.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Substituting a campaign narrative for analysis; name the Continental System, the Peninsular War and the Russian campaign, and use each to demonstrate a mechanism rather than to fill a paragraph.
- Treating the Continental System as a commercial footnote rather than as the policy whose enforcement produced the Peninsular and Russian over-extensions.
- Reporting only one column of the empire's record - either the reforms or the conquest - when the same armies produced both.
- Presenting the resulting nationalism as uniformly liberating, without the recorded qualification that it was also exclusionary and war-making.
- Claiming that all European resistance to France was nationalist, when much of it was dynastic, religious and local.
- Missing the structural point that victory, unlike law and title, must be continuously renewed.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Under Napoleon, **war became imperial conquest** and popular sovereignty was **subordinated to military-political authority**.
2. ✅ In the same record, **nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe**.
3. ✅ The phase's achievements include **anti-feudal reform exported**; its ending is **empire, censorship and conquest ending republican liberty**.
4. ✅ The instruments, dated: the **Continental System**, launched by the **Berlin Decree of 21 November 1806**; the **Peninsular War, 1808-14**; the invasion of **Russia, 1812** - one causal chain of imposed compliance, popular resistance and over-extension.
5. ✅ The Revolution **inspired movements in almost every country of Europe and in South and Central America**.
6. ✅ It gave the term **nation** its modern meaning and **relocated sovereignty in the people**.
7. ✅ This is the **strongest bridge** to nineteenth-century nationalism, constitutionalism and anti-feudal reform.
8. ✅ Qualification: the nationalism derived from it was also **exclusionary and war-making**.
9. ⚠️ Exported reform has three effects: privileged orders lose standing; cultivators gain an interest in the reform's survival; and the reform arrives with an occupier who may be resisted while the reform is kept.
10. ⚠️ The boomerang in six steps: authority derives from the nation → legitimises France at home → contains no geographical limit → condemns French rule abroad → cannot be repudiated without repudiating the regime → resistance becomes available in the conqueror's own language.
11. ⚠️ Law and title persist once granted; **prestige from victory must be renewed**, which is why such a regime expands until it fails.
12. ⚠️ Reforms can be separated from their author, which is why they remained in Europe after the armies left.
13. ⚠️ Conservative, religious and local resistance coexisted with national resistance; the strongest evidence for the national principle's spread is the nineteenth century rather than 1813.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the principle of popular sovereignty, which legitimised the Napoleonic regime in France, operated to delegitimise it abroad - and state what this implies generally about empires founded on universal principles.

**Model answer:** Popular sovereignty holds that authority derives from the nation, and in France that proposition supported the regime, since the state could claim to act for the French nation and to have secured the social settlement the nation valued. The proposition, however, contains no geographical restriction: it is a claim about the relation between any people and the authority governing it, not a privilege of one people. Carried into a conquered territory by armies that also carried anti-feudal reform and legal rationalisation, the same proposition necessarily reads as a statement that authority over that territory derives from its own nation, so that French rule there fails precisely the test French rule at home passes. The conqueror therefore cannot answer the resistance in his own terms, because repudiating the principle abroad would destroy the basis of his authority at home; and resistance acquires a vocabulary supplied by the very power it opposes. The general implication is that an empire founded on a universal principle carries within its own legitimising doctrine the argument for its dissolution, which distinguishes it from an empire founded on dynastic or religious right, where the ruler's claim is particular and cannot be turned against him in the same way. Two qualifications complete the answer: the reforms could be separated from their author, so anti-feudal reform and the national idea remained in Europe after the armies withdrew; and the nationalism that resulted was also exclusionary and war-making, so the inheritance was not simply emancipatory.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the spread of revolutionary reform and the conduct of imperial conquest as opposed facts about the period. The sources place them in opposite columns of the same table because they were produced by the same campaigns; an answer must report both, and the interest of the topic lies precisely in their combination.

**Bridge to Lesson 16:** An empire whose authority requires continuous victory meets its limit when victory stops. The next lesson takes the settlement to its end, asks what survived it, and confronts the one question that makes this topic examinable in the present tense.

## Lesson 16 - 1815 and after: what survived, in what form, and why the legacy is not a straight line

Progress: 16/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Waterloo and the end of the Napoleonic phase; the six enduring principles; the non-linear legacy ladder; the double relevance; the handover to the Vienna settlement

### Visual: the ladder, not the line

```text
   WRONG MODEL                        RIGHT MODEL
   -----------                        -----------
   1789 rights claim                  1789 RIGHTS CLAIM
        |                                   |
        | (uninterrupted                    v
        |  institutional               1793-94 TERROR
        |  continuity)                 rights invoked against rights
        v                                   |
```
