# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - Foundations, Lessons 1 to 3

### Cumulative check A1 - from a legal order to a terminal crisis

**Question:** Trace the path from "the old regime was a legally constituted order of privilege" to "the crisis of 1789 could not be resolved inside the constitution", stating at each step what the structure supplied and what it could not.

**Model answer:** The structure supplied a society in which exemption from taxation, access to office and jurisdictional advantage followed from estate membership rather than from wealth, so that the First and Second Estates were exempt by legal status while the Third Estate, about ninety-five per cent of the population, carried the burden. What it could not supply is a date, because an arrangement of this kind had persisted for centuries and had survived earlier shocks. The fiscal crisis supplied the date: a monarchy financially troubled and pushed toward bankruptcy needed revenue from exactly the groups whose exemption it itself guaranteed, which meant it could obtain that revenue only by repudiating the legal order on which it rested or by asking. It asked, and the summoning of the Estates-General in May 1789 conceded three propositions in one act - that royal authority alone was insufficient, that consent was required, and that a body existed entitled to give or withhold it. What the fiscal crisis could not supply is force or a programme, and those came from two further sources: subsistence stress, which mobilised crowds and is recorded as supplying the mass force behind elite constitutionalism though bread riots are not themselves revolutionary programmes, and the rights-argument, which supplied legitimacy rather than the grievance and converted a complaint about burdens into a claim that the burdens were unauthorised. The crisis could not be resolved inside the constitution because each available remedy required conceding the point at issue: taxing the exempt required abolishing exemption, and obtaining consent required recognising a body whose composition raised the question of where sovereignty lay.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a long list of grievances explains a revolution. Grievance was continuous; what changed was that a fiscal impasse forced the crown to create an institution, and that the men summoned to it already held an argument which turned grievance into a claim of right.

### Cumulative check A2 - the causal arrow, drawn under pressure

**Question:** A stem names both the American and the French Revolutions. Set out the three propositions about their relationship that you must get right, and the evidence for each.

**Model answer:** The first proposition is parallel derivation: the Enlightenment argument that authority must justify itself and that privilege is conventional rather than natural ran to both revolutions independently, and the standard chronology states this explicitly as a trap, recording that the causal arrow runs from ideas to both revolutions in parallel rather than sequentially from one to the other. The second is influence without causation: the American Revolution of 1775-83 influenced and encouraged the French Revolution of 1789-99, which are separate events roughly a decade apart, but did not cause it; the corresponding writing rule is to say influenced or supplied vocabulary and never caused. The third is difference of depth: the comparative demand is the difference in social depth, violence and the property or feudal question, and the instruction is explicit that the two must not be treated as one Atlantic revolution, because one overthrew an external imperial authority and transformed settler society comparatively little while the other attacked a domestic order of legal privilege and seigneurial obligation and consequently passed through monarchy, republic, Terror, Directory and empire. The single distinction that generates the rest of the comparison is that a revolution against an external authority can end when that authority withdraws, whereas a revolution against an internal order can end only when the beneficiaries of that order have been defeated, conciliated or destroyed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because both revolutions used the same vocabulary, the later must have copied the earlier. Shared vocabulary is evidence of a shared source, not of transmission between the two, and the opposite error of denying any kinship is equally costly because it cannot explain why the same language recurs.

## Block B - Rupture to republic, Lessons 4 to 8

### Cumulative check B1 - the five conversions of 1789, and what protected them

**Question:** State what each step of 1789 converted, and identify which single step made the others irreversible.

**Model answer:** The summoning of the Estates-General in May 1789 converted a revenue emergency into a constitutional question, because an assembly with a legal title to be in the room necessarily raises the questions of how it votes and whom it represents. The declaration of 17 June converted representation of an order into representation of the nation, and the sources record that sovereignty thereby begins shifting from king to nation. The Tennis Court Oath of 20 June converted a claim to represent into a claim to constitute, so that constitution-making became the revolutionary claim and the Assembly's dissolution became a public defeat rather than an administrative act. The storming of the Bastille on 14 July converted a legal claim into an irreversible public fact, and its significance is recorded as the fall of autocracy becoming a mass symbol, which is why the standard correction insists that its symbolic political value mattered more than the prisoner count. The August decrees and the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen converted a new authority into a standard binding on all authority, proclaiming rights, equality before law and citizenship, though the sources add that this transformed law and legitimacy while practice remained conflict-ridden. The step that made the others irreversible is 14 July, because the June acts changed what the Assembly was while July changed what could be done to it: after the capital had acted, dispersing the Assembly meant confronting the population of Paris rather than dismissing a body of lawyers.

**Misconception to avoid:** Opening an answer with the Bastille. The legal rupture preceded it by four weeks, and an answer that begins on 14 July has lost the sovereignty argument that 17 and 20 June contain.

### Cumulative check B2 - why the settlement of 1791 could not hold

**Question:** Give the three crises that destroyed the constitutional monarchy, state which were generated by the settlement's own achievements, and explain what each contributed to the outcome of 1792.

**Model answer:** The sources record that the phase gave way because fiscal, religious and war crises remained unresolved. The fiscal crisis was inherited: the Revolution had abolished the old fiscal order without building one that could fund the state, so the problem that opened the Revolution in 1789 was still open. The religious and the war crises were generated by the settlement's own achievements. Abolishing privilege necessarily reached the First Estate, whose privileges are recorded as what linked the Church to the old order, and any settlement of the Church's position raised the question of its authority, which is not a divisible or negotiable question; the result was a division running through the villages rather than between villages and lords, so that counter-revolution acquired a popular constituency it could not otherwise have had. Proclaiming universal principles and abolishing a monarch's prerogatives alarmed a Europe of monarchies, and the settlement had no means of making itself safe abroad. In 1792 the two combined: war supplied an external threat, the internal religious and political division supplied plausible domestic allies for that threat, and the conjunction dissolved the distinction between opposition and treason. Once that distinction had gone, defending a limited constitution became evidence of complicity, moderation became personally dangerous, and authority flowed to those willing to act most decisively - which is how the monarchy was abolished and the republic declared, with equal political rights extended for the first time to workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the collapse by the king's conduct. The sources record three structural crises, two of them produced by the settlement's own success, and a structural account is both better supported and stronger than a speculative one about an individual whose specific acts the sources do not record.

## Block C - Terror to transformation, Lessons 9 to 12

### Cumulative check C1 - the Terror, explained and costed

**Question:** Give the three causes of the Terror with the work each does, state the cost with its exact scope, and give the recorded verdict on why the phase ended.

**Model answer:** War emergency explains why extraordinary powers were claimed and widely accepted, and it cannot explain why coercion continued as the military position improved or why it was directed inward as much as outward. Ideological logic explains why coercion could be justified from inside revolutionary doctrine rather than against it, since a belief in direct democracy makes procedure look like obstruction of the sovereign people and the tradition the revolutionaries drew on ranked the common good above private interests; it cannot explain why particular individuals were targeted at particular moments. Factional struggle explains why the coercion consumed revolutionaries as well as counter-revolutionaries and why its intensity tracked political conflict rather than military danger; it cannot explain why an emergency apparatus existed for factions to capture. The cost is recorded as around seventeen thousand people tried and executed in some fourteen months, and the scope must be stated: that is a count of judicial executions, so deaths in prison, killings without formal trial and the far greater mortality of the civil war in the west lie outside it, and writing that seventeen thousand died in the Terror misreports the only figure the sources authorise. The recorded verdict on why the phase ended is that emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification, and any complete answer must also record that the same government produced the period's most advanced constitutional programme, since the Jacobin constitution carried universal male political rights, a right of insurrection and a duty on government to provide work or livelihood.

**Misconception to avoid:** Choosing between "necessary" and "insane". The sources refuse both as complete verdicts and require the explanation to carry three causes and to state the cost, because an account that omits either the emergency or the coercion has described half of the phenomenon.

### Cumulative check C2 - the transformation, graded

**Question:** Grade the Revolution's transformation of French society by layer, and state the single principle that explains why the grading falls as it does.

**Model answer:** At the level of legal status and political legitimacy the transformation was complete: privilege was abolished as a legal category, equality before the law replaced distinction by estate, sovereignty was relocated in the people and the term nation acquired its modern political meaning. For the peasantry it was substantial: feudal laws were annulled and the annulment survived even Napoleon's seizure of power, converting a relation of status and obligation into one of property and contract. For office and everyday life it was real: careers open to talent expanded in practice, and new language, symbols and forms of address remade daily conduct, which is how the Revolution reached a population that never read its documents. For urban workers it was partial: they obtained political voice, dated in the French context to 1792, but not economic equality, and the sources forbid any claim that full social equality was achieved. For women and colonial subjects it was least of all, since women were central to revolutionary action yet denied equal political citizenship and had their legal position narrowed by the Code, while slavery was abolished in the revolutionary period, reversed under Napoleon and only finally ended in 1848. The principle that explains the grading is the difference between an abolition, a provision and an admission. An abolition can be legislated once and becomes almost irreversible when the obligations have already lapsed in practice; a provision must be delivered continuously by a state with the resources and will to do so; and an admission of a new category of person to full citizenship requires the group already in possession of power to concede voluntarily, which no fiscal or military emergency compelled it to do.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring a redistribution of land from the annulment of feudal law. The sources record the extinction of seigneurial obligations and say nothing about landholding; the two are different claims with different evidence.

## Block D - Napoleon and legacy, Lessons 13 to 16

### Cumulative check D1 - why the political settlement fell and the social one did not

**Question:** Explain why a regime that ended representative government was accepted in 1799, and why the social settlement it guaranteed proved more durable than the political settlement it dismantled.

**Model answer:** By 1799 each major constituency had obtained a social gain and feared losing it more than it feared losing the republic. The peasantry had the annulment of feudal laws and feared their restoration; the bourgeoisie and notables had property rights, office by merit and status, and were threatened from both the Jacobin and the royalist directions; army officers had promotion on ability, which any restoration would have reversed; and the population generally had the end of privilege and a decade of instability behind it. The republic's committed constituency had been the sans-culotte movement and the Jacobin left, and both were defeated in the reaction, so the political settlement had no group prepared to fight for it. Meanwhile the Directory, being propertied, republican and unstable, had no available coalition and had to survive each crisis by force, which is how instability shifted power toward the army - the only institution that was national in reach, revolutionary in its own interest and able to enforce a decision. The resulting regime held de facto sovereignty, which is temporary until legitimised, and it legitimised itself through law, title and victory. The social settlement proved more durable because codification converted it into an institution: a written, systematic body of civil law preserving legal equality and anti-feudal reform, enforceable by ordinary courts, intelligible to officials with no revolutionary commitment, and adoptable by states elsewhere - which is why the sources identify codification as the mechanism that carried the settlement beyond France, and why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading 1799 as a counter-revolution imposed from outside. The regime's coalition was the army, the notables and a codifying state; its offer was the guarantee of the Revolution's social achievements; and the framework recorded for de facto authority explicitly allows obedience to rest on a strong following among the people as well as on force.

### Cumulative check D2 - the legacy, without a straight line

**Question:** State the shape of the Revolution's legacy, give the evidence that establishes the shape, and explain the boomerang by which the empire was undone by a principle it was spreading.

**Model answer:** The shape is a ladder rather than a line. The 1789 rights claim leads to the Terror of 1793-94, in which rights were invoked against rights; then to Napoleon between 1799 and 1815, where the social settlement was kept and the republic ended; then to the restoration of 1815; then to the renewed revolutions of 1830 and 1848; and then to nineteenth-century nationalism and constitutionalism - so that the principles survived through contestation, defeat and re-assertion rather than through uninterrupted institutional continuity. The evidence that establishes the shape is the abolition sequence: slavery was abolished in the revolutionary period, the abolition was reversed under Napoleon, and final French abolition came only in 1848, which is the same claim asserted, defeated and re-asserted in the same polity across more than half a century, and it was re-assertable precisely because the standard remained available in the society's own law. The boomerang follows from the same universality. The proposition that authority derives from the nation legitimised the regime in France, contains no geographical limit, and therefore says of any conquered territory that authority over it derives from its own nation; French rule abroad accordingly failed the test French rule at home passed, resistance became available in the conqueror's own language, and the principle could not be repudiated abroad without destroying the regime's basis at home. Two qualifications complete the account: the exported reforms could be separated from their author and remained in Europe after the armies withdrew, and the nationalism that resulted was also exclusionary and war-making.

**Misconception to avoid:** Claiming that the Revolution caused a later revolution or that Indian constitutional language descends from the French model. The sources require influence and shared vocabulary rather than causation, and record that routes of influence were indirect and layered.

## Block E - Judgement and synthesis, Lessons 17 to 18

### Cumulative check E1 - weighting without hedging

**Question:** The sources record that no single-cause theory is sufficient. Show how to convert that statement into a weighting rather than a hedge, and state the limitation attached to each cause.

**Model answer:** The conversion works by assigning each cause the job it can do and marking where it fails. Legal privilege supplies the target and explains why the demand was abolition rather than redistribution; its limitation is that the Third Estate was internally divided and bourgeois and peasant aims were not identical, so the target was shared while the programmes were not. Fiscal breakdown supplies the timing and the institutional opening, explaining why 1789 and not earlier; its limitation is that fiscal crisis alone had been survived before and became fatal only in combination with ideology and subsistence stress. Subsistence stress supplies the force that made elite constitutionalism survivable; its limitation is that bread riots are not by themselves revolutionary programmes, so hunger explains the availability of crowds and not the content of what they achieved. The rights-argument supplies legitimacy rather than the grievance, converting complaint into a claim that privilege was unauthorised; its limitation is that most peasants did not read the philosophes and transmission was mediated through pamphlets, clubs and lawyers, so the argument acted through intermediaries with a professional stake in it rather than on the population directly. The counterfactual test confirms the weighting: removing any one cause produces a serious crisis and none produces 1789, which is what "necessary but not sufficient" means when it is doing work. A hedge lists the causes and declines to relate them; a weighting states the function and the failure of each.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a balanced conclusion as evidence of judgement. A synthesis asserted without the distinction that generates it - different questions, leadership against causation, refusal of completeness, two distinct objects - is worth less than a clearly argued one-sided case.

### Cumulative check E2 - choosing the architecture

**Question:** For each of the five stem families on this topic, name the architecture required and the single sentence with which the answer should close.

**Model answer:** For a causes stem, the architecture is the four-function division - privilege supplies the target, bankruptcy the timing and opening, hunger the force, and rights-language the claim of illegitimacy - and the closing sentence is that the Revolution was not caused by ideas alone, since Enlightenment vocabulary converted a fiscal-agrarian crisis into a challenge to the legitimacy of privilege. For a phases stem, the architecture is periodisation logic in which each phase's social base and unresolved problem produce the next, and the closing sentence is that the phases record the successive dominance of the different programmes contained within a single legal category. For a transformation stem, the architecture is layer by layer with extent and durability, and the closing sentence is the graded verdict: complete at the level of legal status and political legitimacy, substantial for the peasantry through the destruction of feudal dues, only partial for urban workers, and least of all for women and colonial subjects. For a Napoleon stem, the architecture is the two ledgers - preserved and curtailed - with the item that crosses the line, and the closing sentence is that he was heir to the social revolution and betrayer of the political revolution, which is precisely why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789. For a relevance or comparison stem, the architecture is the categories made unavoidable, with the enduring principles carrying their own qualifications, the exclusions as the double relevance and the non-linear ladder as the mandatory shape, and the closing sentence is that the Revolution is relevant not as an institutional model but as the origin of the standard by which modern states are judged and, through its own exclusions, as the origin of the critique of that standard.

**Misconception to avoid:** Preparing one comprehensive answer and adapting it to whatever is asked. The five families require genuinely different internal structures, and the wrong architecture cannot be repaired by knowing more; choosing it is the first thirty seconds of the answer, not the last.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Six original questions, none reproduced from a past paper. Each model answer follows the examiner-grade pattern of claim, named evidence, analysis and qualification, and sits at or below its stated word ceiling. The scoring note after each identifies the specific thing that answer is demonstrating.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Examine why the abolition of privilege, rather than the redistribution of wealth, was the central demand of 1789.

### Model answer

Privilege in France was a legal category, not a level of income. The First and Second Estates held tax exemptions, office and land by status; the Third Estate, about ninety-five per cent of the population, carried the tax burden and political exclusion. The Declaration of August 1789 accordingly proclaimed equality before law and ruled that social distinctions may rest only upon public utility - a justification requirement no hereditary claim could meet. Because the grievance was legal, it could not be met by lightening burdens; only abolishing the categories answered it, and abolition is a constitutional act. The annulment of feudal laws followed the same logic and proved irreversible, since lapsed obligations cannot be re-collected village by village. The limit is exact: abolition was not redistribution. Workers and artisans obtained political voice without economic equality, and no claim of full social equality is defensible.

**Scoring note:** this answer is marked on the legal-against-economic distinction. Stating it in the first two sentences, and then using it to explain both why abolition succeeded and why economic inequality persisted, is the whole of the demand at ten marks.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Explain why the storming of the Bastille is treated as a turning point although the decisive legal acts of 1789 preceded it.

### Model answer

The decisive legal acts came first. On 17 June the Third Estate declared itself the National Assembly, and sovereignty began shifting from king to nation; on 20 June the Tennis Court Oath made constitution-making the revolutionary claim. Both were assertions of title by a body with no force of its own. The events of 14 July supplied what the June acts could not: they demonstrated that the state's coercive machinery could fail, that the capital's population would act, and that the Assembly's claim now had an armed constituency. Dispersing it thereafter meant confronting Paris rather than dismissing lawyers. This is why the fall of autocracy became a mass symbol, and why the fortress mattered as the embodiment of arbitrary detention rather than for its prisoner count. The qualification is that July changed enforceability, not legitimacy: the constitutional claim was already made, and an answer beginning on 14 July loses it.

**Scoring note:** this answer is marked on separating a claim of authority from the capacity to sustain it. Candidates who narrate the assault score poorly; candidates who state what each date converted score well.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Analyse the causes of the French Revolution, indicating the function performed by each.

### Model answer

The directive requires relation, not enumeration; four causes performed four different functions.

Legal privilege supplied the **target**. Tax exemptions, office and land followed estate membership rather than wealth, which is why the demand was abolition rather than reform. Its limitation is that the Third Estate was internally divided, and bourgeois and peasant aims were not identical.

Fiscal breakdown supplied the **timing and the opening**. A monarchy financially troubled and pushed toward bankruptcy could tax the exempt only by repudiating the legal order it guaranteed, or by asking; the Estates-General of May 1789 conceded that royal authority was insufficient, that consent was required, and that a body existed entitled to answer. Its limitation is decisive: fiscal crisis alone had been survived before, and became fatal only combined with ideology and subsistence stress.

Subsistence stress supplied the **force**. Tax burden and bread crisis mobilised the crowds behind elite constitutionalism. Its limitation is that bread riots are not themselves revolutionary programmes.

The rights-argument supplied the **claim of illegitimacy**, converting grievance into the assertion that privilege was unauthorised - a claim concessions cannot satisfy. Its limitation is that most peasants did not read the philosophes; transmission ran through pamphlets, clubs and lawyers.

Remove any one and a serious crisis follows, but not 1789: without the summons there is no legal vehicle; without hunger, an elite quarrel; without the legal structure, a negotiable revenue dispute; without the argument, petitions for relief.

Class, fiscal crisis, contingency, popular action and war all mattered; no single-cause theory suffices.

**Scoring note:** this answer is marked on the counterfactual paragraph. Assigning a function to each cause is competent; showing what each removal would have produced is what converts the list into an analysis.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Discuss the view that the Terror joined mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence in a single unstable package.

### Model answer

The formulation is exact, and each element is independently attested.

The **mass democracy** is real and usually omitted. The Jacobin constitution carried universal male political rights, a right of insurrection, and a duty on government to provide work or livelihood - a social right rare before the twentieth century, and far in advance of most nineteenth-century European constitutions. Equal political rights for non-propertied men date, in the French context, to this phase.

The **war emergency** is equally real. France faced invasion and an internal opposition that could be represented as its agents; the phase's achievements are recorded as the republic, wider male political rights and national defence.

The **coercive violence** must be stated precisely: around seventeen thousand people were tried and executed in some fourteen months. That figure counts judicial executions, so prison deaths, killings without trial and the far greater mortality of the western civil war lie outside it.

The instability lies in the combination. A doctrine of direct democracy makes procedure look like obstruction of the sovereign people; a war emergency makes opposition indistinguishable from treason; and factional struggle then directs the resulting apparatus against revolutionaries themselves. Explanation therefore requires three causes - emergency, ideology and faction - not one, and neither "necessary" nor "insane" is a complete verdict.

The qualification is the phase's own epitaph: emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification. That locates the failure in a period rather than a principle, and preserves what condemnation destroys - that popular sovereignty answers who rules, not what may be done.

**Scoring note:** this answer is marked on restoring the constitutional content that most answers omit. Without the three provisions of the Jacobin constitution, the "package" claim cannot be demonstrated and the answer becomes a narrative of coercion.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Evaluate the argument that Napoleon completed the social revolution while aborting the political revolution.

### Model answer

The argument is defensible and requires two ledgers kept apart.

**Preserved.** The abolition of feudal remnants stayed; the Code of 1804 preserved legal equality, anti-feudal reform and modern civil order; careers open to talent expanded in practice; and anti-feudal reform and the national idea spread across Europe. The mechanism matters more than the list: codification converted the settlement into a portable institution, enforceable by ordinary courts and adoptable even by hostile states. That is why the social order survived 1815 when the republic did not.

**Curtailed.** The republic was ended; empire and censorship returned the concentration of power; war became imperial conquest; and popular sovereignty was subordinated to military-political authority. Censorship identifies the mechanism precisely - equality before the law was retained while the means of invoking it against the government were removed.

**Why both.** The regime rested on the army, the notables and a codifying state, and on a rural population whose overriding fear was the return of seigneurial dues. It could therefore end the republic *because* it guaranteed the social settlement; the two facts are causally connected, not merely simultaneous. No constituency remained that would fight for the republic, the sans-culotte movement and Jacobin left having been defeated.

**Qualification.** The ledgers are not independent. The same Code narrowed married women's legal position, so transmission was selective and the instrument of preservation was also an instrument of reversal.

Heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political - which is precisely why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789.

**Scoring note:** this answer is marked on the causal link between the two ledgers. Listing preservations and curtailments produces a balance sheet; explaining that the first purchased the second produces a thesis, and the selective-transmission point prevents it from reading as an endorsement.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> "The French Revolution's enduring significance lies in the categories it made unavoidable rather than in the survival of its institutions." Critically examine.

### Model answer

The proposition is correct and needs proving in three moves.

**The categories.** Rights became a public standard - the Declaration of August 1789 proclaimed equality before law and rights of citizenship, creating a measure against which exclusion could be assessed. Citizenship replaced subjecthood in everyday language, symbols and forms of address. Popular sovereignty, asserted on 17 June 1789 and completed in 1792, made constituent power a permanent question. The nation acquired its modern political meaning, and sovereignty was relocated in the people. Secular public authority established that office and law need no religious sanction. Each remains a term in which legitimacy is claimed and contested: a regime may reject them, not ignore them.

**The institutions.** None survived. Constitutional monarchy fell in 1792, the republic in 1799, the empire in 1815, and the Bourbon monarchy returned. Only the social settlement persisted, carried by codification.

**The shape.** The legacy is a ladder: 1789 claim, Terror, Napoleon, restoration, 1830 and 1848, nineteenth-century nationalism and constitutionalism. The abolition sequence proves it: abolished, reversed under Napoleon, abolished finally in 1848. Principles travelled by defeat and re-assertion, not continuity.

**Critically.** Each category carries a cost. Popular sovereignty was invoked by the Terror; the nation's nationalism was exclusionary and war-making; anti-clericalism was itself coercive; codification narrowed married women's position. The relevance is therefore double - the universal claim, and the demonstration of how such claims get bounded.

Relevant not as a model but as the origin of the standard by which modern states are judged, and of its critique.

**Scoring note:** this answer is marked on the third move. Most answers supply categories and qualifications; the ladder, proved by a single claim asserted, defeated and re-asserted across half a century, is what demonstrates that the candidate understands how a legacy without institutional continuity actually works.

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | Error | Why it is wrong | Correct formulation |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "The Revolution happened because France was unequal" | Inequality was continuous; the legal form of it was the target | A legally constituted order of privilege, attacked by abolition rather than redistribution |
| 2 | Treating the Third Estate as a class | It is a legal category containing three incompatible programmes | Bourgeoisie, peasantry and urban artisans, with different aims and different outcomes |
| 3 | "Pamphlets toppled the old regime" | The sources explicitly forbid this | The Revolution rested on workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd as well as elite thinkers |
| 4 | Inventing a deficit figure, minister or named tax | The sources record bankruptcy without composition | A monarchy financially troubled and pushed toward bankruptcy; the Estates-General of May 1789 |
| 5 | "Hunger caused the Revolution" | Bread riots are not revolutionary programmes | Subsistence stress supplied the force behind elite constitutionalism |
| 6 | "The Enlightenment caused the Revolution" | Ideas cannot explain date, force or direction | Ideas supplied legitimacy, not the grievance |
| 7 | "America 1776 caused France 1789" | The standard chronology records this as a trap | Ideas ran to both in parallel; America influenced and encouraged France, and its war was a fiscal cause |
| 8 | Treating the two as one "Atlantic revolution" | Flattens the difference in social depth | External authority against internal order; the depth difference generates everything else |
| 9 | Opening an answer with 14 July | Loses the June sovereignty argument | 17 June, 20 June, then 14 July as enforcement |
| 10 | Explaining the Bastille by prisoner numbers | Its value was symbolic and political | The fall of autocracy became a mass symbol |
| 11 | Merging the August decrees and the Declaration | They do different work and are separately dated | 4 August decree abolishes the feudal system; the Declaration states the standard |
| 12 | "The Declaration ended inequality" | It transformed law and legitimacy only | Practice remained conflict-ridden; it created a measurable standard |
| 13 | Claiming universal suffrage in 1791 | Active citizens were about 4 million of 28 million | Men over 25 paying three days' wages in tax; all women passive |
| 14 | Blaming the collapse of 1791 on the king alone | Three structural crises are recorded | Fiscal, religious and war crises unresolved |
| 15 | Writing "a measure against the Church" | The instrument can be named and dated | Civil Constitution of the Clergy, 12 July 1790; oath of November 1790; jurors and non-jurors |
| 16 | Merging the republic (1792) and the regicide (1793) | Separate markers, separate years and functions | Republic creates; regicide makes irreversible |
| 17 | Treating the Jacobins as extremists from the outset | They were a club founded in 1789 that became radical | Founded 1789, increasingly radical, instituted the Terror, closed 1794 |
| 18 | "17,000 died in the Terror" | The figure counts judicial executions | About 17,000 tried and executed in some 14 months; total violence was greater |
| 19 | One cause for the Terror | The handling rule requires three | War emergency, ideological logic, factional struggle |
| 20 | Omitting the Jacobin constitution | It is what makes the phase a programme | Universal male political rights, right of insurrection, work or livelihood |
| 21 | Dismissing the Directory in a clause | It explains why power was available to seize | Propertied, republican, unstable; instability shifted power toward the army |
| 22 | Treating the army as counter-revolutionary | Advancement by merit is a revolutionary demand | Order, victory and advancement by merit |
| 23 | "The Revolution abolished slavery" | Abolition was reversed under Napoleon | Abolished, reversed, finally abolished in 1848 |
| 24 | Claiming full social equality | Workers and artisans never obtained economic equality | Political voice without economic equality |
| 25 | Inferring land redistribution from the annulment of feudal law | The sources record obligations, not landholding | Seigneurial obligations extinguished |
| 26 | Dating the Code to the 1790s | It is 1804 | Code and imperial title both 1804 |
| 27 | "Napoleon restored the old order" | He preserved anti-feudal legal change | He ended the republic, not the legal transformation |
| 28 | "Napoleon ended all revolutionary change" | The opposite error, equally penalised | Heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political |
| 29 | Listing the Code as an achievement without the mechanism | Misses how the legacy travelled | Codification carried the settlement beyond France |
| 30 | Substituting a campaign narrative for analysis | Battles earn marks only as evidence for a mechanism | Continental System 1806, Peninsular War 1808-14, Russia 1812 - one clause each |
| 31 | Straight line from 1789 to modern democracy | Refused by the mandatory ladder | Contestation, defeat and re-assertion |
| 32 | Asserting Indian constitutional descent from the French model | Routes were indirect and layered | A shared modern vocabulary, adapted locally |
| 33 | Quoting Rousseau, Robespierre or the Declaration from memory | Unverified quotation is forbidden | Paraphrase, or use only sourced wording |
| 34 | Attaching an unverifiable historian's name to a causal position | An invented attribution is a visible and needless risk | Name the four checkable readings; present the causal debate as positions |

## Corrective drills

### Drill 1 - the inequality opening

Rewrite "France was unequal, so there was a revolution" so that it explains the form of the demand. Target: a legally constituted order of privilege in which exemption, office and jurisdiction followed estate membership, so that only abolition of the categories could answer the grievance.

### Drill 2 - the Third Estate

List the three social forces inside the Third Estate with what each wanted and what each obtained. Target: bourgeoisie - end of privilege, office by merit, property rights, obtaining gains in law, status and institutional power; peasantry - relief from dues, rents and feudal burdens, obtaining major anti-feudal gains; urban workers - cheap bread, political voice, social equality, obtaining the second only.

### Drill 3 - the date question

State why the crisis became terminal in 1789 rather than earlier. Target: Louis XVI inherited an empty treasury in 1774 and a debt swollen past two billion livres by the American war; bankruptcy forced the summons of 5 May 1789, the first Estates-General since 1614; the summons conceded consent and created a rival authority; hunger supplied the crowds; and the argument was already held by the men summoned.

### Drill 4 - the function of ideas

State the contribution of the Enlightenment in one sentence with its limitation attached. Target: ideas supplied legitimacy rather than the grievance, converting complaint into a claim of right, but reached the population through pamphlets, clubs and lawyers rather than by direct readership.

### Drill 5 - the causal arrow

Draw the relationship between the American and French Revolutions in three lines. Target: ideas to both in parallel; America influenced and encouraged France; America did not cause France.

### Drill 6 - the six weeks

Give the five conversions of 1789 in order without narrating any event. Target: order into nation; representing into constituting; legal claim into public fact; new authority into binding standard; and a standard outliving every government built on it.

### Drill 7 - the Bastille correction

State what made the Bastille significant, and what did not. Target: the symbolic political value of capturing the physical form of arbitrary detention; not the number of prisoners.

### Drill 8 - reading the Declaration as engineering

Explain what "social distinctions can be based only upon public utility" abolishes and what it installs. Target: it abolishes distinction by estate and installs a justification requirement that hereditary privilege cannot meet - which is why it can coexist with, and still condemn, the later exclusions.

### Drill 9 - the three equalities

Attach each stage of the equality sequence to its actors. Target: equality before the law to the men of 1789; political equality or universal suffrage to the Jacobins and the nineteenth-century Republicans; social equality to socialists.

### Drill 10 - the three crises

Name the three crises that destroyed the constitutional monarchy and say which were self-generated. Target: fiscal, religious and war; the religious and the war crises were produced by the settlement's own achievements, and only the fiscal one was inherited.

### Drill 11 - the religious rupture

Explain why the Church question had no available compromise. Target: leaving its position intact contradicts the abolition of privilege; dissolving its public role reaches into every parish; absorbing the clergy into the state demands an allegiance their office denies - and a quarrel about ultimate authority is not divisible.

### Drill 12 - the radicalisation mechanism

State the five steps by which war radicalises a revolution. Target: opposition becomes treason; emergency powers become defensible; moderation becomes dangerous; authority flows to the decisive; the emergency's institutions outlive the emergency.

### Drill 13 - the Terror, costed

Give the Terror's cost with its scope in one sentence. Target: around seventeen thousand people tried and executed in some fourteen months, a count of judicial executions that excludes prison deaths, killings without trial and the far greater mortality of the western civil war.

### Drill 14 - the Jacobin constitution

Give its three recorded provisions and say why they matter. Target: universal male political rights, the right of insurrection, and a duty on government to provide work or livelihood - which make the phase a constitutional programme rather than only an emergency.

### Drill 15 - why the Directory matters

Explain the Directory's role in producing Napoleon in three clauses. Target: propertied, so cut off from the republic's popular base; republican, so unable to compromise with royalism; unstable, so dependent on force - and the supplier of force acquires the casting vote.

### Drill 16 - de facto sovereignty

Name the three instruments by which de facto power was legitimised after 1799 and match each to its output. Target: law to the Code of 1804; title to the imperial dignity of 1804; victory to the campaigns - and note that only the third requires continuous renewal.

### Drill 17 - the two ledgers

Reconstruct the preserved and curtailed columns, then name the item that crosses between them. Target: preserved - feudal abolition, legal rationalisation, careers open to talent, exported reform; curtailed - republic ended, empire and censorship, conquest, sovereignty subordinated; crossing - the Code narrowed married women's legal position.

### Drill 18 - the transmission mechanism

Explain why codification mattered more than conquest for the Revolution's European legacy. Target: conquest lasts as long as occupation; codification converts the settlement into a portable institution that hostile states can adopt and ordinary courts can enforce.

### Drill 19 - the boomerang

State in two sentences how popular sovereignty undid the empire that spread it. Target: the proposition that authority derives from the nation contains no geographical limit, so it condemns French rule abroad by the test that legitimises it at home; and it cannot be repudiated abroad without destroying the regime's basis in France.

### Drill 20 - the ladder

Reproduce the non-linear legacy ladder and give the single best proof of its shape. Target: 1789 claim, Terror, Napoleon, 1815 restoration, 1830 and 1848, nineteenth-century nationalism and constitutionalism; proved by abolition, reversal under Napoleon, and final abolition in 1848.

### Drill 21 - the graded verdict

Answer "how far did it transform society" in one sentence with four grades. Target: complete for legal status and political legitimacy; substantial for the peasantry; only partial for urban workers; least for women and colonial subjects.

### Drill 22 - the India caution

State the Indian linkage in a form that cannot be attacked. Target: a shared modern vocabulary of liberty, equality, citizenship and rights, strengthened by the French revolutionary tradition, with routes of influence indirect and layered - never a genealogy, never a claim of causation.

### Drill 23 - weighting against hedging

Convert "no single-cause theory is sufficient" into an analytical statement. Target: assign each cause its function - target, timing, force, claim of illegitimacy - and attach each one's recorded limitation, then apply the counterfactual test.

### Drill 24 - choosing the architecture

Given a stem, name the architecture in one line. Target: causes to four functions; phases to periodisation logic; transformation to the graded verdict; Napoleon to the two ledgers; relevance and comparison to the categories, the ladder and the exclusions.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

```text
  1774              Louis XVI accedes; empty treasury, war debt above
                    2 billion livres
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1789   5 MAY      Estates-General meets at Versailles (first since 1614)
                    fiscal crisis opens the old regime to challenge
         17 JUNE    Third Estate declares itself the National Assembly
                    sovereignty begins shifting from king to nation
         20 JUNE    Tennis Court Oath (Mirabeau, Abbé Sieyès)
                    constitution-making becomes the revolutionary claim
         14 JULY    Bastille stormed
                    the fall of autocracy becomes a mass symbol
         JULY-AUG   The Great Fear: chateaux attacked, records of
                    manorial dues burnt
         4 AUGUST   Decree abolishing the feudal system of obligations
                    and taxes; tithes abolished; Church lands confiscated
         AUGUST     Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen
                    rights, equality before law and citizenship proclaimed
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1790   12 JULY    Civil Constitution of the Clergy
         NOVEMBER   Clerical oath required; jurors and non-jurors split
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1791              Constitution of 1791: constitutional monarchy,
                    active and passive citizens
                    the old regime is legally broken; instability continues
         OCT        Legislative Assembly sits (to September 1792)
                    Olympe de Gouges, Declaration of the Rights of Woman
                    and Citizen
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1792   APRIL      War declared on Prussia and Austria
         10 AUGUST  Tuileries stormed; royal family imprisoned;
                    universal male suffrage at 21
         21 SEPT    Convention abolishes the monarchy; republic declared
                    equal political rights extended to non-propertied men
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1793   21 JAN     Louis XVI executed for treason, Place de la Concorde
         MARCH      Revolutionary Tribunal; revolt in the Vendée begins
         6 APRIL    Committee of Public Safety created
         24 JUNE    Constitution of 1793 (Year I) adopted - then suspended
         23 AUGUST  Levée en masse
         17 SEPT    Law of Suspects
  1793-94           Reign of Terror
                    ~17,000 tried and executed in some 14 months
                    Girondins and Montagnards divide the Convention
  1794   9 THERM    9 Thermidor Year II = 27 July 1794: Robespierre
                    overthrown; guillotined 28 July
                    Convention abolishes slavery in the colonies
                    The Jacobin club is closed
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1795   AUGUST     Constitution of Year III: two councils, five Directors
  1795-99           Directory: retreat from Terror; civilian rule weakens
                    instability shifts power toward the army
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1799   9-10 NOV   Coup of 18 Brumaire; Directory falls; Consulate
                    a new authoritarian-consolidating phase begins
  1801              Concordat with Pope Pius VII
  1802              Slavery restored in the French colonies
  1804   21 MARCH   Civil Code promulgated
         18 MAY     Napoleon proclaimed Emperor (crowned 2 December)
                    legal equality survives, the republic does not
  1806   21 NOV     Berlin Decree: the Continental System
  1808-14           Peninsular War
  1812              Invasion of Russia
  1814   6 APRIL    Abdication; Bourbon restoration under Louis XVIII
  1815   20 MARCH   The Hundred Days begin
         18 JUNE    Waterloo
                    the Napoleonic phase ends; the legacy remains
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  1848              Final French abolition of slavery
                    the revolutionary claim, defeated and re-asserted
  1946              French women win the vote
```
