## The four phases, in one table

| Phase | Dominant coalition | What it achieved | Why it gave way |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1789-91** | ⚠️ Bourgeois-led constitutionalism with peasant and crowd pressure | ✅ End of legal privilege; the Declaration; constitutional monarchy | ✅ Fiscal, religious and war crises unresolved |
| **1792-94** | ⚠️ Jacobin republicanism resting on sans-culotte Paris | ✅ Republic; wider male political rights; national defence | ✅ Emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification |
| **1795-99** | ⚠️ Propertied republicans | ✅ Retreat from Terror | ✅ Instability shifted power toward the army |
| **1799-1815** | ⚠️ Army, notables and codifying state | ✅ Napoleonic Code; careers open to talent; anti-feudal reform exported | ✅ Empire, censorship and conquest ended republican liberty |

## The two settlements

| | Social settlement | Political settlement |
|---|---|---|
| **Content** | Privilege abolished; feudal law annulled; equality before the law; careers open to talent; property secured | Sovereignty in the people; a republic; representative institutions; liberty of person and press |
| **Constituency after 1795** | Peasantry, bourgeoisie, notables, promoted officers - a broad coalition | The defeated sans-culotte movement and Jacobin left |
| **Fate in 1799** | Guaranteed | Dismantled |
| **Fate in 1815** | Survives; the Restoration cannot undo it | The Bourbon monarchy returns |
| **Mechanism of survival** | Codification - a portable, court-enforceable institution | None; recovery required renewed political struggle |
| **Verdict** | Napoleon is its heir | Napoleon is its gravedigger |

## The American and French Revolutions compared

| Dimension | American Revolution | French Revolution |
|---|---|---|
| Core period | ✅ 1775-1783 | ✅ 1789-1799; Napoleonic phase to 1815 |
| Immediate conflict | ✅ Colonies against British imperial authority | ✅ Society and nation against the Bourbon old regime |
| Main claim | ✅ No taxation without representation; independence | ✅ End privilege; citizenship, popular sovereignty, equality before law |
| Social transformation | ✅ More limited within settler society | ✅ Directly attacked feudal privilege and the estate order |
| Constitutional result | ✅ Durable federal republic and written constitution | ✅ Monarchy, republic, Terror, Directory, Napoleonic empire |
| Key contradiction | ✅ Liberty coexisted with slavery and exclusion | ✅ Universal rights coexisted with gender, colonial and political exclusions |
| World-historical effect | ✅ Model of anti-colonial constitutional revolution | ✅ Wider transformation of citizenship, nationalism and social hierarchy |

⚠️ The generating distinction: a revolution against an **external** authority can end when that authority withdraws; a revolution against an **internal** order can end only when the beneficiaries of that order are defeated, conciliated or destroyed.

## Argument map: the Terror

```text
  CLAIM A: necessary defence in a wartime emergency
      evidence: invasion; internal opposition representable as its agents;
                the phase achieved the republic, wider rights, national defence
      cannot explain: why coercion continued as the danger receded;
                      why it was directed inward and consumed revolutionaries

  CLAIM B: coercive excess and political narrowing
      evidence: ~17,000 tried and executed in some 14 months (judicial only);
                the constitution's own provisions did not restrain it
      cannot explain: why the same movement produced universal male political
                      rights, a right of insurrection and a duty to provide
                      work or livelihood

  RESOLUTION: not a midpoint but a THREE-CAUSE explanation
      war emergency      -> why powers were claimed and accepted
      ideological logic  -> why coercion was justified from INSIDE the doctrine
      factional struggle -> why it consumed revolutionaries and tracked
                            political rather than military danger

  VERDICT: "emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification"
           - a judgement with a date, not an adjective

  RESIDUAL: whether the Revolution could have survived militarily without
            the apparatus remains an open counterfactual
```

## Argument map: Napoleon

```text
  THE QUESTION CONTAINS TWO OBJECTS
            |
   +--------+--------+
   |                 |
 SOCIAL           POLITICAL
 SETTLEMENT       SETTLEMENT
   |                 |
 preserved,       ended, and
 codified,        replaced by
 exported         empire and censorship
   |                 |
   +--------+--------+
            |
   HEIR to one, GRAVEDIGGER of the other
            |
   AND THE TWO ARE CAUSALLY LINKED:
   guaranteeing the first is what made
   reversing the second acceptable
            |
   ONE ITEM CROSSES THE LINE:
   the Code narrowed married women's legal position
   -> transmission was SELECTIVE
            |
   CONSEQUENCE CLAUSE:
   "which is precisely why the Restoration
    could not simply undo 1789"
```

## Argument map: enduring relevance

```text
  THE WRONG THESIS                    THE RIGHT THESIS
  1789 -> modern democracy            categories made UNAVOIDABLE
  by institutional descent            (citizen, rights, popular
                                       sovereignty, nation)
        |                                       |
   refuted by:                          proved by:
   - constitutional monarchy fell 1792   - each category still the term
   - republic fell 1799                    in which legitimacy is claimed
   - empire fell 1815                    - the social settlement carried
   - Bourbon monarchy restored             abroad by CODIFICATION
        |                                       |
        +------------------+--------------------+
                           |
              THE LADDER (mandatory qualification)
              1789 claim -> Terror -> Napoleon -> 1815
              -> 1830 / 1848 -> 19th-c. nationalism
                           |
              PROOF OF THE LADDER'S SHAPE
              slavery: abolished -> reversed -> 1848
                           |
              THE DOUBLE RELEVANCE
              the universal claim + the demonstration
              of how such claims get bounded
                           |
              CLOSING VERDICT
              "relevant not as an institutional model but as
               the origin of the standard by which modern states
               are judged - and, through its own exclusions,
               as the origin of the critique of that standard"
```

## Answer-writing spine

```text
  1. IDENTIFY THE STEM FAMILY
     causes | phases | transformation | Napoleon | relevance or comparison

  2. OPEN WITH A QUALIFIED THESIS
     concede the strongest counter-point in the same sentence

  3. BODY: USE THE MATCHING ARCHITECTURE
     causes         -> target / timing and opening / force / claim
     phases         -> each phase's base, achievement and unresolved problem
     transformation -> layer by layer, extent and durability
     Napoleon       -> two ledgers, plus the item that crosses
     relevance      -> categories with qualifications, exclusions, ladder

  4. INSERT THE MANDATORY QUALIFICATION
     15 marks -> one full paragraph on women, slavery or workers
     20 marks -> add comparison or legacy, plus historiographical caution

  5. CLOSE WITH A GRADED VERDICT THAT ANSWERS THE EXACT WORDING
     not a summary; a judgement carrying its own qualification

  6. CHECK THE CONTROLS BEFORE SUBMITTING
     Code = 1804 | abolition reversed, final 1848 | no universal suffrage
     1789 | 17,000 TRIED AND EXECUTED, not died | influenced, not caused
     | no unverified quotation | no invented historian
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The old regime as a legal order

- Three estates: clergy, nobility, and a Third Estate of **about 95 per cent** of the population.
- First Estate: privilege and **tax exemptions**; those privileges **linked the Church to the old order**.
- Second Estate: privilege, **office and much land**; beneficiary of the feudal hierarchy.
- Third Estate: carried the **tax burden** and the **political exclusion**.
- The decisive point: privilege was **legally, not merely economically, entrenched**; the target of 1789 was therefore **legal privilege, not wealth as such**.
- Three forces inside one legal category - peasantry (rent, dues, taxes, bad harvests → anti-feudal anger); bourgeoisie (wealth without matching status or power → institutional leadership); urban workers and artisans (long hours, poverty, secret societies, unrest → mass pressure from below).
- The sources' own caution: the Third Estate was **internally divided**; bourgeois and peasant aims were not identical.
- Rousseau, *Discourse on the Origin of Inequality* (1755): natural inequality against **conventional inequality** - privileges some enjoy to the exclusion of others.
- Raphael (1976): the revolutionaries demanded the removal of **arbitrary privilege**, such as that confining political rights to the rich and the well-born.

## 2. Why 1789 - the fiscal and subsistence conjuncture

- The crisis is attributed to **social inequality + fiscal disorder + intellectual challenge**.
- The monarchy was **financially troubled and ultimately pushed toward bankruptcy**.
- **Estates-General, May 1789**; fiscal crisis **opens the old regime to challenge**.
- Fiscal breakdown **opened the political door** and explains why 1789 and not earlier; but fiscal crisis alone **had been survived before**.
- *The struggle to survive*: **tax burden and bread crisis**; subsistence stress supplied **the mass force behind elite constitutionalism**; but bread riots are **not themselves revolutionary programmes**.
- The summons conceded three things at once: royal authority insufficient; consent required; a rival site of authority created.
- The defensible fiscal particulars are the accession of **1774**, the empty treasury, the **Versailles** court, the American war adding over a **billion livres** to a debt already above **2 billion livres**, and **10 per cent** interest.

## 3. Ideas, transmission and the Atlantic frame

- Ideas supplied **legitimacy, not the grievance**; they convert grievance into a **claim of right**.
- Transmission ran through **pamphlets, clubs and lawyers**; most peasants did not read the philosophes.
- Debate: Enlightenment delegitimisation against explosive material crisis; synthesis - **ideas and crisis interacted**.
- Rousseau is recorded as **the source of inspiration of the French Revolution (1789)**; *Social Contract* (1762) defines the citizen as **free and autonomous**, entitled to participate in all decisions binding on all; he gave **prominence to the common good over private interests**.
- Citizenship redefined in seventeenth-century England, popularised after **1688**, popular during the **American Revolution (1776)**, and reaching its **zenith with 1789** and the Declaration, which **echoes Rousseau's views**.
- **American 1775-83** and **French 1789-99** are separate events about a decade apart; America **influenced and encouraged** France, and did not cause it; the arrow runs from ideas to **both in parallel**.
- Comparative demand: difference in **social depth, violence and the property or feudal question**; never one "Atlantic revolution".
- Natural-rights doctrine is recorded as **ambiguous**, "capable of immense variations", and interpretable to uphold the values of the class invoking it.

## 4. The sequence of 1789

- **1774** Louis XVI accedes; **5 May 1789** Estates-General meets at Versailles, the first since **1614**, with 300 deputies from each privileged order and 600 from the Third Estate; **17 June** Third Estate declares itself the National Assembly - sovereignty begins shifting from king to nation; **20 June** Tennis Court Oath, led by **Mirabeau** and **Abbé Sieyès**, author of *What is the Third Estate?* - constitution-making becomes the revolutionary claim; **14 July** Bastille, with only seven prisoners inside - the fall of autocracy becomes a mass symbol; **4 August 1789** the decree abolishing the feudal system of obligations and taxes, abolishing tithes and confiscating Church lands; **August 1789** the **Declaration**.
- The Declaration proclaimed **equality before law and rights of citizenship**; verified wording: "Men are born and remain free and equal in rights. Social distinctions can be based only upon public utility"; and "The aim of every political association is the preservation of natural and imprescriptible rights of man. These rights are liberty, property, security and resistance to oppression."
- Corrections: the Bastille mattered for **symbolic political value, not prisoner count**; the Declaration **transformed law and legitimacy** but did not end inequality, and practice remained conflict-ridden.
- 17 and 20 June, with 1792, establish **popular sovereignty** and make **constituent power** a permanent question.
- The five conversions: order → nation; representing → constituting; legal claim → public fact; new authority → binding standard; a standard that outlives every government built on it.

## 5. Popular agency

- The Revolution rested **not only on elite thinkers but on workers, artisans, peasants and the Paris crowd**; never write it as if pamphlets alone toppled the old regime.
- Peasant aims: relief from **dues, rents and feudal burdens**; achievement: **major anti-feudal gains**.
- Urban worker aims: **cheap bread, political voice, social equality**; they helped the radical phase; **economic equality remained incomplete**.
- **1792** is when workers, peasants and other non-propertied classes **first gained equal political rights** in the French context.
- Four functions of popular force: protection, acceleration, radicalisation, extension of citizenship.
- Rural action was **decentralised, self-completing and satisfiable**; which is why it stuck and why the countryside became conservative once satisfied.
- The rising of summer 1789 is the **Great Fear**: rumours of brigands hired by the lords, **chateaux** attacked, grain looted and - decisively - the **records of manorial dues burnt**, which is what made the obligations unenforceable before they were abolished.

## 6. The constitutional monarchy and its three crises

- **1791**: the old regime is **legally broken but instability continues**.
- The **Constitution of 1791**: separation of powers, a king with a **veto**, an indirectly elected assembly, **active citizens** (men over 25 paying three days' wages in tax, about **4 million of 28 million**) against **passive citizens** (the remaining men and **all women**). The assembly elected under it, the **Legislative Assembly**, sat from **October 1791** to **September 1792**.
- Phase base: bourgeois-led constitutionalism with peasant and crowd pressure; achievements: end of legal privilege, the Declaration, constitutional monarchy.
- Gave way because **fiscal, religious and war crises** remained unresolved - two of them generated by the settlement's own achievements.
- Citizenship was **gendered and property-conditioned by turns**; contested, not conferred once.
- The equality sequence: **equality before the law** (the men of 1789) → **political equality** or universal suffrage (the Jacobins and later Republicans) → **social equality** (socialists).
- Engels, *Anti-Dühring* (1878): equality "reduced itself to **bourgeois equality before the law**; ... bourgeois property was proclaimed as one of the essential rights of man".

## 7. The religious rupture

- Clerical **privilege and tax exemptions** linked the Church to the old order; the Revolution **attacked its ideological authority** as well as its privileges.
- Principle established: **secular public authority** - public office and law need **no religious sanction**.
- Recorded qualification: revolutionary anti-clericalism was **itself coercive**; **secularism has plural later forms** - so no genealogy from the French model to Indian secularism.
- Three possible settlements of the Church's position - leave, dissolve, absorb - and each fails.
- Consequence: the first revolutionary measure to divide **the villages internally**, giving counter-revolution a popular constituency.
- Burke (1729-97) criticised the Revolution for **abstract rights of man**, glorifying 1688 for reasserting customary rights in **Magna Carta (1215)** and the **Petition of Right (1628)**.
- **Civil Constitution of the Clergy, 12 July 1790**; the **oath** of **November 1790**; the split into **jurors** and **non-jurors**; the **Concordat of 1801** closed the rupture.

## 8. War, the republic and the regicide

- Causal chain: 1789 rupture → **war + counter-revolutionary fear + radicalisation** → Jacobin republic → Directory → Napoleon.
- **1792**: monarchy abolished; republic declared; the Revolution turns more radical. **1793**: regicide. The two are separate markers.
- The sources classify 1789-91 as **reform** and 1792 as the **shift from reform to revolution**.
- Jacobins: a political club **founded in 1789**, named from the former **convent of St Jacob** in Paris; members drawn from small shopkeepers, artisans, servants and wage-earners; leader **Maximilian Robespierre**; the striped-trouser group became the **sans-culottes**. The club became increasingly radical, **instituted the Terror** and was **closed in 1794**. They believed in **direct democracy** and, **fearing danger to the Revolution, adopted harsh measures**.
- The 1792 sequence: war declared on **Prussia and Austria** in **April 1792**; the **Marseillaise** of **Roget de L'Isle**; the **Tuileries** stormed on **10 August**; the franchise opened to **all men of 21**; the **Convention** abolished the monarchy on **21 September 1792**; **Louis XVI** executed for **treason** on **21 January 1793** at the **Place de la Concorde**, **Marie Antoinette** shortly after.
- Radicalisation mechanism: treason-conflation → defensible emergency powers → dangerous moderation → authority to the decisive → institutions outliving the emergency.
- Military necessity and the widening of citizenship are connected: mass defence is hard to demand from half-citizens.

## 9. The Jacobin republic and the Terror

- The **Constitution of 1793**, or **Constitution of Year I**, adopted **24 June 1793** and then **suspended**: **universal male political rights**; the **right of insurrection**; government **must provide work or livelihood**.
- The machinery: **Revolutionary Tribunal** (**March 1793**); **Committee of Public Safety** (**6 April 1793**); **levée en masse** (**23 August 1793**); **Law of Suspects** (**17 September 1793**); the **Vendée** insurgency from **March 1793**; price and wage maximum, rationing, the **pain d'égalité**, **Citoyen** and **Citoyenne**, churches closed; the **guillotine**, named after **Dr Guillotin**.
- The Convention divided between **Girondins** (moderate republicans) and **Montagnards** (radical Jacobins) - which is why the coercion consumed revolutionaries.
- Achievements of 1792-94: republic, wider male political rights, national defence.
- Cost: **around 17,000 tried and executed in some 14 months** - a count of **judicial executions**; prison deaths, killings without trial and the far greater mortality of the western civil war lie outside it.
- Handling rule: **war emergency + ideological logic + factional struggle**; refuse both "necessary" and "insane"; state the cost.
- The Terror joined **mass democracy, war emergency and coercive violence in one unstable package**.
- Why the phase gave way: **emergency coercion outlived the emergency's justification**.
- Popular sovereignty was **invoked by the Terror as well as by liberals**, and does not by itself guarantee liberty.

## 10. The retreat and the Directory

- **9 Thermidor Year II = 27 July 1794**: Robespierre overthrown and guillotined the next day, **28 July 1794**; the **Thermidorian Reaction** follows and the Jacobin club is **closed in 1794**. The next phase achieved the **retreat from Terror**.
- The **Constitution of Year III**, approved **August 1795**, denied the vote to the non-propertied, created **two elected legislative councils** and a **Directory** of **five** members - an anti-one-man-executive design that produced a military dictator within four years.
- **Directory, 1795-99**: base **propertied republicans**; **instability and fatigue weaken civilian rule**; **instability shifts power toward the army**.
- *Propertied* narrowed the political nation again; *republicans* foreclosed restoration; *instability* meant no available coalition - so survival required force, and the supplier of force acquired the casting vote.
- The army wanted **order, victory and advancement by merit**, and became the **vehicle for authoritarian consolidation**.
- **De facto sovereignty**: the actual sovereign differs from the legal one; Napoleon in France is a recorded example, with Cromwell and the Bolsheviks; the condition is temporary until the holder **evolves means to legitimise** it; obedience rests on **sheer force or a strong following among the people**.

## 11. Boundaries of the new citizenship

- **"Did women have a revolution?"** - the gendered limits of citizenship; women were **central to revolutionary action yet denied equal political citizenship**; the **Code narrowed married women's legal position**, so transmission was **selective**.
- Women: about **sixty women's clubs**, the **Society of Revolutionary and Republican Women**; **Olympe de Gouges (1748-1793)** wrote the **Declaration of the Rights of Woman and Citizen** in **1791**, was tried by the **Convention** for **treason** and executed in 1793; women's clubs were **closed during the Terror**; girls' schooling, free marriage, civil registration and **legal divorce** were granted; the **vote came only in 1946**.
- Slavery: **universal rights collided with a colonial slave economy** in **Martinique, Guadeloupe** and **San Domingo (Saint-Domingue)**; the **National Assembly debated and passed nothing**; the **Convention abolished slavery in 1794**; **Napoleon restored it in 1802**; **final French abolition came in 1848**. Never say the Revolution abolished slavery permanently.
- Colonial subjects were governed without consent by a state whose founding text made the preservation of natural rights the aim of every political association.
- Workers and artisans obtained **political voice but not economic equality**; never claim full social equality.
- Four defences of the boundary - **redefinition, deferral, necessity, capacity** - and **deferral** is what made the contradiction survivable.
- Evaluate a period **by the standard it declared for itself**; the exclusions were contested at the time.
- The relevance is **double**: the universal claim, and the demonstration of how such claims get bounded in practice.

## 12. The social and legal transformation

- **Feudal laws were annulled**, and many revolutionary gains **survived Napoleon's seizure of power**.
- Bourgeoisie: gains in **law, status and institutional power**; **careers open to talent expanded in practice**.
- *The Revolution and everyday life*: **new language, symbols and forms of address** remade everyday life - social transformation beyond high politics, and the channel by which the Revolution reached a non-reading population.
- The Revolution gave the term **nation** its **modern political meaning** and **relocated sovereignty in the people**; the derived nationalism was also **exclusionary and war-making**.
- Graded verdict: **complete** for legal status and political legitimacy; **substantial** for the peasantry; **only partial** for urban workers; **least of all** for women and colonial subjects.
- The generating principle: **abolitions** complete and endure; **provisions** must be continuously supplied; **admissions** to citizenship require voluntary concession.
- The sources record extinguished obligations, **not** land redistribution.

## 13. Brumaire and the legitimising of power

- **Coup of 18 Brumaire, 9-10 November 1799**: the **Directory** falls, the **Consulate** is established with Napoleon as **First Consul**, and the Revolution enters a new **authoritarian-consolidating phase**.
- The 1799-1815 coalition: **army, notables and codifying state**.
- Every alternative was disqualified - restoration by the regicide and rural fear; Jacobinism by the reaction; the crowd by lack of machinery; the propertied republicans by their own failure.
- Nobody's price in 1799 was **the republic**; each constituency's greatest risk was a **social** loss, which the new regime guaranteed.
- Three instruments of legitimisation: **law** (the Code), **title** (the Empire) and **victory** (the campaigns) - only the third requires continuous renewal.

## 14. The Napoleonic settlement

- **Civil Code promulgated 21 March 1804**; Napoleon **proclaimed Emperor 18 May 1804** and **crowned 2 December 1804** - "legal equality survives, the republic does not". Never date the Code to the 1790s.
- **Concordat of 1801** with **Pope Pius VII**: public Catholic worship restored, the Church left under state authority and the confiscated lands not returned - the religious rupture of 1790 closed on the Revolution's terms.
- Companion measures: **protection of private property** and a **uniform decimal system of weights and measures**; Napoleon presented himself as a **moderniser of Europe** and placed **members of his family on the thrones** of conquered states.
- Preserved: abolition of feudal remnants; **legal rationalisation** and equality before law; careers open to talent; anti-feudal reform and nationalism spread across Europe.
- Curtailed: the **republic ended**; **empire and censorship** returned the concentration of power; **war became imperial conquest**; **popular sovereignty subordinated to military-political authority**.
- Mechanism: **codification carried the settlement beyond France** - the settlement became a portable, court-enforceable institution.
- Corrections: Napoleon did **not** simply restore the old order; he did **not** end all revolutionary change.
- Formulation: **Napoleon stabilised the social revolution while reversing much of its political republicanism**; synthesis - **both heir and gravedigger**; scaffold - "heir to the social revolution, betrayer of the political revolution - which is precisely why the Restoration could not simply undo 1789."
- Censorship identifies the mechanism: retain equality before the law, remove the means of invoking it against the government.

## 15. Empire, conquest and the nationalism it created

- Under Napoleon **war became imperial conquest**; in the same record, **nationalism and anti-feudal reform spread across Europe**; the phase exported anti-feudal reform and ended republican liberty.
- The Revolution **inspired movements in almost every country of Europe and in South and Central America**; this is the **strongest bridge** to nineteenth-century nationalism, constitutionalism and anti-feudal reform.
- The boomerang: authority derives from the nation → legitimises France at home → contains no geographical limit → condemns French rule abroad → cannot be repudiated without destroying the regime's own basis → resistance becomes available in the conqueror's language.
- Exported reforms can be **separated from their author**, which is why they remained after the armies withdrew.
- **Continental System**, launched by the **Berlin Decree of 21 November 1806**; the **Peninsular War, 1808-14**; the invasion of **Russia, 1812** - three dated links in one causal chain of over-extension.

## 16. 1815 and the shape of the legacy

- The end, dated: **abdication 6 April 1814** and the **Bourbon restoration** under **Louis XVIII**; the **Hundred Days** from **20 March 1815**; **Waterloo, 18 June 1815**. The Napoleonic phase ends, **but the revolutionary legacy remains**; the era runs **1799-1815**, with the coronation in **1804**.
- The Hundred Days are the proof of this topic's central claim: a restoration that one man could displace within a year was resting on allied victory, not on recovered consent - the throne could be recovered, the society could not.
- Six enduring principles, each with its own qualification: rights as a measurable public standard; citizenship replacing subjecthood but gendered and property-conditioned; popular sovereignty, invoked by the Terror too; the modern nation, whose nationalism was exclusionary and war-making; secular public authority, established coercively and plural in later forms; equality before law transplanted by codification, selectively.
- Relevance thesis: the **categories made unavoidable** - citizen, rights, popular sovereignty, nation - **not the survival of institutions**; the exclusions are as instructive as the proclamations.
- The **ladder**: 1789 claim → Terror → Napoleon → 1815 restoration → 1830 and 1848 → nineteenth-century nationalism and constitutionalism. Use it to refuse the straight-line story.
- India caution: a **shared modern vocabulary**, with routes of influence **indirect and layered**; never a genealogy, never causation of a specific later revolution.
- Boundary: the post-1815 congress settlement - the conservative response to the Revolution and Napoleon, restoring the Bourbon monarchy - belongs to the next topic.
- Closing verdict: "relevant **not as an institutional model** but as the origin of the standard by which modern states are judged - and, through its own exclusions, as the origin of the critique of that standard."

## 17. Historiography and causal weighting

- Four debates with four syntheses: causes → **ideas and crisis interacted**; class → **multi-class revolution with shifting leadership**; Terror → **explain both context and cost**; Napoleon → **both heir and gravedigger**.
- Each synthesis works by a different move: different questions; leadership against causation; refusal of completeness; two distinct objects.
- **Burke**: abstract rights against customary right; rights grow out of custom.
- **Tocqueville (1805-59)**: the absence of **intermediary groups** in France was responsible for the Revolution, while the United States maintained democracy through their presence; such groups should be free from state interference.
- **Marxian reading**: the abolition of feudalism meant the rise to power of the middle class; the Revolution was "**essentially a political revolution**", transferring social dominance from nobility and clergy to the industrial and commercial middle class, with natural rights as its "**ideal justification and rationalization**"; the slogan enlisted the masses while the makers settled for the liberty that served the new entrepreneurial class.
- Recorded verdict: **class, fiscal crisis, political contingency, popular action and war all mattered; no single-cause theory is sufficient** - which licenses weighting, not hedging.
- Attribution discipline: name Burke, Tocqueville, Raphael, Engels and the Marxian reading, which are checkable; present the broader causal debate as **positions**, and never attach a name you cannot verify.

## 18. Answer architecture

- Syllabus clause: eighteenth-century world events and **"their forms and effect on the society"** - an instruction to write analytically.
- Directive map: *analyse the causes* (weighting, not narrative); *trace the phases* (periodisation logic, not dates); *how far transform* (measured verdict); *heir or betrayer* (split verdict); *enduring relevance* (principles and vocabulary, not institutions); *compare with America* (social depth); *women and slavery* (universalism against its limits).
- Thesis templates: causal, transformation, Napoleon, relevance - each conceding in the sentence in which it asserts.
- Mark-scaled structures: 10 - thesis, two dimensions, one qualification, verdict; 15 - full sequence plus one qualification paragraph; 20 - add comparison or legacy plus historiographical caution.
- Five-part Mains frame: rupture defined → estates and crisis → 1789, republic, war, Jacobins → consequences → Napoleon as consolidation plus reversal → conclusion on changed legitimacy.
- Three verdict scaffolds: transformation, heir-or-betrayer, enduring relevance.

## 20. Prohibited formulations

- "17,000 died in the Terror" - the figure counts people **tried and executed**.
- Any other casualty figure for the Terror or the Revolution.
- "The Declaration granted universal suffrage in 1789."
- "The Revolution abolished slavery permanently."
- "The Napoleonic Code was promulgated in the 1790s."
- "The French Revolution caused the Russian Revolution", or any similar claim of direct causation of a named later revolution - say **influenced** or **supplied vocabulary**.
- "Napoleon reintroduced slavery in 1804" - the restoring law is **1802**; the loose "ten years later" formulation in some school accounts is what produces the error.
- Verbatim quotation of Rousseau, Robespierre or the Declaration in unverified wording.
- "The French Revolution achieved full social equality."
- "America 1776 caused France 1789" or "one Atlantic revolution".
- An invented or unverifiable attribution of any kind - a historian, a school, a minister, a decree or a treaty. Verified names and dates are an asset and should be used; manufactured ones are the error this list guards against.

## Book evidence and its controls

1. **NCERT, Class IX History, *India and the Contemporary World - I*, Chapter I, *The French Revolution*** (Reprint 2025-26, 24 pages) - the school text named in both topic owners as their primary grounding, retrieved and read in full for this repair pass. Publisher-domain download failed; the identical reprint was obtained from a mirror and its complete text extracted. It is the primary owner-grounded source for the restored named coverage and supplied, among other items: Louis XVI, the Bourbon accession of 1774, Marie Antoinette and the Versailles court; the debt magnitudes and the American intervention; the three estates, the ninety per cent peasant share, tithe, taille and feudal dues; the population rise from 23 to 28 million; the 5 May 1789 summons, the 1614 precedent and the 300/300/600 composition; Mirabeau, Abbé Sieyès and *What is the Third Estate?*; the seven prisoners in the Bastille; the Great Fear with the burning of manorial records; the decree of the night of 4 August 1789; the Declaration's articles; the Constitution of 1791 with active and passive citizens and the four-million franchise; the April 1792 war vote against Prussia and Austria; the Marseillaise and Roget de L'Isle; the convent of St Jacob, Robespierre and the sans-culottes; 10 August 1792 and the Tuileries; the Convention and 21 September 1792; the execution of Louis XVI on 21 January 1793 at the Place de la Concorde; the revolutionary tribunal, the guillotine and Dr Guillotin; the price and wage maximum, rationing, the pain d'égalité and Citoyen/Citoyenne; Robespierre's conviction in July 1794; the Directory's constitution, two councils and five members; the sixty women's clubs, the Society of Revolutionary and Republican Women, the 1791 Declaration of the Rights of Woman and Citizen, Olympe de Gouges's trial and execution, and 1946; Martinique, Guadeloupe and San Domingo, the Convention's abolition of 1794 and the final abolition of 1848; the abolition of censorship; and the 1804 coronation, the decimal weights and measures, the family thrones and Waterloo in 1815.

2. `knowledge-export\General Studies\An Introduction to Political Theory by O P Gauba www.upscpdf.com.md` - the extracted, text-searchable edition of a locally held standard political-theory textbook, corresponding to `books\philosphy_books\An Introduction to Political Theory by O P Gauba www.upscpdf.com.pdf`. This is the one core searchable source used under the bounded History research rule. It was searched in full for the topic's terms and supplied: the verified wording of the Declaration's first two propositions, with the record that it was inspired by Rousseau's revolutionary ideas; Rousseau as the recorded source of inspiration of the Revolution, the *Social Contract* (1762) definition of the citizen and the priority of the common good, and the *Discourse on the Origin of Inequality* (1755) distinction between natural and conventional inequality; the citizenship lineage through 1688 and 1776 to its French zenith; the glossary definition of the Jacobins as a club founded in 1789, named from the former Paris monastery, increasingly radical, instituting the Terror and closed in 1794; Burke (1729-97) and the historical theory of rights, with his criticism of the Revolution for abstract rights and his preference for 1688, Magna Carta (1215) and the Petition of Right (1628); Tocqueville (1805-59) on the absence of intermediary groups; the Marxian reading recorded through a standard history of political theory, including the phrase "essentially a political revolution"; the ideology critique of the revolutionary slogan; Engels in *Anti-Dühring* (1878) on bourgeois equality before the law; D. D. Raphael (1976) on the removal of arbitrary privilege; the three-stage legal, political and social equality sequence with the Jacobins and later Republicans attached to the second stage; the natural-rights ambiguity control; and the concept of de facto sovereignty with Napoleon named among its examples. The glossary entry is rendered as a reported definition rather than as a verbatim quotation, because the extraction carries an obvious spelling artefact in that line.
3. `knowledge-export\History\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH __.md` - the extracted edition of the standard modern world-history text that grounds this folder's twentieth-century material, checked as a control rather than used as a source. A full-text search of the extraction for "French Revolution", "Napoleon", "Jacobin", "Robespierre", "Bastille", "Enlightenment", "ancien regime" and "Directory" returns **one** hit in total, a passing reference to the French Revolutionary Wars in an unrelated colonial passage. This independently confirms the folder README's statement that the text is structurally insufficient for this topic, and it is therefore **not** cited anywhere in this session.
4. Further control: a full-text search of every extracted book in `knowledge-export\` for "Civil Constitution of the Clergy", "Concordat", "Continental System", "Committee of Public Safety", "Thermidor", "Brumaire", "Girondin", "Montagnard", "assignat" and "Great Fear" returns no usable French-revolutionary material. The only hits are a banking-supervision concordat in an economics text, a 1933 German concordat and an alphabetical index entry reading "Montagnards" in the twentieth-century world-history text, a passing ethical illustration naming Robespierre in a history of philosophy, and unrelated lexical coincidences such as "assignation" and the surname Vendler. The same search across all canonical World History Markdown outside the prohibited artifact classes returns only "Brumaire" in the topic's own date-control list and a single incidental mention of Robespierre inside a quotation-control instruction. This is why the named and dated items the school chapter does not itself carry were verified separately against standard reference accounts rather than drawn from the local corpus.

## Endpoints identified but not retrieved

Nothing in the teaching depends on text that was not read. The following were identified and **not** fetched: the official French texts of the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen, the constitutions of 1791, 1793 and Year III, the Civil Constitution of the Clergy, the Concordat, the Berlin Decree, the Civil Code and the colonial abolition and restoration decrees; the published works of Rousseau, Locke, Montesquieu, Burke, Tocqueville, Marx, Engels and Raphael; Britannica's own pages, which refused direct retrieval; and the French news and commentary underlying the 2026 constitutional debate beyond the search return. In every case the topic's two owners, the school chapter, the subject's chronology and comparison set, or the standard political-theory text supplied the teaching content.

## Open uncertainties

The following remain genuinely open and are **not** asserted anywhere.

1. The composition of the royal debt beyond the magnitudes recorded, the ministers who attempted fiscal reform, and any figure for the annual deficit.
2. The deliberations, the voting arrangements finally adopted and the king's successive responses within the Estates-General and the National Assembly.
3. The text of the Tennis Court Oath and the identities of individual deputies beyond the two named leaders.
4. The article-by-article provisions of the decree of 4 August 1789, and which clause extinguished which obligation.
5. The geography of the Great Fear and the numbers involved in it.
6. The proportions of clergy who accepted and refused the oath of November 1790, the named regions of religious counter-revolution, and the measures of de-Christianisation.
7. The composition and internal politics of the Girondin and Montagnard groupings, and the personnel of the Committee of Public Safety beyond Robespierre.
8. The casualty scale of the war in the Vendée, and of the revolutionary and Napoleonic wars generally.
9. The risings and internal coups the Directory faced between 1795 and 1799, and the planning and personal calculations behind the coup of 18 Brumaire.
10. The article-by-article content of the Civil Code beyond the two effects recorded, and the regime's administrative, financial and educational institutions.
11. The campaigns, coalitions, treaties and satellite kingdoms of the empire beyond the three dated instruments taught as a causal chain.
12. The fate of landholding after the annulment of feudal obligations, and its effect on rural inequality.
13. Named historians or schools for the causal debate, and any measure of how widely the revolutionary argument was held among the deputies.
14. The relative weighting of the fiscal and subsistence crises; the extent to which the internal threat of 1793-94 was real rather than constructed; whether the Revolution could have survived militarily without the coercive apparatus; and the composition, strength and popular depth of European resistance to French rule.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from the permanently excluded derived-package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the standing live-session source exclusion. No learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning session, solved workbook and complete-topic-package duplicate for this topic; all were identified during source discovery and deliberately not opened.
- Every proper noun and date taught in the lessons was verified in the bounded factual pass before use, and each is attributable to the school chapter or to the reference verification recorded above.
- The Declaration is quoted only in wording checkable against a printed text. The political-theory text renders Article 2 as "natural and imprescriptible" rights and the school chapter as "natural and inalienable"; the two are compatible in substance, and no revolutionary orator is quoted at all.
- The only numbers asserted are those the sources supply: the estate and peasant population shares, the debt magnitudes, the active-citizen franchise figures, the seven Bastille prisoners, the figure of about seventeen thousand tried and executed in some fourteen months with its judicial scope stated, the dates in the master chronology, and the life-dates of the named writers and of Olympe de Gouges.
- No casualty figure is given for the revolutionary wars, the war in the Vendée, the colonial slave economy or the Napoleonic campaigns.
- Two source conflicts are taught rather than resolved silently: the typing of the execution figure across the two owners, and the school chapter's loose "ten years later" dating of the restoration of colonial slavery against the reference date of 20 May 1802, where 1802 is taught and the discrepancy flagged.
- Burke, Tocqueville, Raphael, Engels and the Marxian reading are named as checkable recorded positions. No historian is named for the broader causal debate, which is presented as a set of positions.
- The Enlightenment overview, the American Revolution and the post-1815 congress settlement are routed to their owning topics and are not re-narrated; two facts only are carried across the Vienna boundary, and both are attributed to the owning file.
- Objective-question neutrality does not arise, because this topic owns no objective question. The two Mains questions displayed carry directive, demand and answer approach without a solved model answer, as the governing boundary requires.
- No workbook operation of any kind was performed: no compiled MCQ corpus was constructed, copied or relocated, and no solved-PYQ workbook was created.
- The single current linkage is labelled as reported secondary commentary, is confined to one lesson, and carries no historical claim.
